After bath, took two or three fresh oranges, and a cup of coffee, without milk; for the little milk one uses with coffee, must not be taken with fruit here, even in winter.

===Chinese===
再往前，在要塞监狱与蓬塔之间，士兵们在操练；鼓手和号手在城墙的壁垒上练习。

稍偏左，在圣拉萨罗大道（Calzada de San Lázaro）上，是海滨浴场（Baños de Mar）。那是一个个池子，每个约十二英尺见方、六到八英尺深，直接凿在构成海岸线的岩石里，带石阶，每个池子有一对舷窗似的孔，这片无潮海岸的波浪便从孔里流进流出。这样的安排是必要的，因为鲨鱼太多，在开阔的海面游泳很危险。纯净的岩石和流进流出的活水，使这些浴池非常惬意，而这里的水正是湾流的水，温度 72 度。浴场有顶，内侧半掩着遮蔽，朝海的一面敞开供眺望；你一面洗浴，一面看见大船顺着湾流——那条赤道世界的大航道——浮驶而过。浴池里水深三至五英尺，池面够游个短程。池底是白色的沙和贝壳。这些浴池是公费修的，免费开放。有的标着女用，有的男用，有的「*por la gente de color*」（供有色人种）。沿大道再往前一点，是另一组浴场；再往外，郊外慈善院（Beneficencia）对面，还有一组。

浴后，吃了两三个新摘的橘子，喝了一杯不加奶的咖啡；因为此地连冬天，咖啡里搁的那点奶也不可与水果同吃。

===Original===
To the Cathedral, at 8 o’clock, to hear mass. The Cathedral, in its exterior, is a plain and quaint old structure, with a tower at each angle of the front; but within, it is sumptuous. There is a floor of variegated marble, obstructed by no seats or screens, tall pillars and rich frescoed walls, and delicate masonry of various colored stone, the prevailing tint being yellow, and a high altar of porphyry. There is a look of the great days of Old Spain about it; and you think that knights and nobles worshipped here and enriched it from their spoils and conquests. Every new eye turns first to the place within the choir, under that alto relief, behind that short inscription, where, in the wall of the chancel, rest the remains of Christopher Columbus. Borne from Valladolid to Seville, from Seville to San Domingo, and from San Domingo to Havana, they at last rest here, by the altar side, in the emporium of the Spanish Islands. “What is man that thou art mindful of him!” truly and humbly says the Psalmist; but what is man, indeed, if his fellow men are not mindful of such a man as this! The creator of a hemisphere! It is not often we feel that monuments are surely deserved, in their degree and to the extent of their utterance. But when, in the New World, on an island of that group which he gave to civilized man, you stand before this simple monumental slab, and know that all of him that man can gather up lies behind it, so overpowering is the sense of the greatness of his deeds, that you feel relieved that no attempt has been made to measure it by any work of man’s hands. The little there is, is so inadequate, that you make no comparison. It is a mere finger-point, the *hic jacet*, the *sic itur*.

===Chinese===
八点到教堂去望弥撒。大教堂外表是一座朴素而古雅的老建筑，正面两角各有一座塔楼；但内部却华丽。斑驳的大理石地面，不设座椅也不设屏隔，高大的柱子、华丽的湿绘壁画墙面、各色石头的精美砌工，主调是黄色，还有一座斑岩的高坛。它带着一种旧西班牙全盛时代的气象；你会想到骑士与贵族曾在此礼拜，以他们的战利品和征服所得充实它。每一双初来的眼睛都首先转向唱诗班内那个位置，那幅高浮雕下面、那行简短铭文后面——大教堂圣坛的墙里，安息着克里斯托弗·哥伦布的遗骸。从巴利亚多利德迁到塞维利亚，从塞维利亚迁到圣多明各，又从圣多明各迁到哈瓦那，它们终于在此安息，在祭坛旁，在这西班牙诸岛的都会。「人算什么，你竟顾念他！」诗篇作者说得真切而谦卑；可是，倘若同胞不记念这样一个人，人又算什么呢！半个世界的缔造者！我们并不常有这样的感觉：一座纪念碑确确实实配得上它的主人，配得上它所表达的程度。但是，当你在新世界、在他献给文明人的那片群岛中的一座岛上，站在这块朴素的纪念碑石板前，知道人所能收殓的他的一切就在石后，他功业之伟大的感觉便如此压倒一切，以至于你反倒觉得宽慰：竟没有尝试用任何人手的作品去度量它。这一点点存在是如此不相称，你根本无从比起。它只是一个指路的手指，一句「*hic jacet*」（此处长眠者），一句「*sic itur*」（由此登临）。

===Original===
The priests in the chancel are numerous, perhaps twenty or more. The service is chanted with no aid of instruments, except once the accompaniment of a small and rather disordered organ, and chanted in very loud and often harsh and blatant tones, which reverberate from the marble walls, with a tiresome monotony of cadence. There is a degree of ceremony in the placing, replacing, and carrying to and fro of candles and crucifixes, and swinging of censers, which the Roman service as practised in the United States does not give. The priests seem duly attentive and reverent in their manner, but I cannot say as much for the boys, of whom there were three or four, gentlemen-like looking lads, from the college, doing service as altar boys. One of these, who seemed to have the lead, was strikingly careless and irreverent in his manner; and when he went about the chancel, to incense all who were there, and to give to each the small golden vessel to kiss (containing, I suppose, a relic), he seemed as if he were counting his playmates out for a game and flinging the censer at them and snubbing their noses with the golden vessel.

There were only about half a dozen persons at mass, beside those in the chancel; and all but one of these were women, and of the women two were Negroes. The women walk in, veiled, drop down on the bare pavement, kneeling or sitting, as the service requires or permits. A Negro woman, with devout and even distressed countenance, knelt at the altar rail, and one pale-eyed priest, in cassock, who looked like an American or Englishman, knelt close by a pillar. A file of visitors, American or English women, with an escort of gentlemen, came in and sat on the only benches, next the columns; and when the Host was elevated, and a priest said to them, very civilly, in English, “Please to kneel down,” they neither knelt nor stood, nor went away, but kept their seats.

===Chinese===
圣坛里的司铎很多，大约二十位或更多。仪式经文是诵唱的，不用乐器，只有一次用了小而颇失修的风琴伴奏；唱得非常响，常常尖厉刺耳，在大理石墙间回荡，韵调单调得叫人厌倦。蜡烛和十字架的摆上、摆回、搬来搬去，香炉的摇转，这中间的仪节之繁，是美国的罗马公教仪式里所见不到的。司铎们的举止算是专注而恭敬的；但那些孩子——有三四个，从学院里来做辅祭的、绅士模样的少年——我就不能这么说了。其中一个像是领头的，态度出奇地轻慢无礼；他在圣坛间走动，给在场众人行香，把那只小小的金器（我想里面盛着圣髑）递给每人亲吻时，那模样像是在给一场游戏点数他的玩伴，把香炉朝他们掷过去，拿金器去磕碰他们的鼻子。

望弥撒的除了圣坛里的人，只有六七个；其中除一人外全是女人，女人里有两个是黑人。女人们蒙着面纱走进来，跪坐在光石板上，仪式要求跪便跪、容许坐便坐。一个黑人妇人，满面虔诚甚至愁苦的神情，跪在祭坛栏杆前；一个眼睛苍白、穿黑色长袍、看去像美国人或英国人的司铎，紧靠着一根柱子跪着。一列游人——美国或英国的女士们，由几位绅士陪着——走进来，坐在柱子旁仅有的几条长凳上；圣体举起时，一位司铎用英语很客气地对她们说：「请跪下。」她们既不跪，也不起立，也不走开，只是坐着不动。

===Original===
After service, the old sacristan, in blue woollen dress, showed all the visitors the little chapel and the cloisters, and took us beyond the altar to the mural tomb of Columbus, and though he was liberally paid, haggled for two reals more.

In the rear of the Cathedral is the Seminario, or college for boys, where also men are trained for the priesthood. There are cloisters and a pleasant garden within them.

===Chinese===
仪式之后，穿蓝呢衣的老司事领所有游人看了小礼拜堂和回廊，又带我们到祭坛后面看哥伦布的壁墓；他已得了优厚的赏钱，还要为多两个里亚尔讨价还价。

大教堂后面是修院学校（Seminario），即男童学院，也是培养司铎的地方。其中有回廊，回廊里有一个宜人的花园。

## VII Gold and silver﻿—Coinage﻿—Family breakfasts﻿—Coolies﻿—Jesus del Monte﻿—View of city and harbor from Jesus del Monte﻿—Taste for proper names of towns, shops, etc.﻿—Opera at the Villanueva, the boxes, ladies, Captain-General, soldiers / 七　金银——币制——举家用早餐——华工——赫苏斯-德尔蒙特——从赫苏斯-德尔蒙特望城市与海港——城镇店铺命名之趣味——比亚努埃瓦剧院的歌剧：包厢、女士们、都督、士兵

===Original===
Breakfast, and again the cool marble floor, white-robed tables, the fruits and flowers, and curtains gently swaying, and women in morning toilets. Besides the openness to view, these rooms are strangely open to ingress. Lottery ticket vendors go the rounds of the tables at every meal, and so do the girls with tambourines for alms for the music in the street. As there is no coin in Cuba less than the medio, 6¼ cents, the musicians get a good deal or nothing. The absence of any smaller coin must be an inconvenience to the poor, as they must often buy more than they want, or go without. I find silver very scarce here. It is difficult to get change for gold, and at public places notices are put up that gold will not be received for small payments. I find the only course is to go to one of the Cambios de Moneda, whose signs are frequent in the streets, and get a half doubloon changed into reals and pesetas, at four percent discount, and fill my pockets with small silver.

===Chinese===
早餐，又是清凉的大理石地面、铺白桌布的餐桌、水果和鲜花、轻轻摇曳的窗帘、晨妆未整的女人们。这些房间除了任人观看，还奇怪地任人出入。卖彩票的小贩每餐都在各桌之间巡回，摇着铃鼓为街头音乐求施舍的姑娘们也是。既然古巴没有比梅迪奥（medio，合 6.25 美分）更小的钱币，乐师们要么得到可观的一笔，要么分文皆无。没有更小的钱币，对穷人想必多有不便，因为他们常常必须买下比所需更多的东西，或者干脆不买。我发现此地银币极缺。金子难以破成零钱，公共场所贴着告示：小额付款不收金子。我发现唯一的办法是找一家街边招牌常见的钱兑铺（Cambios de Moneda），把半枚达布隆金币贴百分之四的兑水换成里亚尔和佩塞塔，把口袋装满小银币。

===Original===
Spent the morning, from eleven o’clock to dinnertime, in my room, writing and reading. It is too hot to be out with comfort. It is not such a morning as one would spend at the St. Nicholas, or the Tremont, or at Morley’s or Meurice’s. The rooms all open into the court yard, and the doors and windows, if open at all, are open to the view of all passersby. As there are no bells, every call is made from the veranda rail, down into the courtyard, and repeated until the servant answers, or the caller gives up in despair. Antonio has a compeer and rival in Domingo, and the sharp voice of the woman in the next room but one, who proves to be a subordinate of the opera troupe, is calling out, “Do-meen-go! Do-meen-go!” and the rogue is in full sight from our side, making significant faces, until she changes her tune to “Antonio! Antonio! *adonde está* Domingo?” But as she speaks very little Spanish, and Antonio very little French, it is no difficult for him to get up a misapprehension, especially at the distance of two stories; and she is obliged to subside for a while, and her place is supplied by the parrot. She is usually unsuccessful, being either unreasonable, or bad pay. The opera troupe are rehearsing in the second flight, with doors and windows open. And throughout the hot middle day, we hear the singing, the piano, the parrot, and the calls and parleys with the servants below. But we can see the illimitable sea from the end of the piazza, blue as indigo; and the strange city is lying under our eye, with its strange blue and white and yellow houses, with their roofs of dull red tiles, its strange tropical shade trees, and its strange vehicles and motley population, and the clangor of its bells, and the high pitched cries of the vendors in its streets.

===Chinese===
从十一点到正餐时分，我在房间里写作读书。在外面走动而不觉其苦，实在太热了。这不是人们在圣尼古拉斯饭店、特里蒙特饭店，或在莫利、莫里斯度过的那种上午。所有房间都开向内院，门窗若开着，就开在一切过路人眼前。没有铃，每次呼唤都要从游廊栏杆朝院里喊，一遍遍喊到仆人答应、或呼唤者绝望放弃为止。安东尼奥有一位同侪兼对手多明戈；隔壁再隔壁那个尖嗓子女人——原来她是歌剧团里一个下手——正喊个不住：「Do-meen-go! Do-meen-go!」而那无赖就在我们这一侧望得清清楚楚的地方，做着种种表情，直到她改了调子喊：「Antonio! Antonio! *adonde está* Domingo?」（安东尼奥！安东尼奥！多明戈在哪儿？）但她西班牙话说得很少，安东尼奥法语也懂得有限，他要装出一副听不懂的样子并不难，隔着两层楼的距离尤其如此；她只好消停一阵，由鹦鹉接替她的位置。她通常是不成功的，不是要求无理，就是给钱不爽利。歌剧团正在二楼排练，门窗大开。于是在这炎热的长昼中段，我们听见的尽是歌声、琴声、鹦鹉声，还有朝下面仆人的呼唤与对答。但从游廊尽头我们能望见无际的大海，蓝得像靛青；这座奇异的城市就躺在眼下：蓝白黄各色的奇异房屋，盖着暗红的瓦顶；奇异的热带行道树；奇形的车辆和杂色的人群；还有钟声的铿锵和小贩在街上尖利的叫卖。

===Original===
Going downstairs at about eleven o’clock, I find a table set in the front hall, at the foot of the great staircase, and there, in full view of all who come or go, the landlord and his entire establishment, except the slaves and coolies, are at breakfast. This is done every day. At the café round the corner, the family with their white, hired servants, breakfast and dine in the hall, through which all the customers of the place must go to the baths, the billiard rooms, and the bowling alleys. Fancy the manager of the Astor or Revere, spreading a table for breakfast and dinner in the great entry, between the office and the front door, for himself and family and servants!

Yesterday and today I noticed in the streets and at work in houses, men of an Indian complexion, with coarse black hair. I asked if they were native Indians, or of mixed blood. No, they are the Coolies! Their hair, full grown, and the usual dress of the country which they wore, had not suggested to me the Chinese; but the shape and expression of the eye make it plain. These are the victims of the trade, of which we hear so much. I am told there are 200,000 of them in Cuba, or, that so many have been imported, and all within seven years. I have met them everywhere, the newly arrived, in Chinese costume, with shaved heads, but the greater number in pantaloons and jackets and straw hats, with hair full grown. Two of the cooks at our hotel are Coolies. I must inform myself on the subject of this strange development of the domination of capital over labor. I am told there is a mart of Coolies in the Cerro. This I must see, if it is to be seen.

===Chinese===
十一点左右下楼，见前厅里、大楼梯脚下摆着一张餐桌，店主和他全班人马——奴隶和华工（Coolies）除外——正在用早餐，来往人等看得一清二楚。这是每天照做的事。街角那家咖啡馆里，那家人和他们雇的白人仆役就在大厅里用早餐、吃正餐，而这家店的顾客去澡堂、弹子房、保龄球道，都必须穿过这间大厅。试想阿斯特或里维尔饭店的经理，在账房与前门之间的大过道里，为自己、家人和仆役摆开早晚餐的桌子！

昨天和今天，我在街上、在人家做工的人群里注意到一些印第安人肤色、黑粗头发的男子。我问他们是本地印第安人，还是混血。不是，他们是华工！他们留全发的头和身上本地通行的衣着没有让我想起中国人；但眼睛的形状和神情却一望便知。这些就是那种我们听得很多的买卖的牺牲品。听说他们在古巴有 200,000 人，或者说输入了这么多，而且全在七年之内。我到处遇到他们：新到的穿中国服装，剃着光头；但更多的穿长裤、短上衣，戴草帽，头发留得全长。我们旅馆的两个厨子就是华工。关于资本对劳动的统治的这一奇怪变种，我得认真弄个明白。听说塞罗有个华工市场。只要看得成，我一定去看。

===Original===
After dinner drove out to the Jesus del Monte, to deliver my letter of introduction to the Bishop. The drive, by way of the Calzada de Jesus del Monte, takes one through a wretched portion, I hope the most wretched portion, of Havana, by long lines of one story wood and mud hovels, hardly habitable even for Negroes, and interspersed with an abundance of drinking shops. The horses, mules, asses, chickens, children, and grown people use the same door; and the backyards disclose heaps of rubbish. The looks of the men, the horses tied to the doorposts, the mules with their panniers of fruits and leaves reaching to the ground, all speak of *Gil Blas*, and of what we have read of humble life in Spain. The little Negro children go stark naked, as innocent of clothing as the puppies. But this is so all over the city. In the front hall of Le Grand’s, this morning, a lady, standing in a full dress of spotless white, held by the hand a naked little Negro boy, of two or three years old, nestling in black relief against the folds of her dress.

===Chinese===
正餐后驱车前往赫苏斯-德尔蒙特，去送我那封给主教的介绍信。取道赫苏斯-德尔蒙特大道（Calzada de Jesus del Monte）的一路，穿过哈瓦那的一片凄惨地段——但愿是最凄惨的地段——两旁是一长排一长排单层的木板泥墙窝棚，连黑人住着都勉强，中间夹杂着数不清的酒肆。马、骡、驴、鸡、孩子和大人进出同一个门；后院里垃圾成堆。那些人的模样、拴在门柱上的马、驮篮几乎拖到地上的骡子，都叫人想起《吉尔·布拉斯》，想起我们读到过的西班牙底层生活。黑人小孩光着身子跑，像小狗一样不识衣裳为何物。但全城处处如此。今天早晨，在勒格朗的前厅里，一位穿一身雪白礼服的女士，手牵着个两三岁的光着身子的小黑人男孩，他偎在她裙裾的皱褶间，黑色的身影衬得分外分明。

===Original===
Now we rise to the higher grounds of Jesus del Monte. The houses improve in character. They are still of one story, but high and of stone, with marble floors and tiled roofs, with courtyards of grass and trees, and through the gratings of the wide, long, open windows, I see the decent furniture, the double, formal row of chairs, prints on the walls, and well-dressed women maneuvering their fans.

===Chinese===
此刻我们登上赫苏斯-德尔蒙特的高地。房屋的气象渐渐像样起来。仍是一层，却高敞而石造，大理石地面，瓦顶，院子里有草有树；透过那些宽大敞开的长窗铁栅，我看见体面的家具、正式成对排列的椅子、墙上的版画，和衣着齐整、运着扇子的女人们。

===Original===
As a carriage with a pair of cream-colored horses passed, having two men within, in the dress of ecclesiastics, my driver pulled up and said that was the Bishop’s carriage, and that he was going out for an evening drive. Still, I must go on; and we drive to his house. As you go up the hill, a glorious view lies upon the left. Havana, both city and suburbs, the Morro with its batteries and lighthouse, the ridge of fortifications called the Cabaña and Casa Blanca, the Castle of Atares, near at hand, a perfect truncated cone, fortified at the top﻿—the higher and most distant Castle of Príncipe,

> “And, poured round all,
 

 Old Ocean’s gray and melancholy waste”﻿—

No! Not so! Young Ocean, the Ocean of today! The blue, bright, healthful, glittering gladdening, inspiring Ocean! Have I ever seen a city view so grand? The view of Quebec from the foot of the Montmorenci Falls, may rival, but does not excel it. My preference is for this; for nothing, not even the St. Lawrence, broad and affluent as it is, will make up for the living sea, the boundless horizon, the dioramic vision of gliding, distant sails, and the open arms and motherly bosom of the harbor, “with handmaid lamp attending”:﻿—our Mother Earth, forgetting never the perils of that gay and treacherous world of waters, its change of moods, its “strumpet winds,”﻿—ready is she at all times, by day or by night, to fold back to her bosom her returning sons, knowing that the sea can give them no drink, no food, no path, no light, nor bear up their foot for an instant, if they are sinking in its depths.

===Chinese===
一辆双乳白马拉的车驶过，车里坐着两位穿教士服装的人，我的车夫勒住马说，那是主教的马车，他出来傍晚兜风。可我还是得往前走；我们便驶向他的住宅。上山的路上，左边展开一片壮丽的景色。哈瓦那，城区带郊区；莫罗，带着它的炮台和灯塔；那道称为卡瓦尼亚和白屋的要塞山脊；近处的阿塔雷斯城堡，一座完美的截头圆锥，顶上设防——更高更远的王子城堡，

> 「而倾泻在四周的，
 

 是老海洋那灰暗而忧郁的荒漠」——

不！不是这样！是年轻的海洋，今日的海洋！蓝色、明亮、健康、闪烁、令人欢畅、令人奋发的海洋！我可曾见过比这更壮美的城市景色？从蒙莫朗西瀑布脚下望魁北克，或可与之争锋，却不能胜过它。我偏爱这一边；因为没有什么——连圣劳伦斯河也不能，尽管它宽阔丰沛——能补偿那活生生的大海、无际的地平线、远处帆影徐徐滑过的西洋景，以及港湾那敞开的双臂和母亲般的胸怀，「有执灯的侍女随侍」：——我们的地球母亲，从不忘记那片欢乐而奸诈的水域的种种凶险，它的喜怒无常，它的「淫妇般的风」——无论昼夜，她随时准备把她归来的儿子们重新搂回怀中；她知道，大海给不了他们一口水、一口粮、一条路、一盏灯，他们若沉入它的深处，连一只脚也托不住片刻。

===Original===
The regular episcopal residence is in town. This is only a house which the Bishop occupies temporarily, for the sake of his health. It is a modest house of one story, standing very high, with a commanding view of city, harbor, sea, and suburbs. The floors are marble, and the roof is of open rafters, painted blue, and above twenty feet in height; the windows are as large as doors, and the doors as large as gates. The mayordomo shows me the parlor, in which are portraits in oil of distinguished scholars and missionaries and martyrs.

On my way back to the city, I direct the driver to avoid the disagreeable road by which we came out, and we drive by a cross road, and strike the Paseo de Tacon at its outer end, where is a fountain and statue, and a public garden of the most exquisite flowers, shrubs, and trees; and around them are standing, though it is nearly dark, files of carriages waiting for the promenaders, who are enjoying a walk in the garden. I am able to take the entire drive of the Paseo. It is straight, very wide, with two carriage ways and two foot ways, with rows of trees between, and at three points has a statue and a fountain. One of these statues, if I recollect aright, is of Tacon; one of a Queen of Spain; and one is an allegorical figure. The Paseo is two or three miles in length; reaching from the Campo de Marte, just outside the walls, to the last statue and public garden, on gradually ascending ground, and lined with beautiful villas, and rich gardens full of tropical trees and plants. No city in America has such an avenue as the Paseo de Tacon. This, like most of the glories of Havana, they tell you they owe to the energy and genius of the man whose name it bears.﻿—I must guard myself, by the way, while here, against using the words America and American, when I mean the United States and the people of our Republic; for this is America also; and they here use the word America as including the entire continent and islands, and distinguish between Spanish and English America, the islands and the main.

===Chinese===
主教的正任宅邸在城里。这只是他为健康之故暂住的一所房子。一所朴素的一层房屋，地势极高，城市、海港、大海和郊区尽收眼底。地面是大理石，屋顶椽木敞露，漆成蓝色，净高二十多英尺；窗户大得像门，门大得像大门。管家（mayordomo）领我看客厅，里面挂着油彩的肖像，都是有名的学者、传教士和殉道者。

回城的路上，我吩咐车夫避开来时那条不愉快的路，改走一条叉路，在塔康林荫大道的尽头踏上正道；那里有一座喷泉和雕像，还有一座公园，奇花异木，精妙绝伦；虽已将近天黑，一列列马车还环侍四周，等候在园中散步尽兴的人们。我把这条林荫大道从头到尾乘车走了一遍。它笔直、极宽，两条车路、两条步道，中间夹着成行的树木，在三个地点各有一座雕像和一座喷泉。其中一座雕像，如果我没记错，是塔康本人的；一座是西班牙的一位王后；还有一座是寓意人物。这条大道长两三英里，从城墙外的战神广场起，在渐升的坡地上直抵最后一座雕像和那座公园，两旁尽是美丽的别墅和满是热带花木的丰茂园囿。美洲没有一座城市有这样一条堪比塔康林荫大道的大道。与哈瓦那的多数荣光一样，人们会对你说，这条大道也归功于它以其命名的那位人物的魄力与天才。——顺便说一句，我在此地须当心，想说合众国和我们共和国的人民时，不要用「美洲」「美洲人」这样的字眼；因为这里也是美洲；他们这里用「美洲」一词包括整个大陆和岛屿，并且区分西属美洲与英属美洲、岛屿与大陆。

===Original===
The Cubans have a taste for prodigality in grandiloquent or pretty names. Every shop, the most humble, has its name. They name the shops after the sun and moon and stars; after gods and goddesses, demigods and heroes; after fruits and flowers, gems and precious stones; after favorite names of women, with pretty, fanciful additions; and after all alluring qualities, all delights of the senses, and all pleasing affections of the mind. The wards of jails and hospitals are each known by some religious or patriotic designation; and twelve guns in the Morro are named for the Apostles. Every town has the name of an apostle or saint, or of some sacred subject. The full name of Havana, in honor of Columbus, is San Cristóbal de la Habana; and that of Matanzas is San Carlos Alcazar de Matanzas. It is strange that the island itself has defied all the Spanish attempts to name it. It has been solemnly named Juana, after the daughter of Ferdinand and Isabella; then Ferdinandina, after Ferdinand himself; then Santiago, and, lastly, Ave Maria; but it has always fallen back upon the original Indian name of Cuba. And the only compensation to the hyperbolical taste of the race is that they decorate it, on state and ceremonious occasions, with the musical prefix of “La siempre fidelísima Isla de Cuba.”

===Chinese===
古巴人对浮夸或漂亮的名字有一种挥霍的趣味。每家店铺，再卑微的，也有自己的名号。他们用太阳、月亮和星星给店铺命名；用男女神祇、半神和英雄；用水果和花卉、宝石和珍石；用女子的爱称，缀上美丽动听的附加语；还用一切诱人的品质、一切感官的愉悦、一切讨喜的心绪。监狱和医院的病房，各有某个宗教或爱国的名号；莫罗的十二尊炮，以十二使徒命名。每座城镇都以一位使徒或圣徒、或某个神圣的事物为名。哈瓦那的全名，为纪念哥伦布，是圣克里斯托瓦尔-德洛哈瓦那（San Cristóbal de la Habana）；马坦萨斯的全名是圣卡洛斯要塞-德马坦萨斯（San Carlos Alcazar de Matanzas）。说来奇怪，这座岛屿本身却抗拒了西班牙人的一切命名企图。它曾被郑重命名为胡安娜（Juana），以费迪南德和伊莎贝拉之女为名；后来叫费迪南迪纳（Ferdinandina），以费迪南德本人为名；再后来叫圣地亚哥（Santiago），最后叫圣马利亚（Ave Maria）；可它总是回到原本的印第安名字——古巴。对这个种族的夸张趣味唯一的补偿是：在隆重的典礼场合，他们给它冠上音乐般的前缀——「La siempre fidelísima Isla de Cuba」（永远最忠贞的古巴岛）。

===Original===
At 7:30 p.m. went with my New York fellow passengers to hear an opera, or, more correctly, to see the people of Havana at an opera. The Teatro de Tacon is closed for repairs. This is unfortunate, as it is said by some to be the finest theatre, and by all to be one of the three finest theatres in the world. This, too, is attributed to Tacon; although it is said to have been a speculation of a clever pirate, turned fish-dealer, who made a fortune by it. But I like well enough the Teatro de Villanueva. The stage is deep and wide, the pit high and comfortable, and the boxes light and airy and open in front, with only a light tracery of iron to support the rails, leaving you a full view of the costumes of the ladies, even to their slippers. The boxes are also separated from the passageways in the rear, only by wide lattice work; so that the promenaders between the acts can see the entire contents of the boxes at one view; and the ladies dress and sit and talk and use the tan with a full sense that they are under the inspection of a “committee of the whole house.” They are all in full dress, *décolletées*, without hats. It seemed, to my fancy, that the mature women were divisible into two classes, distinctly marked and with few intermediates, the obese and the shrivelled. I suspect that the effect of time in this climate is to produce a decided result in the one direction or the other. But a single night’s view at an opera is very imperfect material for an induction, I admit. The young ladies had, generally, full figures, with tapering fingers and well rounded arms; yet there were some in the extreme contrast of sallow, bilious, sharp countenances, with glassy eyes. There is evidently great attention to manner, to the mode of sitting and moving, to the music of the voice in speaking, the use of the hands and arms, and, perhaps it may be ungallant to add, of the eyes.

===Chinese===
晚七点半，同我的纽约旅伴们去看歌剧——或者更确切地说，去看歌剧场中的哈瓦那人。塔康剧院闭门修缮。这很可惜，有人说它是世界上最好的剧院，所有人则公认它是世界三大最佳剧院之一。这座剧院也被归功于塔康；虽说它其实是一个聪明的海盗改行鱼贩的一项投机生意，那人靠它发了财。但我也相当喜欢比亚努埃瓦剧院（Teatro de Villanueva）。舞台又深又宽，正厅座位高而舒适，包厢轻巧空敞，正面只用一道纤细的铁花格支着栏杆，让女士们的衣饰从头到脚尽收眼底，连她们的便鞋也看得见。包厢与后面走廊之间也只隔着一道宽格栅；于是幕间散步的人一眼便可把各包厢的陈设连同人物看遍；女士们穿衣、落座、谈笑、摇扇，心里完全明白自己处在「全院委员会」的检阅之下。她们都作盛装，袒胸露肩（*décolletées*），不戴帽子。在我看去，成熟的女人们似可分为界限分明、少有中间类型的两类：臃肿的和干瘪的。我疑心在这种气候里，岁月的作用总要朝这两个方向之一产生决定性的结果。不过我承认，歌剧之夜的一瞥，作为归纳的材料是非常不完备的。年轻女士们大多体态丰满，指尖纤秀，手臂圆润；却也有几个走向另一极端：面容苍黄、带着胆汁质的尖峭，眼睛无神。这里显然极讲究仪态：坐相走相、说话时嗓音的音乐感、双手和手臂的运用——也许还可以加上眼睛的运用，虽然这样说未免不够殷勤。

===Original===
The Governor-General, Concha (whose title is, strictly, Captain-General), with his wife and two daughters, and two aides-de-camp, is in the Viceregal box, hung with red curtains, and surmounted by the royal arms. I can form no opinion of him from his physiognomy, as that is rather heavy, and gives not much indication.

Between the acts, I make, as all the gentlemen do, the promenade of the house. All parts of it are respectable, and the regulations are good. I notice one curious custom, which I am told prevails in all Spanish theatres. As no women sit in the pit, and the boxes are often hired for the season, and are high-priced, a portion of an upper tier is set apart for those women and children who cannot or do not choose to get seats in the boxes. Their quarter is separated from the rest of the house by gates, and is attended by two or three old women, with a man to guard the entrance. No men are admitted among them, and their parents, brothers, cousins and beaux are allowed only to come to the door, and must send in refreshments, and even a cup of water, by the hands of the *dueñas*.

Military, on duty, abound at the doors and in the passageways. The men tonight are of the regiment of Guards, dressed in white. There are enough of them to put down a small insurrection, on the spot. The singers screamed well enough, and the play was a poor one *Maria de Rohan*, but the prima donna, Gazzaniga, is a favorite, and the excitable Cubans shout and scream, and throw bouquets, and jump on the benches, and, at last, present her with a crown, wreathed with flowers, and with jewels of value attached to it. Miss Adelaide Phillips is here, too, and a favorite, and has been crowned, they say; but she does not sing tonight.

===Chinese===
总督孔查（严格说来，他的头衔是都督）偕夫人、两个女儿和两位副官，坐在挂着红幔、顶饰王徽的副王包厢里。我从他的相貌形成不了任何看法；那相貌颇为沉滞，看不出多少名堂。

幕间，我照所有绅士那样在剧院里散步。各处都还体面，规矩也好。我注意到一种奇怪的习俗，据说西班牙所有的戏院都通行。因为女人不坐正厅，包厢又常是整季包下的、价钱昂贵，楼上有一层划出一部分，专给那些坐不起或不屑坐包厢的妇女儿童。她们的这一角与剧院其余部分用栅门隔开，有两三个老妇人照管，另有一名男子把门。男人一概不得进入其中；她们的父母兄弟、表兄弟和相好，也只许走到门口，点心乃至一杯水，都得经陪媪（*dueñas*）的手递进去。

门口和过道里满是执勤的军人。今晚在场的是卫队团的士兵，穿白色军服。人数之多，足以就地平定一场小小的骚乱。歌手们叫喊得还算像样，戏是出乏味的戏——《玛丽亚·德·罗昂》（*Maria de Rohan*）；但女主角加扎尼加是众人的宠儿，易兴奋的古巴人又叫又嚷，抛掷花束，跳上长凳，最后给她献上一顶花冠，编着花朵，还缀着值钱的珠宝。阿德莱德·菲利普斯小姐也在这里，同样受人宠爱，据说也得过加冠；但她今晚不唱。

## VIII A *máscara*﻿—Spanish troops﻿—Sunday in Havana﻿—Dinner at the Bishop’s﻿—Interest in the Thirty Millions Bill﻿—Visits at evening / 八　一场化装舞会——西班牙军队——哈瓦那的星期日——主教府晚宴——备受关注的三千万法案——晚间拜会

===Original===
Tomorrow, I am to go, at eight o’clock either to the church of San Domingo, to hear the military mass, or to the Jesuit church of Belen; for the service of my own church is not publicly celebrated, even at the British Consulate; no service but the Roman Catholic being tolerated on the island.

===Chinese===
明天早晨八点，我要么去圣多明各教堂听军人弥撒，要么去贝伦的耶稣会教堂；因为我们自己教会的礼拜在此地不得公开举行，即便在英国领事馆里也不行；岛上除罗马公教外，不容任何别的教门。

===Original===
Tonight there is a public *máscara* (mask ball) at the great hall, next door to Le Grand’s. My only window is by the side of the numerous windows of the great hall, and all these are wide open; and I should be stifled if I were to close mine. The music is loud and violent, from a very large band, with kettle drums and bass drums and trumpets; and because these do not make noise and uproar enough, leather bands are snapped, at the turns in the tunes. For sleeping, I might as well have been stretched on the bass drum. This tumult of noises, and the heat are wearing and oppressive beyond endurance, as it draws on past midnight, to the small hours; and the servants in the court of the hall seem to be tending at tables of quarrelling men, and to be interminably washing and breaking dishes. After several feverish hours, I light a match and look at my watch. It is nearly five o’clock in the morning. There is an hour to daylight﻿—and will this noise stop before then? The city clocks struck five; the music ceased; and the bells of the convents and monasteries tolled their matins, to call the nuns and monks to their prayers and to the bedsides of the sick and dying in the hospitals, as the maskers go home from their revels at this hideous hour of Sunday morning. The servants ceased their noises, the cocks began to crow and the bells to chime, the trumpets began to bray, and the cries of the streets broke in before dawn, and I dropped asleep just as I was thinking sleep past hoping for; when I am awaked by a knocking at the door, and Antonio calling, “*Usted! Usted! Un caballero quiere ver á Usted!*” to find it half-past nine, the middle of the forenoon, and an ecclesiastic in black dress and shovel hat, waiting in the passageway, with a message from the bishop.

===Chinese===
今晚，紧挨着勒格朗的大厅里有一场公共化装舞会（*máscara*）。我唯一的窗户就在大厅那一排众多窗户的旁边，而那些窗全都敞着；我要是关上自己的窗，就得闷死。乐声喧天动地，是一支很大的乐队，有铜鼓、大鼓和小号；这些还嫌吵闹得不够，乐曲转折处还要甩响皮鞭。要睡觉，我不如干脆摊在大鼓上睡。这阵喧嚣加上暑热，过了午夜、临近凌晨时分，简直折磨压迫得人忍无可忍；大厅院子里的仆役们像是在伺候一桌桌争吵的男人，没完没了地洗着、摔着盘子。熬了几个钟头发烧似的不宁之后，我划了根火柴看表。已是清晨快五点了。离天亮还有一个钟头——这噪音到时能停吗？城里的大钟敲了五下；音乐停了；修道院和修士院的钟敲起晨祷，唤修女和修士们去祈祷、去医院到病者和临终者的榻前，而化装舞客们正是在星期日早晨这个可怕的时辰，从他们的狂欢中回家去。仆役们的响动停了，鸡开始啼，钟开始鸣，小号开始吹，街上的叫卖在天亮前闯进来，我正当觉得睡觉已无可指望的时候沉沉睡去；忽然被一阵敲门声惊醒，安东尼奥在喊：「*Usted! Usted! Un caballero quiere ver á Usted!*」（先生！先生！有位先生要见您！）醒来已是九点半，上午过了一半，一位穿黑衣、戴铲形帽的教士等在过道里，捎来主教的口信。

===Original===
His Excellency regrets not having seen me the day before, and invites me to dinner at three o’clock, to meet three or four gentlemen; an invitation which I accept with pleasure.

I am too late for the mass, or any other religious service, as all the churches close at ten o’clock. A tepid, soothing bath, at “Los baños públicos,” round the corner, and I spend the morning in my chamber. As we are at breakfast, the troops pass by the Paseo, from the mass service. Their gait is quick and easy, with swinging arms, after the French fashion. Their dress is seersucker, with straw hats and red cockades: the regiments being distinguished by the color of the cloth on the cuffs of the coat, some being yellow, some green, and some blue.

===Chinese===
主教大人为前日未能见我表示遗憾，并邀我三时赴宴，与三四位先生会面；这一邀请我欣然接受。

弥撒是赶不上了，别的礼拜也一样，因为所有教堂十点就关门了。在街角「Los baños públicos」（公共浴场）洗了个温热的、令人舒泰的澡，上午便在房中度过。我们正用早餐时，军队做完弥撒，从林荫大道那头走过。他们步子轻快洒脱，双臂摆动，是法国式的路数。军装是泡泡纱的，草帽缀红帽章；各团以袖口布的颜色相区别，有的是黄的，有的是绿的，有的是蓝的。

===Original===
Soon after two o’clock, I take a carriage for the bishop’s. On my way out I see that the streets are full of Spanish sailors from the men of-war, ashore for a holiday, dressed in the style of English sailors, with wide duck trousers, blue jackets, and straw hats, with the name of their ship on the front of the hat. All business is going on as usual, and laborers are at work in the streets and on the houses.

The company consists of the bishop himself, the Bishop of Puebla de los Angeles in Mexico, Father Yuch, the rector of the Jesuit College, who has a high reputation as a man of intellect, and two young ecclesiastics. Our dinner is well cooked, and in the Spanish style, consisting of fish, vegetables, fruits, and of stewed light dishes, made up of vegetables, fowls and other meats, a style of cooking well adapted to a climate in which one is very willing to dispense with the solid, heavy cuts of an English dinner.

The Bishop of Puebla wore the purple, the Bishop of Havana a black robe with a broad cape, lined with red, and each wore the Episcopal cross and ring. The others were in simple black cassocks. The conversation was in French; for, to my surprise, none of the company could speak English; and being allowed my election between French and Spanish, I chose the former, as the lighter infliction on my associates.

===Chinese===
两点刚过，我雇车上主教家去。一路出去，只见街上满是西班牙水兵，从军舰上岸过节，穿的是英国水手的样式：宽大的帆布裤、蓝色短上衣、草帽，帽子正面写着舰名。一切营生照常进行，工人们还在街上和房上做工。

在座的有主教本人、墨西哥普埃布拉-德洛斯安赫莱斯主教、尤奇神父（Father Yuch）——耶稣会学院院长，享有才智之士的盛名——还有两位年轻教士。我们的正餐烧得很好，是西班牙式的，有鱼、蔬菜、水果，还有蔬菜与鸡和其他肉类合烩的清淡炖菜；这种烹饪的路数很适合这种气候——英国式正餐里那些厚实的大块肉，人在此地巴不得免去。

普埃布拉主教穿紫色，哈瓦那主教穿带红色衬里的宽斗篷黑袍，两位都佩主教十字和戒指。其余的人都穿素黑长袍。谈话用法语；因为叫我吃惊的是，座中竟没有一位会说英语；蒙他们许我在法语和西班牙语之间选择，我选了前者，对我的东道们算是最轻的磨难。

===Original===
I am surprised to see what an impression is made on all classes in this country by the pending “Thirty Millions Bill” of Mr. Slidell. It is known to be an Administration measure, and is thought to be the first step in a series which is to end in an attempt to seize the island. Our steamer brought oral intelligence that it had passed the Senate, and it was so announced in the *Diario* of the day after our arrival, although no newspaper that we brought so stated it. Not only with these clergymen, but with the merchants and others whom I have met since our arrival, foreigners as well as Cubans, this is the absorbing topic. Their future seems to be hanging in doubt, depending on the action of our government, which is thought to have a settled purpose to acquire the island. I suggested that it had not passed the Senate, and would not pass the House; and, at most, was only an authority to the President to make an offer that would certainly be refused. But they looked beyond the form of the act, and regarded it as the first move in a plan, of which, although they could not entirely know the details, they thought they understood the motive.

===Chinese===
令我不解的是，斯利德尔先生（Mr. Slidell）那项悬而未决的「三千万法案」（Thirty Millions Bill）在这个国家的所有阶层中造成了怎样的震动。大家知道这是政府方面提出的措施，并被视为一系列步骤的第一步，终将以夺取该岛的企图收场。我们的轮船带来口信，说它已在参议院通过，我们到达次日出版的《日报》（*Diario*）也这样宣布，尽管我们带来的报纸没有一家这样登过。不仅这几位教士，自我到达后遇见的商人和其他人——外国人也好，古巴人也好——都把这当作头等话题。他们的未来似乎悬而未决，系于我国政府的举动——人们认为我国政府有一个攫取该岛的既定图谋。我提出，法案并未在参议院通过，也不会在众议院通过；充其量只是授权总统出一笔注定要被拒绝的价。但他们看的是法案形式之外的东西，把它看作一项计划的第一步棋；计划的详情他们虽不能尽知，动机却自认看得明白。

===Original===
These clergymen were well informed as to the state of religion in the United States, the relative numbers and force of the various denominations, and their doctrinal differences; the reputations of Brownson, Parker, Beecher, and others; and most minutely acquainted with the condition of their own church in the United States, and with the chief of its clergy. This acquaintance is not attributable solely to their unity of organization, and to the consequent interchange of communication, but largely also to the tie of a common education at the Propaganda or St. Sulpice, the catalogues of whose alumni are familiar to the educated Catholic clergy throughout the world.

The subject of slavery, and the condition and prospects of the Negro race in Cuba, the probable results of the Coolie system, and the relations between Church and State in Cuba, and the manner in which Sunday is treated in Havana, the public school system in America, the fate of Mormonism, and how our government will treat it, were freely discussed. It is not because I have any reason to suppose that these gentlemen would object to all they said being printed in these pages, and read by all who may choose to read it in Cuba, or the United States, that I do not report their interesting and instructive conversation; but because it would be, in my opinion, a violation of the universal understanding among gentlemen.

===Chinese===
这些教士对美国宗教的状况、各教派的人数与势力消长、教义分歧，都相当熟悉；对布朗森、帕克、比彻诸人的名声也知道；对他们自己教会在美国的情形、以及教士中的首要人物，更是了如指掌。这份熟悉，不能只归功于他们组织的统一和由此而来的讯息互通，很大程度上也得归功于同在罗马传信部或圣苏尔皮斯受教的纽带——这两处的校友名录，全世界受过教育的公教教士无不烂熟。

奴役制度、黑人种族在古巴的境况与前景、华工制度可能的结果、古巴政教之间的关系、哈瓦那对待礼拜日的态度、美国的公立学校制度、摩门教的命运以及我国政府将如何处置，这些都畅谈过。我不报导他们那些有趣而有益的谈话，并不是因为我有理由认为这几位先生会反对把他们的谈话印在这些书页里、让古巴或合众国一切愿意读的人去读；而是因为在我看来，那将违背绅士之间普遍的信守。

===Original===
After dinner, we walked on the piazza, with the noble sunset view of the unsurpassed panorama lying before us; and I took my leave of my host, a kind and courteous gentleman of Old Spain, as well as a prelate, just as a few lights were beginning to sprinkle over the fading city, and the Morro Light to gleam on the untroubled air.

Made two visits in the city this evening. In each house, I found the double row of chairs, facing each other, always with about four or five feet of space between the rows. The etiquette is that the gentlemen sit on the row opposite to the ladies, if there be but two or three present. If a lady, on entering go to the side of a gentleman, when the other row is open to her, it indicates either familiar acquaintance or boldness. There is no people so observant of outguards, as the Spanish race.

I notice, and my observation is supported by what I am told by the residents here, that there is no streetwalking, in the technical sense, in Havana. Whether this is from the fact that no ladies walk in the streets﻿—which are too narrow for comfortable or even safe walking﻿—or by reason of police regulations, I do not know. From what one meets with in the streets, if he does not look farther, one would not know that there was a vice in Havana, not even drunkenness.

===Chinese===
正餐后，我们在游廊上散步，眼前铺展着那无与伦比的全景和壮丽的落日；我在零星灯火开始洒向渐渐隐没的城市、莫罗灯塔的光在宁谧的空气里初闪的时候，向东道辞了别——他是旧西班牙的一位和蔼谦恭的绅士，也是一位高级教士。

今晚在城里做了两次拜会。在每一家，我都见到那成对的椅子行列，相对而列，两排之间总留着大约四五英尺的空当。礼俗是：倘若在座的女士只有两三位，绅士便坐在与女士相对的那一排。一位女士进门时若走到某位绅士那一边去，而另一排明明对她空着，那就表示不是过从亲密，便是举止大胆。没有哪个民族像西班牙种族这样严守这些防闲的界线。

我注意到——我的观察也从此地居民的口中得到印证——哈瓦那没有技术意义上的街头拉客。这究竟是因为没有女士在街上步行——街道窄得连舒适、甚至安全地步行都谈不上——还是因为警察章程的缘故，我不知道。只看街面上的情形而不深究的话，人不会知道哈瓦那有什么恶行，连酗酒也没有。

## X / 十

===Original===
Steamer for Matanzas﻿—Harbor and water by night﻿—Matanzas﻿—Coolies﻿—Gabriel de la Concepcion Valdez.

As there are no plantations to be seen near Havana, I determine to go down to Matanzas, near which the sugar plantations are in full tide of operation at this season. A steamer leaves here every night at ten o’clock, reaching Matanzas before daylight, the distance by sea being between fifty and sixty miles.

Took this steamer tonight. She got underway punctually at ten o’clock, and steamed down the harbor. The dark waters are alive with phosphorescent light. From each ship that lies moored, the cable from the bows, tautened to its anchor, makes a run of silver light. Each boat, gliding silently from ship to ship, and shore to shore, turns up a silver ripple at its stem, and trails a wake of silver behind; while the dip of the oar-blades brings up liquid silver, dripping, from the opaque deep. We pass along the side of the two-decker, and see through her ports the lanterns and men; under the stern of one frigate, and across the bows of another (for Havana is well supplied with men-of-war); and drop leisurely down by the Cabaña, where we are hailed from the rocks; and bend round the Morro, and are out on the salt, rolling sea. Having a day of work before me, I went early to my berth, and was waked up by the letting off of steam, in the lower harbor of Matanzas, at three o’clock in the morning. My fellow passengers, who sat up, said the little steamer tore and plunged, and jumped through the water like a thing that had lost its wits. They seemed to think that the Cuban engineer had got a machine that would some day run away with him. It was, certainly, a very short passage.

===Chinese===
开往马坦萨斯的轮船——夜色中的港湾与海水——马坦萨斯——苦力——加布里埃尔·德拉·孔塞普西翁·巴尔德斯。

哈瓦那附近看不到什么种植园，我便决定南下马坦萨斯（Matanzas）；这个季节，那一带的糖园正值生产的高潮。每晚十点有一班轮船从这里启航，天亮之前抵达马坦萨斯，海上航程在五六十英里之间。

当晚我便乘上了这班轮船。它准十点启航，向港湾下游驶去。幽暗的水面处处闪动着磷光。每艘系泊的船，从船头绷紧到锚上的锚缆，拉出一道银亮的光痕。每只小艇悄然滑过船与船之间、岸与岸之间，船首翻起银色的涟漪，身后拖出一道银色的尾迹；桨叶每没入水中再抬起，便从深不可测的幽暗里带出淋漓的液态白银。我们沿着那艘双层甲板战舰的侧舷驶过，从她的炮眼里望得见灯火与人影；从一艘护卫舰的舰尾下面穿过，又绕过另一艘的船头（哈瓦那停泊的战舰着实不少）；再从容不迫地从卡瓦尼亚（Cabaña）脚下驶过，岩石上有人向我们喝问；随后绕过莫罗（Morro），便出了港，到了咸涩翻涌的汪洋大海上。想到第二天有事要办，我早早就上了铺位，凌晨三点，被马坦萨斯外港里泄放的蒸汽声唤醒。彻夜未睡的同船乘客说，这条小轮船一路横冲直撞，在水里又蹿又跳，活像发了疯一般。他们似乎觉得，那位古巴轮机员手里的机器，终有一天会脱缰把他一块儿带跑。不过，这航程确实短得很。

===Original===
We passed a good many vessels lying at anchor in the lower harbor of Matanzas, and came to anchor about a mile from the pier. It was clear, bright moonlight. The small boats came off to us, and took us and our luggage ashore. I was landed alone on a quay, carpetbag in hand, and had to guess my way to the inn, which was near the waterside. I beat on the big, close-barred door; and a sleepy Negro, in time, opened it. Mine host was up, expecting passengers, and after waiting on the very tardy movements of the Negro, who made a separate journey to the yard for each thing the room needed, I got to bed by four o’clock, on the usual piece of canvas stretched over an iron frame, in a room having a brick floor, and windows without glass closed with big-bolted shutters.

*Tuesday, February 22*.﻿—After coffee, walked out to deliver my letters to Mr. —﻿—﻿—, an American merchant, who has married the daughter of a planter, a gentleman of wealth and character. He is much more agreeable and painstaking than we have any right to expect of one who is served so frequently with notice that his attentions are desired for the entertainment of a stranger. Knowing that it is my wish to visit a plantation, he gives me a letter to Don Juan C﻿—﻿— who has an *ingenio* (sugar plantation), called La Ariadne, near Limonar, and about twenty-five miles back in the country from Matanzas. The train leaves at 2:30 p.m., which gives me several hours for the city.

Although it is not yet nine o’clock, it is very hot, and one is glad to keep on the shady side of the broad streets of Matanzas. This city was built later and more under foreign direction than Havana, and I have been told, not by persons here however, that for many years the controlling influences of society were French, English, and American; but that lately the policy of the government has been to discourage foreign influence, and now Spanish customs prevail﻿—bullfights have been introduced, and other usages and entertainments which had had no place here before. Whatever may be the reason, this city differs from Havana in buildings, vehicles, and dress, and in the width of its streets, and has less of the peculiar air of a tropical city. It has about 25,000 inhabitants, and stands where two small rivers, the Yumurí and the San Juan, crossed by hand some stone bridges, run into the sea, dividing the city into three parts. The vessels lie at anchor from one to three miles below the city, and lighters, with masts and sails, line the stone quays of the little rivers. The city is flat and hot, but the country around is picturesque, hilly, and fertile. To the westward of the town, rises a ridge, bordering on the sea, called the Cumbre, which is a place of resort for the beauty of its views; and in front of the Cumbre, on the inland side, is the deep rich valley of the Yumurí, with its celebrated cavern. These I must see, if I can, on my return from the plantation.

===Chinese===
我们驶过马坦萨斯外港里锚泊的许多船只，在离码头约一英里处下了锚。此时月色澄澈明亮。小船纷纷靠拢过来，把我们连同行李送上岸。我独自一人被撂在一座码头上，手提旅行毡包，只得自己摸索着寻往客栈的路——客栈就在水边附近。我敲响那扇紧闩的大门；过了好一阵，一个睡眼惺忪的黑人才来开了门。店老板已经起身，正候着乘客；那黑人每取一件房里要用的东西，都要独自往院子里跑上一趟，动作又慢得出奇，等把他伺候完，到四点钟我才得上床——照例是一方帆布绷在铁架上——屋子是砖铺的地，窗上没有玻璃，用上了大插销的百叶窗关得严严实实。

*二月二十二日，星期二*。——喝过咖啡，我便出门投递信件，收信人是———先生，一位美国商人，娶了一位种植园主的女儿为妻——那是一位富有而有身份的绅士。有些人频频接到通知，说请他费心招待某位远客，对这样的人，我们本无权指望他如何周到，他却远比情理所能期待的更叫人愉快、更肯用心。他知道我想参观一座种植园，便替我写了封信给唐·胡安·C——（Don Juan C——），此人在利莫纳尔（Limonar）附近拥有一座名为「拉·阿里阿德涅」（La Ariadne）的*糖坊*（ingenio，即甘蔗种植园），地处马坦萨斯内陆约二十五英里。火车下午两点半开，我倒有几个钟头可以看看这座城。

虽然还不到九点钟，天已经热得很，走在马坦萨斯宽阔的街道上，人人都乐意拣阴凉的一边。这座城比哈瓦那建得晚，受外国左右的程度也更深。我曾听人说过（不过并非此地人士所言），多年以来，左右此地的社会势力是法国人、英国人和美国人；但近来政府的政策是压制外国影响，如今西班牙风习占了上风——斗牛引进来了，还有种种此前此地不曾有过的习俗与娱乐。无论原因何在，这座城市在房屋、车辆、衣着乃至街道的宽度上都与哈瓦那不同，热带城市特有的那股气息也淡薄些。全城约有居民两万五千人，坐落在两条小河入海的地方——尤穆里河（Yumurí）与圣胡安河（San Juan），河上架着几座美观的石桥，把城市分作三片。船只停泊在城下游一至三英里处，带桅挂帆的驳船排列在小河的石码头旁。城内平坦而炎热，四郊却是山峦起伏、土地肥沃，风景如画。城西临海耸起一道山脊，名叫昆布雷（Cumbre），因景色秀美而成为游赏胜地；昆布雷前方靠内陆的一侧，是深邃丰饶的尤穆里河谷，谷中有驰名的岩洞。这些地方，若来得及，我打算从种植园归来时一游。

===Original===
In my morning walk, I see a company of Coolies, in the hot sun, carrying stones to build a house, under the eye of a taskmaster who sits in the shade. The stones have been dropped in a pile, from carts, and the Coolies, carry them, in files, to the cellar of the house. They are naked to the waist, with short-legged cotton trousers coming to the knees. Some of these men were strongly, one or two of them powerfully built, but many seemed very thin and frail. While looking on, I saw an evident American face near me, and getting into conversation with the man, found him an intelligent shipmaster from New York, who had lived in Matanzas for a year or two, engaged in business. He told me, as I had heard in Havana, that the importer of the Coolies gets $400 a head for them from the purchaser, and that the Coolies are entitled from the purchaser to four dollars a month, which they may demand monthly if they choose, and are bound to eight years’ service, during which time they may be held to all the service that a slave is subject to. They are more intelligent, and are put to higher labor than the Negro. He said, too, it would not do to flog a Coolie. Idolaters as they are, they have a notion of the dignity of the human body, at least as against strangers, which does not allow them to submit to the indignity of corporal chastisement. If a Coolie is flogged, somebody must die; either the Coolie himself, for they are fearfully given to suicide, or the perpetrator of the indignity, or someone else, according to their strange principles of vicarious punishment. Yet such is the value of labor in Cuba, that a citizen will give $400, in cash, for the chance of enforcing eight years’ labor, at $1 per month, from a man speaking a strange language, worshipping strange gods or none, thinking suicide a virtue, and governed by no moral laws in common with his master﻿—his value being yet further diminished by the chances of natural death, of sickness, accident, escape, and of forfeiting his services to the government, for any crime he may commit against laws he does not understand.

The Plaza is in the usual style﻿—an enclosed garden, with walks; and in front is the Government House. In this spot, so fair and so still in the noonday sun, some fourteen years ago, under the fire of the platoons of Spanish soldiers, fell the patriot and poet, one of the few popular poets of Cuba, Gabriel de la Concepcion Valdez. Charged with being the head of that concerted movement of the slaves for their freedom which struck such terror into Cuba, in 1844, he was convicted and ordered to be shot. At the first volley, as the story is told, he was only wounded. “Aim here!” said he, pointing to his head. Another volley, and it was all over.

The name and story of Gabriel de la Conception Valdez are preserved by the historians and tourists of Cuba. He is best known however, by the name of Placido, that under which he wrote and published, than by his proper name. He was a man of genius and a man of valor, but﻿—he was a mulatto!

===Chinese===
早晨散步时，我看见一队苦力（Coolies），在烈日下搬石头盖房子，监工则坐在荫凉里盯着。石头由大车卸成一堆，苦力们排成行列，把石头搬进房屋的地窖。他们上身赤裸，下身是一条齐膝的短腿棉裤。这些人里有的体格结实，有一两个堪称健壮有力，但许多看上去十分瘦弱单薄。正看着，我身旁出现一张一望而知的美国面孔，同他攀谈起来，才知道他是位精明的纽约船长，来马坦萨斯经商已经一两年。他告诉我——这话我在哈瓦那也听说过——苦力的进口商每把一名苦力转卖给买主，可到手四百元；苦力则有权向买主按月领取四元工钱，愿意的话尽可按月索要；他们受八年劳役的约束，在此期间，凡奴隶所须服的一切劳役，都可以加诸其身。他们比黑人聪明，被派去做比黑人更高级的活计。他还说，鞭打苦力是行不得的。这些人虽是拜偶像的，却有一种观念，认为人的身体自有其尊严——至少在陌生人面前如此——不容他们甘受肉刑的羞辱。苦力若遭鞭打，必定要死人：或是苦力自己，因为他们动辄自杀，可怕得很；或是施辱的那个人，或是别的什么人，全看他们那套奇特的代人受过的惩罚道理。然而古巴的劳动力就是这么值钱：一个公民肯掏四百元现款，去换取迫使一个这样的人服役八年的机会，每月代价不过一元——此人说的语言闻所未闻，拜的神明闻所未闻（或者干脆什么也不拜），把自杀当作美德，与主人之间没有任何共同的道德律令——何况他的价值还要再打折扣：天然的死亡、疾病、事故、逃亡，乃至他因触犯自己根本不懂的法令而获罪、劳役被政府没收，种种可能都得算进去。

广场是通常的式样——一座带步道的封闭花园；广场前面是政府大厦。就在这个地方，如今正午阳光下这般明媚安宁之处，约莫十四年前，爱国者兼诗人——古巴寥寥可数的几位民众诗人之一——加布里埃尔·德拉·孔塞普西翁·巴尔德斯（Gabriel de la Concepcion Valdez），在西班牙士兵排枪的火力下倒下了。他被指控为 1844 年那场震骇古巴全境的奴隶争自由串联行动的首领，定罪之后被判枪决。据人们传说，第一排枪响过，他只是受了伤。「朝这儿打！」他指着自己的头说。第二排枪响，一切便都结束了。

加布里埃尔·德拉·孔塞普西翁·巴尔德斯的名字与事迹，由古巴的史家与游人记载流传。不过，他更为人知的是普拉西多（Placido）这个名字——他写作和发表作品时用的便是它——而非本名。他是天才，也是勇士，可是——他是个穆拉托（mulatto，黑白混血儿）！

## XI / 十一

===Original===
Railroad from Matanzas﻿—Views of interior of Cuba﻿—Trees, flowers, fruits, and cane fields﻿—Sugar estates﻿—Slaves laboring﻿—Ingenio, La Ariadne.

Took the train for Limonar, at 2:30 p.m. There are three classes of cars, all after the American model, the first of about the condition of our first-class cars when on the point of being condemned as worn out; the second, a little plainer; and the third, only covered wagons with benches. The car I entered had “Davenport & Co., makers, Cambridgeport, Mass.,” familiarly on its front, and the next had “Eaton, Gilbert & Co., Troy, N. York.” The brakemen on the train are Coolies, one of them a handsome lad, with coarse, black hair, that lay gracefully about his head, and eyes handsome, though of the Chinese pattern. They were all dressed in the common shirt, trousers and hat, and, but for their eyes, might be taken for men of any of the Oriental races.

As we leave Matanzas, we rise on an ascending grade, and the bay and city lie open before us. The bay is deep on the western shore, under the ridge of the Cumbre, and there the vessels lie at anchor; while the rest of the bay is shallow, and its water, in this state of the sky and light, is of a pale green color. The lighters, with sail and oar, are plying between the quays and the vessels below. All is pretty and quiet and warm, but the scene has none of those regal points, that so impress themselves on the imagination and memory in the surroundings of Havana.

===Chinese===
马坦萨斯铁路——古巴内陆风光——树木、花卉、果木与蔗田——糖业庄园——劳作中的奴隶——糖坊「拉·阿里阿德涅」。

下午两点半，我登上了开往利莫纳尔的火车。车厢分三等，全仿美国式样：头等车厢大约相当于我们头等车厢行将报废时的光景；二等略简朴些；三等就只是带条凳的篷车了。我进的那节车厢，正面熟稔地印着「制造商：马萨诸塞州坎布里奇波特达文波特公司」（Davenport & Co., makers, Cambridgeport, Mass.），旁边一节则是「纽约州特洛伊伊顿·吉尔伯特公司」（Eaton, Gilbert & Co., Troy, N. York）。车上的制闸工是苦力，其中一个小伙子生得清秀，一头粗黑的头发披覆在头上，颇见风致，眼睛也好看，虽是地道的中国人的样式。他们都穿着寻常的衬衫、长裤，戴着帽子，若不是那双眼睛，简直可以认作东方任何一族的人。

离开马坦萨斯时，列车驶上一段上坡，海湾与城市豁然展现在眼前。海湾西岸水深，紧贴着昆布雷山脊，船只都锚泊在那里；海湾其余部分水浅，在这样的天色与光线之下，海水呈淡淡的绿色。驳船张帆划桨，往来于码头与下游的船只之间。一切都明媚、安宁而温暖，只是这景色全无哈瓦那四周那种君临天下的气象——那种气象，是深深烙进人的想象与记忆里去的。

===Original===
I am now to get my first view of the interior of Cuba. I could not have a more favorable day. The air is clear, and not excessively hot. The soft clouds float midway in the serene sky, the sun shines fair and bright, and the luxuriance of a perpetual summer covers the face of nature. These strange palm trees everywhere! I cannot yet feel at home among them. Many of the other trees are like our own, and though, tropical in fact, look to the eye as if they might grow as well in New England as here. But the royal palm looks so intensely and exclusively tropical! It cannot grow beyond this narrow belt of the earth’s surface. Its long, thin body, so straight and so smooth, swathed from the foot﻿—in a tight bandage of tawny gray, leaving only its deep-green neck, and over that its crest and plumage of deep-green leaves! It gives no shade, and bears no fruit that is valued by men. And it has no beauty to atone for those wants. Yet it has more than beauty﻿—a strange fascination over the eye and the fancy, that will never allow it to be overlooked or forgotten. The palm tree seems a kind of *lusus naturae* to the northern eye﻿—an exotic wherever you meet it. It seems to be conscious of its want of usefulness for food or shade, yet has a dignity of its own, a pride of unmixed blood and royal descent﻿—the hidalgo of the soil.

What are those groves and clusters of small growth, looking like Indian corn in a state of transmigration into trees, the stalk turning into a trunk, a thin soft coating half changed to bark, and the ears of corn turning into melons? Those are the bananas and plantains, as their bunches of green and yellow fruits plainly enough indicate, when you come nearer. But, that sad, weeping tree, its long yellow-green leaves drooping to the ground! What can that be? It has a green fruit like a melon. There it is again, in groves! I interrupt my neighbor’s tenth *cigarrito*, to ask him the name of the tree. It is the cocoa! And that soft green melon becomes the hard shell we break with a hammer. Other trees there are, in abundance, of various forms and foliage, but they might have grown in New England or New York, so far as the eye can teach us; but the palm, the cocoa, the banana and plantain are the characteristic trees you could not possibly meet with in any other zone.

===Chinese===
此刻我将初见古巴的内陆。天时再好不过：空气清朗，又不过分炎热。柔软的云朵浮在澄澈天空的半腰，太阳明朗地照耀着，永不凋歇的盛夏的繁茂覆满大自然的面容。到处都是这些陌生的棕榈树！置身其间，我至今仍觉得难称安适。其余许多树倒同我们那边的一样，虽然实际上是热带之物，看在眼里却仿佛在新英格兰也能长得同这里一样好。可那王棕看起来是那样彻头彻尾、不容分说的热带！它长不到地球表面这条窄窄的地带之外去。它那修长纤细的身躯，那样笔直，那样光滑，自脚跟起裹着一层黄灰色的紧绷的绷带，只露出深绿的颈项，颈上又顶着深绿叶片的冠冕与羽饰！它不遮荫，也不结任何为人所重的果，又没有足以补偿这些缺憾的姿色。然而它拥有比美更多的东西——一种令眼睛与想象莫名着迷的魔力，使人永远不会对它视而不见，也不会将它忘怀。在北方人的眼里，棕榈树仿佛是造物的一种奇玩（*lusus naturae*）——无论在哪里遇见，它都是异乡之物。它似乎自知于食用、荫翳皆无所用，却自有一种尊严，一种血统纯正、出身王室的骄傲——土地上的绅士贵族（hidalgo）。

那边一丛丛低矮的树是什么？像极了正在转世为树木的印第安玉米——秸秆化作了树干，薄薄一层软皮半已变成树皮，玉米穗则变成了甜瓜。走近了看，那成串或青或黄的果实说得明明白白：那是香蕉和大蕉。可是，那棵凄然垂泪的树呢？长长的黄绿色叶子一直垂到地上！那能是什么呢？它结着一种甜瓜似的青果。那边又有一片一片的！我打断了邻座客人的第十支小雪茄（*cigarrito*），问他那树叫什么名字。原来是可可树！那柔软的青「甜瓜」，日后便成了我们得用锤子才敲得开的硬壳。此外还有许许多多形态枝叶各异的树，但就眼力所及，它们在新英格兰或纽约也照样能长；唯有棕榈、可可、香蕉与大蕉，是你走遍别的气候带也断然遇不到的特色之树。

===Original===
Thickets﻿—jungles I might call them﻿—abound. It seems as if a bird could hardly get through them; yet they are rich with wild flowers of all forms and colors, the white, the purple, the pink, and the blue. The trees are full of birds of all plumage. There is one like our brilliant oriole. I cannot hear their notes, for the clatter of the train. Stone fences, neatly laid up, run across the lands;﻿—not of our cold bluish-gray granite, the color, as a friend once said, of a miser’s eye, but of soft, warm brown and russet, and well overgrown with creepers, and fringed with flowers. There are avenues, and here are clumps of the prim orange tree, with its dense and deep-green polished foliage gleaming with golden fruit. Now we come to acres upon acres of the sugarcane, looking at a distance like fields of overgrown broom corn. It grows to the height of eight or ten feet, and very thick. An army could be hidden in it. This soil must be deeply and intensely fertile.

There, at the end of an avenue of palms, in a nest of shade trees, is a group of white buildings, with a sea of cane fields about it, with one high furnace chimney, pouring out its volume of black smoke. This is a sugar plantation﻿—my first sight of an *ingenio*; and the chimney is for the steam works of the sugar house. It is the height of the sugar season, and the untiring engine toils and smokes day and night. Ox carts, loaded with cane, are moving slowly to the sugar house from the fields; and about the house, and in the fields, in various attitudes and motions of labor, are the Negroes, men and women and children, some cutting the cane, some loading the carts, and some tending the mill and the furnace. It is a busy scene of distant industry, in the afternoon sun of a languid Cuban day.

Now these groups of white one-story buildings become more frequent, sometimes very near each other, all having the same character﻿—the group of white buildings, the mill, with its tall furnace chimney, and the look of a distillery, and all differing from each other only in the number and extent of the buildings, or in the ornament and comfort of shade trees and avenues about them. Some are approached by broad alleys of the palm, or mango, or orange, and have gardens around them, and stand under clusters of shade trees; while others glitter in the hot sun, on the flat sea of cane fields, with only a little oasis of shade trees and fruit trees immediately about the houses.

===Chinese===
灌木丛——我简直要叫它们丛林——四处丛生。鸟儿似乎都难从中间穿飞过去；然而其间遍布形色各异的野花，白的、紫的、粉红的、蓝的。树上满是各种羽色的鸟儿，有一种像我们那边艳丽的黄鹂。车轮嘈杂，我听不见它们的啼鸣。石砌的围栏齐整地横过田野——不是我们那种冷冷的蓝灰色花岗岩（那颜色，正如一位朋友说过的，像守财奴的眼睛），而是柔和温暖的棕色与褐黄，爬满了藤蔓，镶满了花边。有一道道林荫道，有一簇簇齐整的橘树，浓密深绿、光洁的枝叶间金果闪闪。接着便是一英亩连着一英亩的甘蔗，远看像一片疯长的扫帚高粱田。甘蔗长到八至十英尺高，密密匝匝，一支军队都藏得进去。这土壤必定深厚肥沃极了。

瞧，在一条棕榈林荫道的尽头，一团荫凉树的环抱之中，有一群白色建筑，四周是蔗田的海洋，一根高高的炉烟囱吐着滚滚黑烟。这是一座甘蔗种植园——我头一回见到糖坊（*ingenio*）；那烟囱是供糖坊的蒸汽机房用的。眼下正是榨糖季节的高潮，不知疲倦的引擎日夜操劳、日夜冒烟。装载甘蔗的牛车从田里缓缓驶向糖坊；屋旁田间，黑人男女和孩子以各种劳作的姿势与动作忙碌着——有的砍甘蔗，有的装车，有的看管磨坊和炉灶。在古巴慵懒时日的午后阳光里，这是一幅远景式的繁忙劳作图。

这一群群白色的单层建筑越来越常见，有时彼此相距很近，全都一个性质——白房子群、磨坊、高高的炉烟囱，外加一副酿酒厂似的相貌；彼此的差别只在房屋的多寡大小，或在四周荫凉树与林荫道带来的装饰与舒适。有的由棕榈、芒果或橘树的宽阔林荫道引入，四围有花园，坐落在成片的荫凉树下；有的则在蔗田的平坦海洋上顶着烈日闪闪发亮，只有紧挨着房子的一小片荫凉树与果树的绿洲。

===Original===
I now begin to feel that I am in Cuba; in the tropical, rich, sugar-growing, slave-tilled Cuba. Heretofore, I have seen only the cities and their environs, in which there are more things that are common to the rest of the world. The country life tells the story of any people that have a country life. The New England farmhouse shows the heart of New England. The mansion house and cottage show the heart of Old England. The plantation life that I am seeing and about to see, tells the story of Cuba, the Cuba that has been and that is.

As we stop at one station, which seems to be in the middle of a cane field, the Negroes and Coolies go to the cane, slash off a piece with their knives, cut off the rind and chew the stick of soft, saccharine pulp, the juice running out of their mouths as they eat. They seem to enjoy it so highly, that I am tempted to try the taste of it, myself. But I shall have time for all this at La Ariadne.

These stations consist merely of one or two buildings, where the produce of the neighborhood is collected for transportation, and a which there are very few passengers. The railroad is intended for the carriage of sugar and other produce, and gets its support almost entirely in that way; for it runs through a sparse, rural population, where there are no towns; yet so large and valuable is the sugar crop that I believe the road is well supported. At each station, are its hangers-on of free Negroes, a few slaves on duty as carriers, a few low whites, and now and then someone who looks as if he might be an overseer or *mayoral* of a plantation.

===Chinese===
我此刻才渐渐觉得自己到了古巴——到了这热带的、富庶的、出产蔗糖的、由奴隶耕种的古巴。此前我所见到的只是城市及其近郊，那里与世界其余地方共通的东西更多。乡野生活，道出一切拥有乡野生活的民族的故事。新英格兰的农舍显出新英格兰的心肠，庄园大宅与村舍显出旧英格兰的心肠。我正看到、也将要看到的种植园生活，道出的便是古巴的故事——往昔的古巴与眼下的古巴。

列车在一个仿佛居于蔗田正中的小站停靠时，黑人和苦力们走向甘蔗，用刀劈下一截，削去皮，嚼那柔软含糖的蔗芯，吃得汁水顺着嘴角往外流。他们嚼得那样有滋有味，引得我也想亲口尝一尝。不过到了拉·阿里阿德涅，这些都来得及。

这些车站不过一两座房子，四乡的物产汇集于此待运，乘客寥寥。这条铁路本是为运送食糖及其他物产而设，进项几乎全由此而来；因为它穿行的是人烟稀少的乡野，沿途没有城镇；然而糖的收成如此巨大而值钱，我相信这条路的生意必定不坏。每个车站都围着它那群闲人：几个自由黑人，几个当搬运的值勤奴隶，几个下等白人，偶尔还有一个看上去像是种植园监工（*mayoral*）的人物。

===Original===
**Limonar**, appears in large letters on the small building where we next stop, and I get out and inquire of a squad of idlers for the plantation of Señor C﻿—﻿—. They point to a group of white buildings, about a quarter of a mile distant, standing prettily under high shade trees, and approached by an avenue of orange trees. Getting a tall Negro to shoulder my bag, for a real, I walk to the house. It is an afternoon of exquisite beauty. How can anyone have a weather sensation, in such an air as this? There is no current of the slightest chill anywhere, neither is it oppressively hot. The air is serene and pure and light. The sky gives its mild assurance of settled fair weather. All about me is rich verdure, over a gently undulating surface of deeply fertile country, with here and there a high hill in the horizon, and, on one side, a ridge that may be called mountains. There is no sound but that of the birds, and in the next tree they may be counted by hundreds. Wild flowers, of all colors and scents, cover the ground and the thickets. This is the famous red earth, too. The avenue looks as if it had been laid down with pulverized brick, and all the dust on any object you see is red. Now we turn into the straight avenue of orange trees﻿—prim, deep-green trees, glittering with golden fruit. Here is the one-story, high-roofed house, with long, high piazzas. There is a high wall, carefully whitewashed, enclosing a square with one gate, looking like a garrisoned spot. That must be the Negroes’ quarters; for there is a group of little Negroes at the gate, looking earnestly at the approaching stranger. Beyond is the sugar house, and the smoking chimney, and the ox carts, and the field hands. Through the wide, open door of the mansion, I see two gentlemen at dinner, an older and a younger﻿—the head of gray, and the head of black, and two Negro women, one serving, and the other swinging her brush to disperse the flies. Two big, deep-mouthed hounds come out and bark; and the younger gentleman looks at us, comes out, and calls off the dogs. My Negro stops at the path and touches his hat, waiting permission to go to the piazza with the luggage; for Negroes do not go to the house door without previous leave, in strictly ordered plantations. I deliver my letter, and in a moment am received with such cordial welcome that I am made to feel as if I had conferred a favor by coming out to see them.1

===Chinese===
**利莫纳尔**（Limonar），几个大字写在我们下一站停靠的小房子上。我下了车，向一群闲人打听C——先生的种植园。他们指着一群白色建筑，约四分之一英里开外，雅致地掩映在高大的荫凉树下，一条橘树林荫道直通门前。我雇了个高个子黑人替我扛行李，付他一个雷亚尔，步行前往。这是一个精致之美的下午。在这样的空气里，人怎么还会对天气有知觉呢？四处没有一丝带凉意的气流，也不闷热。空气澄澈、纯净而轻盈。天空温和地许下持续晴好的保证。我四周全是丰茂的绿色，覆在缓缓起伏的深厚肥沃的原野上，地平线上这儿一座、那儿一座高山，一侧还有一道称得上是山的山岭。除了鸟鸣，万籁俱寂；隔壁一棵树上的鸟便数以百计。各色各样、芬芳各异的野花开满地面和灌木丛。这还是那有名的红土。林荫道像铺了一层碾碎的砖屑，你看到的任何东西，上面的尘土都是红的。这时我们拐入笔直的橘树林荫道——一排排齐整的深绿树，金果闪闪。就是这座单层高顶的宅子了，带着长长的游廊。一道精心粉刷过的高墙围出一方院落，只开一门，看着像一处设防之地。那想必是黑人的住处；门口正聚着一群黑孩子，怔怔地望着走来的生人。再过去是糖坊、冒烟的烟囱、牛车和田间劳作的人手。透过宅子敞开的大门，我看见两位先生在用正餐，一老一少——一个灰发，一个黑发——还有两个黑人妇女，一个伺候着，一个挥着拂帚驱赶苍蝇。两条嗓音低沉的大狗奔出来吠叫；年轻些的先生望了望我们，走出来喝退了狗。我的黑人停在路口，碰了碰帽子，等着获准提行李上游廊；因为在规矩森严的种植园里，黑人不得事先许可不能走近宅门。我递上信，不一刻便受到那样热诚的欢迎，倒叫我觉得，肯跑这一趟来看望他们，像是赏了他们多大面子似的。1

## XII / 十二

===Original===
First day on a sugar estate﻿—The coffee estates﻿—Change from coffee estates to sugar estates﻿—Causes and effects of this change﻿—Cultivation of sugarcane﻿—Making of sugar﻿—Profits of sugar making﻿—Process of sugar making, in the fields and mill﻿—Division of labor﻿—Engineer from the United States﻿—Treatment and labor of Negroes﻿—Officers of a plantation, and their duties: *mayoral*, mayordomo, *contra-mayorales*, *boyero*﻿—Duties and cares of the master﻿—Visit to Negro quarters.

At some seasons, a visit may be a favor, on remote plantations; but I know this is the height of the sugar season, when every hour is precious to the master. After a brief toilet, I sit down with them; for they have just begun dinner. In five minutes, I am led to feel as if I were a friend of many years. Both gentlemen speak English like a native tongue. To the younger it is so, for he was born in South Carolina, and his mother is a lady of that State. The family are not here. They do not live on the plantation, but in Matanzas. The plantation is managed by the son, who resides upon it; the father coming but occasionally for a few days, as now, in the busy season.

The dinner is in the Spanish style, which I am getting attached to. I should flee from a joint, or a sirloin. We have rice, excellently cooked, as always in Cuba, eggs with it, if we choose, and fried plantains, sweet potatoes, mixed dishes of fowl and vegetables, with a good deal of oil and seasoning, in which a hot red pepper, about the size of the barberry, prevails. Catalonia wine, which is pretty sure to be pure, is their table claret, while sherry, which also comes direct from the mother country, is for dessert. I have taken them by surprise, in the midst of the busiest season, in a house where there are no ladies; yet the table, the service, the dress and the etiquette, are nonetheless in the style of good society. There seems to be no letting down, where letting down would be so natural and excusable.

===Chinese===
糖业庄园的第一天——咖啡庄园——咖啡庄园向糖业庄园的转变——这一转变的原因与后果——甘蔗的种植——制糖——制糖的利润——田间与磨坊的制糖工序——劳动分工——来自美国的轮机师——黑人的待遇与劳作——种植园的职员及其职责：总管（*mayoral*）、管家（mayordomo）、副总管（*contra-mayorales*）、牛倌（*boyero*）——主人的职责与操劳——探访黑人住区。

在有些季节，到偏僻的种植园做客或许算得上赏脸；但我知道眼下正是榨糖旺季，主人的每个钟头都金贵。略作盥洗，我便同他们一起落座；他们刚开始用正餐。不出五分钟，我就觉得自己仿佛成了相交多年的老友。两位先生说起英语来都像母语。对年轻的那位这本就是母语，因为他生在南卡罗来纳，母亲是那个州的一位淑媛。家眷不在这里。他们不住在种植园上，而是住在马坦萨斯。种植园由儿子打理，他常住园中；父亲只是偶尔来住几天，比如眼下这农忙时节。

正餐是西班牙式的，我渐渐喜欢上它了。若是端上一大块带骨烤肉或牛腰里脊，我倒要逃之夭夭。我们有米饭——古巴的米饭总是烧得极好，愿意的话还可以配上鸡蛋——还有炸大蕉、红薯、禽肉蔬菜合烧的杂烩，用足了油和作料，其中最出味的是一种红辣椒，个头大小同伏牛花相仿。加泰罗尼亚葡萄酒——这酒几乎可以放心是纯的——是他们的佐餐红酒；雪利酒同样直接来自母国，留待餐后。我在最忙的季节、在一户没有女眷的宅子里不速而至，然而餐桌、侍应、衣着与礼数，依然一派上流社会的做派。在这最容易、也最可原谅松懈下来的地方，竟看不出丝毫松懈。

===Original===
I suppose the fact that the land and the agricultural capital of the interior are in the hands of an upper class, which does no manual labor, and which has enough of wealth and leisure to secure the advantages of continued intercourse with city and foreign society, and of occasional foreign travel, tends to preserve throughout the remote agricultural districts, habits and tone and etiquette, which otherwise would die out, in the entire absence of large towns and of high local influences.

Whoever has met with a book called *Evenings in Boston*, and read the story of the old Negro, Saturday, and seen the frontispiece of the Negro fleeing through the woods of St. Domingo, with two little white boys, one in each hand, will know as much of Mr. C﻿—﻿— the elder, as I did the day before seeing him. He is the living hero, or rather subject, for Saturday was the hero, of that tale. His father was a wealthy planter of St. Domingo, a Frenchman, of large estates, with wife, children, friends and neighbors. These were gathered about him in a social circle in his house, when the dreadful insurrection overtook them, and father, mother, sons and daughters were murdered in one night, and only two of the children, boys of eight and ten, were saved by the fidelity of Saturday, an old and devoted house servant. Saturday concealed the boys, got them off the island, took them to Charleston, South Carolina, where they found friends among the Huguenot families, and the refugees from St. Domingo. There Mr. C﻿—﻿— grew up; and after a checkered and adventurous early life, a large part of it on the sea, he married a lady of worth and culture, in South Carolina, and settled himself as a planter, on this spot, nearly forty years ago. His plantation he named El Labarinto (The Labyrinth), after a favorite vessel he had commanded, and for thirty years it was a prosperous cafetal, the home of a happy family, and much visited by strangers from America and Europe. The causes which broke up the coffee estates of Cuba, carried this with the others; and it was converted into a sugar plantation, under the new name of La Ariadne, from the fancy of Ariadne having shown the way out of the Labyrinth. Like most of the sugar estates, it is no longer the regular home of its proprietors.

===Chinese===
我想，内地土地与农业资本握在一个不事体力劳动的上层阶级手中，而这个阶级有足够的财富与闲暇，得以维持与城市及外国社会的不断往来，偶尔还能出国游历——正是这一事实，倾向于在偏远的农业地区保存那些习惯、格调与礼数；若没有大城镇和强有力的本地影响，这些东西本会消亡殆尽。

凡是读过一本题为《波士顿夜话》（*Evenings in Boston*）的书、读过老黑人「星期六」（Saturday）的故事、见过那幅卷首插图——黑人牵着两个小白男孩，一手一个，逃过圣多明各（St. Domingo）树林——的人，对C——老先生的认识，就同我见面头一天一样多了。他就是那段故事的活主人公——或者不如说活的题材，因为主人公本是「星期六」。他的父亲是圣多明各一位富有的种植园主，法国人，坐拥大片田产，有妻子儿女、朋友近邻。那场可怕的大暴动降临时，这些人正聚在他宅中的交际圈里，一夜之间，父亲、母亲、儿子、女儿尽遭杀戮，只有两个孩子——八岁和十岁的男孩——靠「星期六」的忠心得救；「星期六」是家中一个年迈而赤诚的仆人。「星期六」把两个孩子藏起来，送出海岛，带他们到了南卡罗来纳的查尔斯顿，在那里，胡格诺派人家与圣多明各难民之中颇多朋友。C——先生便是在那里长大的；早年生涯坎坷多险，一大部分在海上度过，后来在南卡罗来纳娶了一位贤淑而有教养的女士，落脚此地做了种植园主，至今已近四十年。他把种植园命名为「埃尔·拉瓦林托」（El Labarinto，即「迷宫」），取自他曾指挥过的一条心爱的船；此后三十年，这里是一座兴旺的咖啡园（cafetal），是一个幸福家庭的家宅，美洲与欧洲的游人常来造访。摧毁古巴各咖啡园的那种种原因，把这一座也一并卷走；园子改成了甘蔗种植园，另起新名「拉·阿里阿德涅」（La Ariadne），取阿里阿德涅指引出迷宫之义。同多数糖业庄园一样，它不再是业主安居的家宅了。
===Original===
The change from coffee plantations to sugar plantations﻿—from the cafetal to the *ingenio*, has seriously affected the social, as it has the economic condition of Cuba.

Coffee must grow under shade. Consequently the coffee estate was, in the first place, a plantation of trees, and by the hundred acres. Economy and taste led the planters, who were chiefly the French refugees from St. Domingo to select fruit trees, and trees valuable for their wood, as well as pleasing for their beauty and shade. Under these plantations of trees, grew the coffee plant, an evergreen, and almost an ever-flowering plant, with berries of changing hues, and, twice a year, brought its fruit to maturity. That the coffee might be tended and gathered, avenues wide enough for wagons must be carried through the plantations, at frequent intervals. The plantation was, therefore, laid out like a garden, with avenues and foot paths, all under the shade of the finest trees, and the spaces between the avenues were groves of fruit trees and shade trees, under which grew, trimmed down to the height of five or six feet, the coffee plant. The labor of the plantation was in tending, picking, drying, and shelling the coffee, and gathering the fresh fruits of trees for use and for the market, and for preserves and sweetmeats, and in raising vegetables and poultry, and rearing sheep and horned cattle and horses. It was a beautiful and simple horticulture, on a very large scale. Time was required to perfect this garden﻿—the Cubans call it paradise﻿—of a cafetal; but when matured, it was a cherished home. It required and admitted of no extraordinary mechanical power, or of the application of steam, or of science, beyond the knowledge of soils, of simple culture, and of plants and trees.

For twenty years and more it has been forced upon the knowledge of the reluctant Cubans, that Brazil, the West India Islands to the southward of Cuba, and the Spanish Main, can excel them in coffee raising. The successive disastrous hurricanes of 1843 and 1845, which destroyed many and damaged most of the coffee estates, added to the colonial system of the mother country, which did not give extraordinary protection to this product, are commonly said to have put an end to the coffee plantations. Probably, they only hastened a change which must at some time have come. But the same causes of soil and climate which made Cuba inferior in coffee growing, gave her a marked superiority in the cultivation of sugar. The damaged plantations were not restored as coffee estates, but were laid down to the sugarcane; and gradually, first in the western and northern parts, and daily extending easterly and southerly over the entire island, the exquisite cafetals have been prostrated and dismantled, the groves of shade and fruit trees cut down, the avenues and footpaths ploughed up, and the denuded land laid down to wastes of sugarcane.

===Chinese===
咖啡园向甘蔗园的转变——从咖啡园（cafetal）到糖坊（*ingenio*）——既深刻改变了古巴的经济状况，也深刻改变了它的社会状况。

咖啡必须在荫凉下生长。因此咖啡园首先是一座树木的园子，而且是成百英亩的。节俭与趣味引导种植园主们——他们主要是圣多明各来的法国难民——选择果树和木材珍贵的树，兼取其美观与荫凉。在这些树林之下生长着咖啡树，一种常绿、而且几乎常年开花的植物，果实色泽多变，一年两度成熟。为了照料和采收咖啡，园中必须每隔相当距离就开出容得下马车的林荫道。因此咖啡园布置得如同一座花园，有林荫道，有小径，全都掩在最美妙树木的浓荫下；道与道之间的空地是果树林与荫凉树林，树下便是一行行修剪到五六英尺高的咖啡树。园中的活计，是照料、采摘、晾晒、脱壳咖啡，采集树上的鲜果供自用、应市场、制蜜饯果酱，还有种菜养禽、牧羊饲牛喂马。这是一种规模极大的、美好而简朴的园艺。造就这样一座花园——古巴人管它叫天堂——的咖啡园，需要经年累月；而一旦长成，它便是一处为人眷恋的家。它不需要、也容不下什么非凡的机械力量，用不上蒸汽，也用不上科学，除了关于土壤、简单栽培和草木树艺的知识。

二十多年来，古巴人虽不情愿，却不得不认清：巴西、古巴以南的西印度群岛和西班牙大陆美洲，种咖啡都能胜过他们。1843 年和 1845 年接连的毁灭性飓风毁掉了许多咖啡园、损坏了其余大多数，加上母国的殖民地制度并未给这种产品特别的保护——人们通常说，正是这些终结了咖啡园。也许，它们只是催快了一场迟早要来的变革。但令古巴种咖啡逊色的那些土壤与气候原因，恰恰给了她种蔗制糖的显著优势。受损的园子没有恢复为咖啡园，而是种上了甘蔗；渐渐地，先是西部和北部，继而日益向东、向南蔓延到全岛，那些精致的咖啡园被夷平、拆毁，荫凉树与果树林被砍倒，林荫道与小径被犁翻，赤裸的土地种上了一望无际的甘蔗。

===Original===
The sugarcane allows of no shade. Therefore the groves and avenues must fall. To make its culture profitable, it must be raised in the largest possible quantities that the extent of land will permit. To attempt the raising of fruit, or of the ornamental woods, is bad economy for the sugar planter. Most of the fruits, especially the orange, which is the chief export, ripen in the midst of the sugar season, and no hands can be spared to attend to them. The sugar planter often buys the fruits he needs for daily use and for making preserves, from the neighboring cafetals. The cane ripens but once a year. Between the time when enough of it is ripe to justify beginning to work the mill, and the time when the heat and rains spoil its qualities, all the sugar making of the year must be done. In Louisiana, this period does not exceed eight weeks. In Cuba it is full four months. This gives Cuba a great advantage. Yet these four months are short enough; and during that time, the steam engine plies and the furnace fires burn night and day.

Sugar making brings with it steam, fire, smoke, and a drive of labor, and admits of and requires the application of science. Managed with skill and energy, it is extremely productive. Indifferently managed, it may be a loss. The sugar estate is not valuable, like the coffee estate, for what the land will produce, aided by ordinary and quiet manual labor only. Its value is in the skill, and the character of the labor. The land is there, and the Negroes are there; but the result is loss or gain, according to the amount of labor that can be obtained, and the skill with which the manual labor and the mechanical powers are applied. It is said that at the present time, in the present state of the market, a well-managed sugar estate yields from fifteen to twenty-five percent on the investment. This is true, I am inclined to think, if by the investment be meant only the land, the machinery, and the slaves. But the land is not a large element in the investment. The machinery is costly, yet its value depends on the science applied to its construction and operation. The chief item in the investment is the slave labor. Taking all the slaves together, men, women, and children, the young and the old, the sick and the well, the good and the bad, their market value averages above $1,000 a head. Yet of these, allowing for those too young or too old, for the sick, and for those who must tend the young, the old and the sick, and for those whose labor, like that of the cooks, only sustains the others, not more than one half are able-bodied, productive laborers. The value of this chief item in the investment depends largely on moral and intellectual considerations. How unsatisfactory is it, then, to calculate the profits of the investment, when you leave out of the calculation the value of the controlling power, the power that extorts the contributions of labor from the steam and the engine and the fire, and from the more difficult human will. This is the “plus x” of the formula, which, unascertained, gives us little light as to the result.

===Chinese===
甘蔗容不得半点荫凉。所以树林与林荫道必须倒下。要使种植有利可图，就得在土地允许的范围内尽量多种。对甘蔗园主而言，想兼营水果或观赏木材，是糟糕的经济打算。多数水果——尤其是主要出口的橘子——恰恰在榨糖季节当中成熟，抽不出人手去照料。甘蔗园主日常吃用和做蜜饯的水果，常常要向邻近残存的咖啡园购买。甘蔗一年只熟一茬。从熟到足以开榨，到暑热与雨水败坏其品质，一年的全部制糖必须在这期间完成。在路易斯安那，这个期限不超过八周；在古巴则足足四个月。这给了古巴很大的优势。然而四个月也够紧的了；在这期间，蒸汽机昼夜运转，炉火昼夜不熄。

制糖带来的是蒸汽、烈火、浓烟和连轴转的劳作，它容得下科学，也需要科学。经营得法、干劲十足，则产出极丰；经营不善，则可能亏本。糖业庄园的价值，不像咖啡园那样在于土地借寻常安稳的手工劳动所能出产的东西。它的价值在于技艺，在于劳动的品格。土地在那里，黑人在那里；结果或亏或赚，全看能榨出多少劳动，全看人力与机械之力运用得巧不巧。据说照眼下、按眼下的市况，经营良好的糖业庄园可获投资额百分之十五至二十五的回报。我想这话不假，倘若所谓投资只指土地、机器和奴隶。但土地在投资里并非大头。机器昂贵，但其价值有赖于建造与运转它所应用的学识。投资中的主要项目是奴隶劳动。把奴隶全部算上——男人、女人、孩子，年幼的、年老的、患病的、健康的，好的、坏的——他们平均每人的市价在一千元以上。然而其中，除去太年幼或太年老的、患病的，以及必须照料幼、老、病者的，还有那些活计——比如厨役——只能养活其余人的，能劳动的壮劳力不过一半。投资中这一主要项目的价值，很大程度上取决于道德与智识层面的因素。那么，当你把支配力的价值——那从蒸汽、引擎与烈火之中、也从更难驾驭的人的意志之中榨取劳动贡献的力量——排除在计算之外时，这种投资利润的算法是多么靠不住啊。这就是公式里的「加 x」一项，它一天不确定，我们就一天无从明了结果。

===Original===
But, to return to the changes wrought by this substitution of sugar for coffee. The sugar plantation is no grove, or garden, or orchard. It is not the home of the pride and affections of the planter’s family. It is not a coveted, indeed, hardly a desirable residence. Such families as would like to remain on these plantations, are driven off for want of neighboring society. Thus the estates, largely abandoned by the families of the planters suffer the evils of absenteeism, while the owners live in the suburbs of Havana and Matanzas, and in the Fifth Avenue of New York. The slave system loses its patriarchal character. The master is not the head of a great family, its judge, its governor, its physician, its priest and its father, as the fond dream of the advocates of slavery, and sometimes, doubtless, the reality, made him. Middlemen, in the shape of *administradores*, stand between the owner and the slaves. The slave is little else than an item of labor raised or bought. The sympathies of common home, common childhood, long and intimate relations and many kind offices, common attachments to house, to land, to dogs, to cattle, to trees, to birds﻿—the knowledge of births, sicknesses, and deaths, and the duties and sympathies of a common religion﻿—all those things that may ameliorate the legal relations of the master and slave, and often give to the face of servitude itself precarious but interesting features of beauty and strength﻿—these they must not look to have.

This change has had some effect already, and will produce much more, on the social system of Cuba.

There are still plantations on which the families of the wealthy and educated planters reside. And in some cases the *administrador* is a younger member or a relative of the family, holding the same social position; and the permanent *administrador* will have his family with him. Yet, it is enough to say that the same causes which render the *ingenio* no longer a desirable residence for the owner, make it probable that the *administrador* will be either a dependent or an adventurer; a person from whom the owner will expect a great deal, and the slaves but little, and from whom none will get all they expect, and perhaps none all they are entitled to.

===Chinese===
不过，还是回到以糖代咖啡所造成的变革上来。甘蔗园不是树丛，不是花园，也不是果园。它不是种植园主一家骄傲与情感所系的家。它算不上令人艳羡的居所，甚至谈不上可取。想留在这些园子上的那样一些人家，也因四邻没有社会往来而被挤走了。于是这些庄园大量失去园主家眷，患上业主离园的弊病，而业主们自己则住在哈瓦那和马坦萨斯的郊外，住在纽约的第五大道上。奴隶制失去了它的宗法色彩。主人不再是大家族的首脑——不再是它的法官、长官、医生、祭司与父亲，尽管奴隶制的鼓吹者曾痴梦如此，而且无疑有时也确是现实。中间人以管理员（*administradores*）的形态，插在业主与奴隶之间。奴隶差不多就是一项自养或购进的劳动条目。共同的家、共同的童年、长久亲密的关系与种种善意的照拂、对房屋、土地、狗、牛、树木、飞鸟的共同眷恋——对生老病死的知闻、共同宗教的义务与温情——凡此种种，本可以缓和主奴之间的法律关系，也常给奴役本身的面孔添上虽脆弱却动人的美与力量——这些，都不必指望再有了。

这一变化对古巴的社会制度已经产生了一些影响，将来还会产生更多。

也还有一些种植园，富裕而有教养的种植园主家眷仍住在上面。有些情形下，管理员（*administrador*）是家族中的年轻成员或亲戚，社会地位与主人相当；常驻的管理员也会把家眷带在身边。然而只需说一句：那使糖坊不再为园主所乐于居住的同样原因，也使管理员多半不是个依附者，就是个冒险家；是这样一个人——业主对他期望很高，奴隶对他期望甚微，而谁也得不到所期望的全部，也许谁也得不到应得的全部。
===Original===
In the afternoon we went to the sugar house, and I was initiated into the mysteries of the work. There are four agents: steam, fire, cane juice, and Negroes. The results are sugar and molasses. At this *ingenio*, they make only the Muscovado, or brown sugar. The processes are easily described, but it is difficult to give an idea of the scene. It is one of condensed and determined labor.

To begin at the beginning.﻿—The cane is cut from the fields, by companies of men and women, working together, who use an instrument called a machete, which is something between a sword and a cleaver. Two blows with this slash off the long leaves, and a third blow cuts off the stalk, near to the ground. At this work, the laborers move like reapers, in even lines, at stated distances. Before them is a field of dense, high-waving cane and behind them, strewn wrecks of stalks and leaves. Near, and in charge of the party stands a driver, or more grandiloquently, a *contra-mayoral*, with the short, limber plantation whip, the badge of his office, under his arm.

Oxcarts pass over the field, and are loaded with the cane, which they carry to the mill. The oxen are worked in the Spanish fashion, the yoke being strapped upon the head, close to the horns, instead of being hung round the neck, as with us, and are guided by goads, and by a rope attached to a ring through the nostrils. At the mill, the cane is tipped from the carts into large piles, by the side of the platform. From these piles, it is placed carefully, by hand, lengthwise, in a long trough. This trough is made of slats, and moved by the power of the endless chain, connected with the engine. In this trough, it is carried between heavy, horizontal, cylindrical rollers, where it is crushed, its juice falling into receivers below, and the crushed cane passing off and falling into a pile on the other side.

===Chinese===
下午我们去了糖坊，我被引入这门行当的种种奥妙。作用者有四：蒸汽、炉火、蔗汁和黑人。产出是糖和糖蜜。这座糖坊只造穆斯卡瓦多糖（Muscovado），也就是红糖。工序说来容易，难的是让人对现场有个概念。那是一幅浓缩的、下了死力气的劳动图景。

从头说起。——砍蔗由一队队男女合力进行，他们用一种叫砍刀（machete）的家伙，介于长剑与剁刀之间。两刀削去长叶，第三刀贴地把蔗秆砍断。干这活时，劳动者像收割队一样列成整齐的横排，间隔划一地推进。他们面前是一片密不透风、高高翻涌的蔗海，身后撒满了蔗秆与蔗叶的残骸。近旁督队站着一名监工——说得堂皇些，一名副总管（*contra-mayoral*），腋下夹着那柄柔软的短柄种植园皮鞭，那是他职位的徽记。

牛车从田里驶过，装上甘蔗，运往磨坊。牛是照西班牙式使唤的：轭绑扎在头上、紧贴牛角，而不像我们那样套在颈上；赶牛用刺棒，再用一根拴在鼻环上的绳子引导。到了磨坊，甘蔗从车上翻卸成几大堆，码在平台旁边。再由人手把蔗从堆上一根根顺次码进一条长槽。这槽用板条拼成，由连着引擎的环链带动。甘蔗在槽中被送进几只沉重的卧式圆柱轧辊之间，轧扁了，蔗汁落入下方的接槽，轧过的蔗渣从另一侧排出，堆成一座。

===Original===
This crushed cane, (*bagazo*) falling from between the rollers, is gathered into baskets, by men and women, who carry it on their heads into the fields and spread it for drying. There it is watched and tended as carefully as new-mown grass in haymaking, and raked into cocks or winrows, on an alarm of rain. When dry, it is placed under sheds for protection against wet. From the sheds and from the fields, it is loaded into carts and drawn to the furnace doors, into which it is thrown by Negroes, who crowd it in by the armful, and rake it about with long poles. Here it feeds the perpetual fires by which the steam is made, the machinery moved, and the cane juice boiled. The care of the *bagazo* is an important part of the system; for if that becomes wet and fails, the fires must stop, or resort be had to wood, which is scarce and expensive.
