Thus, on one side of the rollers is the ceaseless current of fresh, full, juicy cane stalks, just cut from the open field; and on the other side, is the crushed, mangled, juiceless mass, drifting out at the draught, and fit only to be cast into the oven and burned. This is the way of the world, as it is the course of art. The cane is made to destroy itself. The ruined and corrupted furnish the fuel and fan the flame that lures on and draws in and crushes the fresh and wholesome; and the operation seems about as mechanical and unceasing in the one case as in the other.

===Chinese===
这轧碎的蔗渣（*bagazo*）从轧辊间落下，由男男女女装进筐里，顶在头上运到田里摊开晾晒。在那里，它像制干草时新割的牧草一样被人细心看守照料，一听有雨，便耙成堆、拢成垄。晒干后，堆进棚里防潮。再从棚里、从田里装上车，拉到炉门口，由黑人扔进炉膛，一抱一抱往里填，用长杆拨来拨去。它喂养着永不停歇的炉火——蒸汽由此而生，机器由此而动，蔗汁由此而沸。照料蔗渣是整个体系里要紧的一环；蔗渣一旦淋湿失效，炉火就得停，否则就得改烧木柴，而柴又稀罕又昂贵。

就这样，轧辊的一侧，是刚从露天田野砍来的、新鲜饱满多汁的蔗秆川流不息；另一侧，是被轧得稀烂、汁水榨尽的残躯，随排出的气流漂出，只配送进炉膛烧掉。世道便是如此，正如技艺的行程。甘蔗被驱使着自我毁灭。败坏溃烂者供应燃料，扇起火焰，引诱、卷入并轧碎新鲜健全者；这一过程在彼在此，似乎同样地机械，同样地无休无止。

===Original===
From the rollers, the juice falls below into a large receiver, from which it flows into great, open vats, called defecators. These defecators are heated by the exhaust steam of the engine, led through them in pipes. All the steam condensed forms water, which is returned warm into the boiler of the engine. In the defecators, as their name denotes, the scum of the juice is purged off, so far as heat alone will do it. From the last defecator, the juice is passed through a trough into the first cauldron. Of the cauldrons, there is a series, or, as they call it, a train, through all which the juice must go. Each cauldron is a large, deep, copper vat, heated very hot, in which the juice seethes and boils. At each, stands a strong Negro, with long, heavy skimmer in hand, stirring the juice and skimming off the surface. This scum is collected and given to the hogs, or thrown upon the muck heap, and is said to be very fructifying. The juice is ladled from one cauldron to the next, as fast as the office of each is finished. From the last cauldron, where its complete crystallization is effected, it is transferred to coolers, which are large, shallow pans. When fully cooled, it looks like brown sugar and molasses mixed. It is then shovelled from the coolers into hogsheads. These hogsheads have holes bored in their bottoms; and, to facilitate the drainage, strips of cane are placed in the hogshead, with their ends in these holes, and the hogshead is filled. The hogsheads are set on open frames, under which are copper receivers, on an inclined plane, to catch and carry off the drippings from the hogsheads. These drippings are the molasses, which is collected and put into tight casks.

I believe I have given the entire process. When it is remembered that all this, in every stage, is going on at once, within the limits of the mill, it may well be supposed to present a busy scene. The smell of juice and of sugar vapor, in all its stages, is intense. The Negroes fatten on it. The clank of the engine, the steady grind of the machines, and the high, wild cry of the Negroes at the cauldrons to the stokers at the furnace doors, as they chant out their directions or wants﻿—now for more fire, and now to scatter the fire﻿—which must be heard above the din, “*A-a-b’la! A-a-b’la!*” “*E-e-cha candela!*” “*Pu-er-ta!*”, and the barbaric African chant and chorus of the gang at work filling the cane-troughs;﻿—all these make the first visit at the sugar house a strange experience. But after one or two visits, the monotony is as tiresome as the first view is exciting. There is, literally, no change in the work. There are the same noises of the machines, the same cries from Negroes at the same spots, the same intensely sweet smell, the same state of the work in all its stages, at whatever hour you visit it, whether in the morning, or evening, at midnight, or at the dawn of the day. If you wake up at night, you hear the “*A-a-b’la A-a-b’la!*” “*E-e-cha! E-e-cha!*” of the cauldron-men crying to the stokers, and the high, monotonous chant of the gangs filling the wagons or the trough, a short, improvisated stave, and then the chorus;﻿—not a tune, like the song of sailors at the tackles and falls but a barbaric, tuneless intonation.

===Chinese===
蔗汁从轧辊落入下面的大接槽，再从那里流进几口敞口大缸，叫做净汁锅（defecators，澄清锅）。净汁锅由引擎的废汽经管道穿入加热。废汽全部冷凝成水，回暖后再送回引擎的锅炉。净汁锅，顾名思义，是单靠热力把蔗汁的浮沫尽行撇去。从最后一口净汁锅，蔗汁经一槽导入第一口锅。锅有一连串，或者照他们的叫法，一「列」（train），蔗汁必须逐口走完。每口锅都是大而深的铜釜，烧得滚热，蔗汁在其中翻腾鼎沸。每口锅旁站着一个健壮的黑人，手执又长又重的撇勺，搅动蔗汁，撇去表面的浮沫。撇出的浮沫收集起来喂猪，或者抛上粪堆，据说极能肥田。每一口锅的工序一完，蔗汁便被一勺勺舀进下一口。在最后一口锅里，结晶彻底完成，随后转入冷却器——一些大而浅的盘。完全冷却后，它看上去就像红糖掺了糖蜜。然后把它从冷却器铲进大桶（hogsheads）。桶底钻了孔；为助沥干，桶里放上甘蔗条，末端插进孔里，再把桶装满。大桶架在敞开的架子上，下面是斜置的铜接槽，承接并导走桶里滴下的液滴。滴下来的就是糖蜜，收集起来装进密封的桶。

我想我已经把全部工序说完了。若记得这一切、每一道环节，都同时发生在磨坊这方寸之地里，便可想见那是怎样一幅忙碌景象。蔗汁与糖汽的气味，在各个阶段都浓烈逼人。黑人们被它养得肥壮。引擎的铿锵、机器匀速的碾磨、锅边的黑人朝炉门口的司炉高声呼号、唱出他们的指令或需求——时而要加火，时而要拨火——那必须盖过一切喧嚣：「*A-a-b'la! A-a-b'la!*」「*E-e-cha candela!*」「*Pu-er-ta!*」，还有往蔗槽里填蔗那一班人蛮荒的非洲调子和唱；——凡此种种，使初访糖坊成了一场奇异的经历。但去过一两回之后，那单调便同初见的兴奋一样叫人厌倦。这活计简直没有变化。无论你何时去看——清晨、傍晚、半夜还是黎明——机器是同样的声响，黑人在同样的位置呼喊，同样浓烈的甜香，各道工序停在同样的状态。夜里若醒来，会听见锅边人朝司炉喊的「*A-a-b'la A-a-b'la!*」「*E-e-cha! E-e-cha!*」，还有装车、填槽那一班人高亢而单调的号子——短短一段即兴的叠句，然后是齐声的和唱；不像水手在滑车缆索边唱的那种成调的歌，而是一种蛮荒的、不成曲调的吟诵。
===Original===
When I went into the sugar house, I saw a man with an unmistakably New England face in charge of the engine, with that look of intelligence and independence so different from the intelligence and independence of all other persons.

“Is not that a New England man?”

“Yes,” said Mr. C﻿—﻿—, “he is from Lowell; and the engine was built in Lowell.”

When I found him at leisure, I made myself known to him, and he sat down on the brickwork of the furnace, and had a good unburdening of talk; for he had not seen anyone from the United States for three months. He talked, like a true Yankee, of law and politics﻿—the Lowell Bar and Mr. Butler, Mr. Abbott and Mr. Wentworth; of the Boston Bar and Mr. Choate; of Massachusetts politics and Governor Banks; and of national politics and the Thirty Millions Bill, and whether it would pass, and what if it did.

===Chinese===
我走进糖坊时，看见一张一望而知是新英格兰人的面孔在掌管引擎，带着那种聪明而独立的神气，同所有旁人的聪明与独立都判然有别。

「那位不是新英格兰人吗？」

「是的，」C——先生说，「他是洛厄尔人；这台引擎也是洛厄尔造的。」

趁他得空，我上前自报家门，他在炉子的砖台上坐下，痛痛快快地倾诉了一场；他已有三个月没见到合众国来的人了。他像个地道的扬基佬那样谈法律、谈政治——谈洛厄尔律师界和巴特勒先生、阿博特先生、温特沃思先生；谈波士顿律师界和乔特先生；谈马萨诸塞的政治和班克斯州长；还谈全国政治和「三千万法案」，能不能通过，通过了又当如何。

===Original===
This engineer is one of a numerous class, whom the sugar culture brings annually to Cuba. They leave home in the autumn, engage themselves for the sugar season, put the machinery in order, work it for the four or five months of its operation, clean and put it in order for lying by, and return to the United States in the spring. They must be machinists, as well as engineers; for all the repairs and contrivances, so necessary in a remote place, fall upon them. Their skill is of great value, and while on the plantation their work is incessant, and they have no society or recreations whatever. The occupation, however, is healthful, their position independent, and their pay large. This engineer had been several years in Cuba, and I found him well informed, and, I think, impartial and independent. He tells me, which I had also heard in Havana, that this plantation is a favorable specimen, both for skill and humanity, and is managed on principles of science and justice, and yields a large return. On many plantations﻿—on most, I suspect, from all I can learn﻿—the Negroes, during the sugar season, are allowed but four hours for sleep in the twenty-four, with one for dinner, and a half hour for breakfast, the night being divided into three watches, of four hours each, the laborer taking their turns. On this plantation, the laborers are in two watches, and divide the night equally between them, which gives them six hours for sleep. In the day, they have half an hour for breakfast and one hour for dinner. Here, too, the very young and the very old are excused from the sugar house, and the nursing mothers have lighter duties and frequent intervals of rest. The women worked at cutting the cane, feeding the mill, carrying the *bagazo* in baskets, spreading and drying it, and filling the wagons; but not in the sugar house itself, or at the furnace doors. I saw that no boys or girls were in the mill﻿—none but full grown persons. The very small children do absolutely nothing all day, and the older children tend the cattle and run of errands. And the engineer tells me that in the long run this liberal system of treatment, as to hours and duties, yields a better return than a more stringent rule.

===Chinese===
这位轮机师属于一大类人，糖业种植每年把他们带到古巴来。他们秋天离家，受雇一个榨糖季，把机器检修妥当，在四、五个月的运转期里操作它，停机后再清洗、封存，春天返回合众国。他们必须既是机械工，又是轮机师；因为在偏远地方，一切必不可少的修理与改制都落在他们身上。他们的技艺极有价值，而在种植园上，他们的活计没完没了，又全无社交与消遣可言。不过这行当有益健康，地位独立，报酬丰厚。这位轮机师来古巴已有好几年，我发现他见闻广博，而且我想，持论公允而不偏倚。他告诉我——这话我在哈瓦那也听说过——这座种植园无论在技艺还是人道方面都是一个良好的标本，以科学与公正的原则管理，出产丰厚。在许多种植园上——就我所能打听的，我疑心是在大多数上——榨糖季节里，黑人二十四小时中只准睡四个钟头，外加正餐一个钟头、早饭半个钟头；夜晚分作三班，每班四个钟头，由劳动者轮值。在这座园里，劳动者分两班，均分夜晚，这样每人可睡六个钟头。白天，他们有半个钟头吃早饭、一个钟头吃正餐。在这里，太年幼的和太年老的也不派进糖坊，哺乳的母亲干轻活，且常有间歇休息。妇女们砍蔗、喂磨、用筐顶运蔗渣、摊晒蔗渣、装车，但不干糖坊里的活，也不守炉门。我看到磨坊里没有男孩女孩——全是成年的人。很小的孩子整天什么也不干，大些的孩子放牛、跑腿。轮机师还告诉我，从长远看，这种在工时与职责上宽厚的做法，比严苛的章程出产更丰。

===Original===
He thinks the crop this year, which has been a favorable one, will yield, in well-managed plantations a net interest of from fifteen to twenty-five percent on the investment; making no allowance, of course, for the time and skill of the master. This will be a clear return to planters like Mr. C﻿—﻿— who do not eat up their profits by interest on advances, and have no mortgages, and require no advances from the merchants.

But the risks of the investment are great. The cane fields are liable to fires, and these spread with great rapidity, and are difficult to extinguish.2 Last year Mr. C﻿—﻿— lost $7,000 in a few hours by fire. In the cholera season he lost $12,000 in a few days by deaths among the Negroes.

According to the usual mode of calculation, I suppose the value of the investment of Mr. C﻿—﻿— to be between $125,000 and $150,000. On well-managed estates of this size, the expenses should not exceed $10,000.

The gross receipts, in sugar and molasses, at a fair rate of the markets, cannot average less than between $35,000 and $40,000. This should leave a profit of between eighteen and twenty-two percent. Still, the worth of an estimate depends on the principle on which the capital is appraised. The number of acres laid down to cane, on this plantation, is about three hundred. The whole number of Negroes is one hundred, and of these not more than half, at any time, are capable of efficient labor; and there are twenty-two children below the age of five years, out of a total of one hundred Negroes.

===Chinese===
他认为今年这一季——年成不错——在经营良好的种植园，可获投资额百分之十五至二十五的净利；当然，主人付出的时间与技艺是不计价的。对C——先生这样不吃借支利息、没有抵押、也不需商人垫款的园主，这便是净收益了。

但这投资的风险不小。蔗田容易失火，火势蔓延极快，扑灭很难。2 去年C——先生一场火，几个钟头便损失了七千元。霍乱流行的季节，他几天之内因黑人死亡，损失了一万二千元。

照通常的算法，我估计C——先生的投资在十二万五千至十五万元之间。这样规模、经营良好的庄园，开销应不超过一万元。

食糖与糖蜜的毛收入，按市面公道价格计，平均不会低于三万五千至四万元。这该剩下百分之十八至二十二的红利。尽管如此，一项估算值不值钱，全看本金作价依据什么原理。这座园子种蔗的土地约三百英亩。黑人总共一百名，其中任何时点能顶用的劳力不过一半；而一百名黑人中，五岁以下的幼儿有二十二名。
===Original===
Beside the engineer, some large plantations have one or more white assistants; but here an intelligent Negro has been taught enough to take charge of the engine when the engineer is off duty. This is the highest post a Negro can reach in the mill, and this Negro was mightily pleased when I addressed him as *maquinista*. There are, also, two or three white men employed, during the season, as sugar masters. Their post is beside the cauldrons and defecators, where they are to watch the work in all its stages, regulate the heat and the time for each removal, and oversee the men. These, with the engineer, make the force of white men who are employed for the season.

The regular and permanent officers of a plantation are the *mayoral* and mayordomo. The *mayoral* is, under the master or his *administrador*, the chief mate or first lieutenant of the ship. He has the general oversight of the Negroes, at their work or in their houses, and has the duty of exacting labor and enforcing discipline. Much depends on his character, as to the comfort of master and slaves. If he is faithful and just, there may be ease and comfort; but if he is not, the slaves are never sure of justice, and the master is sure of nothing. The *mayoral* comes, of necessity, from the middle class of whites, and is usually a native Cuban, and it is not often that a satisfactory one can be found or kept. The day before I arrived, in the height of the season, Mr. C﻿—﻿— had been obliged to dismiss his *mayoral*, on account of his conduct to the women, which was producing the worst results with them and with the men; and not long before, one was dismissed for conniving with the Negroes in a wholesale system of theft, of which he got the lion’s share.

===Chinese===
除轮机师外，有些大种植园还雇一两名白人助手；这里则是一个聪明的黑人学会了足够的手艺，轮机师下班时由他掌管引擎。这是黑人在磨坊里所能升到的最高职位；我用「轮机师」（*maquinista*）称呼他时，这个黑人得意极了。还有两三个白人受雇于榨糖季，当糖师傅（sugar masters）。他们的岗位在锅与净汁锅旁，负责监察各道工序，掌握火候与每次转倒的时机，并督率工人。这些人加上轮机师，便是榨糖季雇用的白人班底。

种植园常设的职员是总管（*mayoral*）与管家（mayordomo）。总管在主人或其管理员（*administrador*）之下，好比船上的大副。他总管黑人，无论在活计上还是在住屋里，职责便是榨取劳动、整肃纪律。主奴的安适，很大程度系于他的品性。他若忠厚公正，上下还能安生；若不然，奴隶永无公道可指望，主人则什么也拿不稳。总管必然出身白人中等阶层，通常是古巴本地人，而称职的人既难找到，也难留住。我到的前一天，正值旺季顶上，C——先生不得不辞退了他的总管，因为此人对妇女的行径，正在她们和男人们中间酿成最恶劣的后果；不久之前，还有一位因为同黑人串通大规模行窃、自己坐地分赃得了大头而遭辞退。

===Original===
The mayordomo is the purser, and has the immediate charge of the stores, produce, materials for labor, and provisions for consumption, and keeps the accounts. On well regulated plantations, he is charged with all the articles of use or consumption, and with the products as soon as they are in condition to be numbered, weighed, or counted, and renders his accounts of what is consumed or destroyed, and of the produce sent away.

There is also a *boyero*, who is the herdsman, and has charge of all the cattle. He is sometimes a Negro.

Under the *mayoral*, are a number of *contra-mayorales*, who are the boatswain’s mates of the ship, and correspond to the “drivers” of our southern plantations. One of them goes with every gang when set to work, whether in the field or elsewhere, and whether men or women, and watches and directs them, and enforces labor from them. The drivers carry under the arm, at all times, the short, limber plantation whip, the badge of their office and their means of compulsion. They are almost always Negroes; and it is generally thought that Negroes are not more humane in this office than the low whites. On this plantation, it is three years since any slave has been whipped; and that punishment is never inflicted here on a woman. Near the Negro quarters, is a penitentiary, which is of stone, with three cells for solitary confinement, each dark, but well ventilated. Confinement in these, on bread and water, is the extreme punishment that has been found necessary for the last three years. The Negro fears solitude and darkness, and covets his food, fire, and companionship.

===Chinese===
管家好比船上的事务长，直接经管库房、产品、劳动材料和口粮，并掌账目。在规制井然的种植园里，一切用项消耗之物归他管；产品一到可以计数、称重、清点的程度也归他管，他须交代清楚消耗损坏之数与外发产品之数。

另有一名牛倌（*boyero*），是牧牛人，管所有的牲口。有时由黑人担任。

总管之下有若干副总管（*contra-mayorales*），好比船上的水手长助理，相当于我们南方种植园里的「监工」（drivers）。每班人派出干活时——无论在田里还是别处，无论是男是女——都有一名跟着，看管、指点并强制他们劳动。监工们腋下随时夹着那柄柔软的短柄种植园皮鞭，那是职位的徽记，也是强制的手腕。他们几乎全是黑人；人们一般认为，黑人当这差并不比下等白人更仁慈。在这座园里，已有三年没鞭打过任何奴隶；那种刑罚在这里也从不加于妇女。黑人住区近旁有一座监狱，石头砌的，有三间单独监禁的牢房，各自幽暗而通风良好。关押其中，只给面包和水，便是三年来所用过的极刑。黑人怕孤独与黑暗，看重他的饭食、炉火与伙伴。

===Original===
With all the corps of hired white labor, the master must still be the real power, and on his character the comfort and success of the plantation depend. If he has skill as a chemist, a geologist, or a machinist, it is not lost; but, except as to the engineer, who may usually be relied upon, the master must be capable of overseeing the whole economy of the plantation, or all will go wrong. His chief duty is to oversee the overseers; to watch his officers, the *mayoral*, the mayordomo, the *boyero*, and the sugar masters. These are mere hirelings, and of a low sort, such as a slave system reduces them to; and if they are lazy, the work slackens; and if they are ill-natured, somebody suffers. The mere personal presence of the master operates as a stimulus to the work. This afternoon young Mr. C﻿—﻿— and I took horses and rode out to the cane field, where the people were cutting. They had been at work a half hour. He stopped his horse where they were when we came to them, and the next half hour, without a word from him, they had made double the distance of the first. It seems to me that the work of a plantation is what a clock would be that always required a man’s hand pressing on the main spring. With the slave, the ultimate sanction is force. The motives of pride, shame, interest, ambition, and affection may be appealed to, and the minor punishments of degradation in duties, deprivation of food and sleep, and solitary confinement, may be resorted to; but the whip which the driver always carries, reminds the slave that if all else fails, the infliction of painful bodily punishment lies behind, and will be brought to bear, rather than that the question be left unsettled. Whether this extreme be reached, and how often it be reached, depends on the personal qualities of the master. If he is lacking in self-control, he will fall into violence. If he has not the faculty of ruling by moral and intellectual power﻿—be he ever so humane, if he is not firm and intelligent, the bad among the slaves will get the upper hand, and he will be in danger of trying to recover his position by force. Such is the reasoning à priori.

===Chinese===
纵有这一整班雇来的白人劳工，主人仍须是真正的权力所在，庄园的安适与成败全系于他的品格。他若有化学家、地质学家或机械师的本事，绝不会被埋没；但除了轮机师通常可以信赖之外，主人必须能监察种植园的全部经济，否则一切都要乱套。他的首要职责是监察那些监察者；看住他的职员——总管、管家、牛倌和糖师傅们。这些人不过是雇工，而且是奴录制把他们压低成的下等雇工；他们若懒惰，活计便松懈；他们若心地不善，就有人吃苦。主人亲身在场，本身就是对劳作的一种刺激。这天下午，小C——先生和我骑马出去，到人们正在砍蔗的田里。我们到时，他们已经干了半个钟头。他把马停在我们初见他们的地方，随后半个钟头，他一言未发，他们推进的距离却比头半个钟头多了一倍。在我看来，种植园的活计，就像一座永远要人手压住主发条的钟。对奴隶，终极的制裁是强力。骄傲、羞耻、利害、雄心与情感，都可以诉诸；降等罚役、克扣饮食睡眠、单独监禁等轻刑，也可以施用；但监工永远随身带着的那条鞭子提醒奴隶：倘若这一切都不奏效，肉体的痛楚还悬在后头，而且宁可使出来，也不让事情悬而不决。这种极端是否会被触及、多久触及一次，全看主人的个人质地。他若缺乏自制，便会流于暴戾。他若没有以道德与智识之力驾驭的本事——哪怕他再仁慈——只要不够坚定、不够明察，奴隶中的坏种便会占了上风，他便有动用武力以图收复地位的危殆。这便是先验的推论。

===Original===
At six o’clock, the large bell tolls the knell of parting day and the call to the Oracion, which any who are religious enough can say, wherever they may be, at work or at rest. In the times of more religious strictness, the bell for the Oracion, just at dusk, was the signal for prayer in every house and field, and even in the street, and for the benediction from parent to child and master to servant. Now, in the cities, it tolls unnoticed, and on the plantations, it is treated only as the signal for leaving off work. The distribution of provisions is made at the storehouse, by the mayordomo, my host superintending it in person. The people take according to the number in their families; and so well acquainted are all with the apportionment, that in only one or two instances were inquiries necessary. The kitchen fires are lighted in the quarters, and the evening meal is prepared. I went into the quarters before they were closed. A high wall surrounds an open square, in which are the houses of the Negroes. This has one gate, which is locked at dark; and to leave the quarters after that time, is a serious offence. The huts were plain, but reasonably neat, and comfortable in their construction and arrangement. In some were fires, round which, even in this hot weather, the Negroes like to gather. A group of little Negroes came round the strange gentleman, and the smallest knelt down with uncovered heads, in a reverent manner, saying, “*Buenos dias Señor.*” I did not understand the purpose of this action and as there was no one to explain the usage to me, I did them the injustice to suppose that they expected money, and distributed some small coins among them. But I learned afterwards that they were expecting the benediction﻿—the hand on the head, and the “*Dios te haga bueno.*” It was touching to see their simple, trusting faces turned up to the stranger﻿—countenances not yet wrought by misfortune, or injury, or crime, into the strong expressions of mature life. None of these children, even the smallest, was naked, as one usually sees them in Havana. In one of the huts, a proud mother showed me her Herculean twin boys, sprawling in sleep on the bed. Before dark, the gate of the quarters is bolted, and the night is begun. But the fires of the sugar house are burning, and half of the working people are on duty there for their six hours.

===Chinese===
六点钟，大钟敲响，那是白昼离去的丧钟，也是晚祷（Oracion）的召号，凡有足够虔诚的人，无论身在何处、劳作或安歇，都可以诵念。在宗教更严的时代，暮色初临的晚祷钟，是家家户户、田间地头、乃至街头巷尾祈祷的信号，是父母给儿女、主人给仆人的祝福。如今在城市里，钟声敲过无人在意；在种植园上，它只被当作收工的信号。口粮在库房前分发，由管家执掌，我的东家亲自督察。各家按人口领取；人人对分例烂熟于心，只有一两处需要问一问。住区里炊火升起，晚饭忙碌起来。天黑闭门之前，我进了住区。一道高墙围着一片敞开的场院，院里是黑人们的屋子。院门只有一扇，天黑上锁；此后擅出住区是一桩重过。小屋简陋，构造与布置却还算整洁舒适。有的屋里生了火，哪怕在这样的热天，黑人也爱围着火堆坐。一群黑孩子围住这位生客，最小的几个光着头跪下，一脸虔敬地说：「*Buenos dias Señor.*」（先生日安）我不明白此举是什么意思，又无人为我解说这习俗，竟委屈了他们，以为他们是想讨钱，散了些小钱给他们。后来我才知道，他们是在等祝福——等一只手按在头上，等一句「*Dios te haga bueno.*」（愿上帝使你向善）看他们仰起望着生客的一张张单纯、信赖的脸——还不曾被厄运、伤害或罪恶铸成成年生活那种强硬面容的脸——真叫人动容。这些孩子，连最小的在内，没有一个像在哈瓦那常见的那样赤身露体。在一间小屋里，一位自豪的母亲指给我看她那对大力士般的孪生男孩，正摊开手脚睡在床上。天黑之前，住区的大门上了闩，一夜就此开始。但糖坊的炉火还烧着，一半劳作的人正要在那里值他们的六个钟头。

===Original===
I sat for several hours with my host and his son, in the veranda, engaged in conversation, agreeable and instructive to me, on those topics likely to present themselves to a person placed as I was;﻿—the state of Cuba, its probable future, its past, its relations to Europe and the United States, slavery, the Coolie problem, the free-Negro labor problem, and the agriculture, horticulture, trees and fruits of the island. The elder gentleman retired early, as he was to take the early train for Matanzas.

My sleeping room is large and comfortable, with brick floor and glass windows, pure white bed linen and mosquito net, and ewer and basin scrupulously clean, bringing back, by contrast, visions of Le Grand’s, and Antonio, and Domingo, and the sounds and smells of those upper chambers. The only moral I am entitled to draw from this is, that a well-ordered private house with slave labor, may be more neat and creditable than an ill-ordered public house with free labor. As the stillness of the room comes over me, I realize that I am far away in the hill country of Cuba, the guest of a planter, under this strange system, by which one man is enthroned in the labor of another race, brought from across the sea. The song of the Negroes breaks out afresh from the fields, where they are loading up the wagons﻿—the barbaric undulation of sound:﻿—

> “*Na-nú, A-yá﻿—Na-né, A-yá:*”

and the recurrence of here and there a few words of Spanish, among which “Mañana” seemed to be a favorite. Once, in the middle of the night, I waked, to hear the strains again, as they worked in the open field, under the stars.

===Chinese===
我和东家父子在游廊上坐了几个钟头，交谈的内容对我是既愉快又有教益，都是处在我这位置上自然会碰到的话题——古巴的现状、它可能的未来、它的过去、它与欧洲和合众国的关系、奴隶制、苦力问题、自由黑人劳工问题，还有岛上的农业、园艺、树木与果木。老先生早早告退了，他要赶去马坦萨斯的早班火车。

我的卧房宽敞舒适，砖地玻璃窗，卧具雪白，挂着蚊帐，水罐面盆洁净得一丝不苟，对照之下，勒格朗旅馆、安东尼奥、多明戈，还有那些顶层阁楼里的声音与气味，一齐浮上眼前。我从这对比中唯一有权得出的教训是：一座井然有序的、用奴隶劳力的私宅，可以比一座乱糟糟的、用自由劳力的客栈更整洁、更体面。当屋里的静谧笼罩下来，我才意识到，自己已身处古巴的丘陵腹地深处，是一位种植园主的客人，置身于这套奇异的制度之下——凭这套制度，一个人被高置于另一个漂洋过海贩来的种族的劳动之上。黑人的歌声从装车的田里重新响起——那蛮荒起伏的声浪：——

> 「*Na-nú, A-yá—Na-né, A-yá:*」

其中零零星星夹着几个西班牙语的词，「Mañana」（明天）似乎是个爱用的词。半夜里我醒过一回，又听见那调子——他们正在星空下的露天田里干活。

## XIII / 十三

===Original===
Trees and flowers﻿—Chameleon﻿—Fruits﻿—Red ant﻿—Meals and routine of life on plantation﻿—Penitentiary, lying-in room, etc.﻿—Señor Bourgoise﻿—Third day at La Ariadne﻿—Effects of foreign education on planters.

When I came out from my chamber this morning, the elder Mr. C﻿—﻿— had gone. The watchful Negress brought me coffee, and I could choose between oranges and bananas, for my fruit. The young master had been in the saddle an hour or so. I sauntered to the sugar house. It was past six, and all hands were at work again, amid the perpetual boiling of the cauldrons, the skimming and dipping and stirring, the cries of the cauldron-men to the firemen, the slow gait of the wagons, and the perpetual to-and-fro of the carriers of the cane. The engine is doing well enough, and the engineer has the great sheet of the *New York Weekly Herald*, which he is studying, in the intervals of labor, as he sits on the corner of the brickwork.

===Chinese===
树木与花卉——变色蜥蜴——果木——白蚁（红蚁）——种植园的饮食与日常起居——监狱、产房等——布尔茹瓦先生——拉·阿里阿德涅的第三天——外国教育对种植园主的影响。

今天早晨我从房里出来时，C——老先生已经走了。那机警的黑人妇女给我端来咖啡，水果我可以在橘子和香蕉之间挑选。少东家上马已经个把钟头了。我信步走向糖坊。已过六点，所有人手又干上了活，四下是锅子里永不停歇的沸腾，撇沫、舀倒、搅动，锅边人朝司炉的呼号，大车缓慢的步子，还有运蔗人来来回回不停的穿梭。引擎运转得还不错，轮机师手里拿着一大张《纽约每周论坛报》（*New York Weekly Herald*），坐在炉子砖台的角上，趁劳动的间隙研读着。

===Original===
But a turn in the garden is more agreeable, among birds, and flowers, and aromatic trees. Here is a mignonette tree, forty feet high, and every part is full and fragrant with flowers, as is the little mignonette in our flowerpots. There is the allspice, a large tree, each leaf strong enough to flavor a dish. Here is the tamarind tree: I must sit under it, for the sake of the old song. My young friend joins me, and points out, on the allspice tree, a chameleon. It is about six inches long, and of a pea-green color. He thinks its changes of color, which are no fable, depend on the will or on the sensations, and not on the color of the object the animal rests upon. This one, though on a black trunk, remained pale green. When they take the color of the tree they rest on, it may be to elude their enemies, to whom their slow motions make them an easy prey. At the corner of the house stands a pomegranate tree, full of fruit, which is not yet entirely ripe; but we find enough to give a fair taste of its rich flavor. Then there are sweet oranges, and sour oranges, and limes and coconuts, and pineapples, the latter not entirely ripe, but in the condition in which they are usually plucked for our market, and abundance of fuschias, and Cape jasmines, and the highly prized night-blooming cereus.

===Chinese===
不过，到花园里转一圈更惬意，那里有鸟、有花、有芬芳的树。这是一棵木樨草树，高达四十英尺，从头到脚开满了花，香气四溢，正如我们花盆里那小小的木樨草。那是多香果，一株大树，每片叶子都浓得足以给一盘菜提味。这里是罗望子树：看在那支老歌的分上，我得在树下坐一坐。我的年轻朋友过来陪我，指给我看多香果树上一只变色蜥蜴。它约六英寸长，豌豆般的绿色。他认为它变色的本事——这绝非虚传——取决于它的意愿或感觉，而不取决于它所栖止之物的颜色。这一只虽然趴在黑色的树干上，始终保持着淡绿色。它们若变得同栖身的树一个颜色，或许是为了躲避天敌——它们动作迟缓，在天敌眼里是唾手可得的猎物。屋角立着一棵石榴树，果实累累，还未全熟；但我们总能找到几个，足可一品它那浓郁的滋味。此外还有甜橙、酸橙、来檬和椰子，还有菠萝——后者尚未全熟，但正是运往我们市场时通常采摘的状态——还有满树繁盛的倒挂金钟、栀子，以及为人珍视的夜皇后（昙花）。

===Original===
The most frequent shade tree here is the mango. It is a large, dense tree, with a general resemblance, in form and size, to our lime or linden. Three noble trees stand before the door, in front of the house. One is a Tahiti almond, another a mango, and the third a cedar. And in the distance is a majestic tree, of incredible size, which is, I believe, a ceiba. When this estate was a cafetal, the house stood at the junction of four avenues, from the four points of the compass: one of the sweet orange, one of the sour orange, one of palms, and one of mangoes. Many of these trees fell in the hurricanes of 1843 and ’45. The avenue which leads from the road, and part of that leading towards the sugar house, are preserved. The rest have fallen a sacrifice to the sugarcane; but the garden, the trees about the house, and what remains of the avenues, give still a delightful appearance of shelter and repose.

I have amused myself by tracing the progress, and learning the habits of the red ants, a pretty formidable enemy to all structures of wood. They eat into the heart of the hardest woods; not even the lignum vitæ, or iron wood, or cedar, being proof against them. Their operations are secret. They never appear upon the wood, or touch its outer shell. A beam or rafter stands as ever with a goodly outside; but you tap it, and find it a shell. Their approaches, too, are by covered ways. When going from one piece of wood to another, they construct a covered way, very small and low, as a protection against their numerous enemies, and through this they advance to their new labors. I think that they may sap the strength of a whole roof of rafters, without the observer being able to see one of them, unless he breaks their covered ways, or lays open the wood.

===Chinese===
这里最常见的荫凉树是芒果。这是一种高大茂密的树，形状与大小大体像我们的菩提树或椴树。屋前门口挺立着三棵堂皇的大树：一棵是塔希提杏仁树，一棵是芒果，第三棵是雪松。远处还有一株雄伟的树，大得令人难以置信，我想那是木棉。当年这座园子还是咖啡园时，宅子立在四条林荫道的交汇处，四条道通向罗盘的四方位：一条甜橙，一条酸橙，一条棕榈，一条芒果。这些树许多都倒在了 1843 年和 1845 年的飓风里。从大路引来的那条道，还有通往糖坊那条的一段，保存了下来。其余的都作了甘蔗的祭品；但花园、宅旁的树木，加上林荫道的遗迹，仍给这地方一种荫庇安憩的可爱气象。

我拿观察白蚁（红蚁）的行进、探究它们的习性作消遣——对一切木结构来说，它们都是相当可怕的敌人。它们能蛀进最坚硬的木头的心里；连愈疮木、铁木、雪松也挡不住它们。它们的作业是隐秘的。它们从不在木头上露面，也不碰外壳。一根梁或椽子外壳依旧体面如初；你一敲，才发现是个空壳。它们的来路也走暗道。从一根木头转移到另一根时，它们修筑一条廊道，又小又矮，以防御众多的敌人，再从这条道开赴新的工地。我想，它们可以把整面屋顶的椽子蛀空，而观察者连一只也见不着——除非他扒开它们的廊道，或者剖开木头。

===Original===
The course of life at the plantation is after this manner. At six o’clock, the great bell begins the day, and the Negroes go to their work. The house servants bring coffee to the family and guests, as they appear or send for it. The master’s horse is at the door, under the tree, as soon as it is light, and he is off on his tour, before the sun rises. The family breakfasts at ten o’clock, and the people﻿—*la gente*, as the technical phrase is for the laborers, break fast at nine. The breakfast is like that of the cities, with the exception of fish and the variety of meats, and consists of rice, eggs, fried plantains, mixed dishes of vegetables and fowls, other meats rarely, and fruits, with claret or Catalonia and coffee. The time for the siesta or rest, is between breakfast and dinner. Dinner hour is three for the family, and two for the people. The dinner does not differ much from the breakfast, except that there is less of fruit and more of meat, and that some preserve is usually eaten, as a dessert. Like the breakfast, it ends with coffee. In all manner of preserves, the island is rich. The almond, the guava, the cocoa, the soursop, the orange, the lime, and the mamey ap ple, afford a great variety. After dinner, and before dark, is the time for long drives; and, when the families are on the estates, for visits to neighbors. There is no third meal; but coffee, and sometimes tea, is offered at night The usual time for bed is as early as ten o’clock, for the day begins early, and the chief outdoor works and active recreations must be had before breakfast.

In addition to the family house, the Negro quarters, and the sugar house, there is a range of stone buildings, ending with a kitchen, occupied by the engineer, the *mayoral*, the *boyero*, and the mayordomo, who have an old Negro woman to cook for them, and another to wait on them. There is also another row of stone buildings, comprising the storehouse, the penitentiary, the hospital, and the lying-in room. The penitentiary, I have described. The hospital and lying-in room are airy, well ventilated, and suitable for their purposes. Neither of them had any tenants today. In the centre of the group of buildings, is a high frame, on which hangs the great bell of the plantation. This rings the Negroes up in the morning, and in at night, and sounds the hours for meals. It calls all in, on any special occasion, and is used for an alarm to the neighboring plantations, rung long and loud, in case of fire in the cane fields, or other occasions for calling in aid.

===Chinese===
种植园的生活是这样安排的。六点钟，大钟开始了一天，黑人上工去了。宅仆把咖啡送到家人和客人面前，随他们起身或招呼而定。天一亮，主人的马就备在门口树下，太阳还没升起，他已出巡去了。全家用早饭在十点；干活的人们——行话叫「*la gente*」（那些人）——九点用饭。早饭同城市里的相仿，只是没有鱼，肉的种类也少些，有米饭、鸡蛋、炸大蕉、蔬菜禽肉合烧的杂烩，别的肉难得一见，还有水果，佐以红酒或加泰罗尼亚酒和咖啡。午睡或歇息的时间，在早饭与正餐之间。正餐时间，全家在三点，人们在两点。正餐与早饭相差不大，只是水果少些、肉多些，饭后照例吃点蜜饯作甜点。同早饭一样，正餐以咖啡收尾。各式蜜饯，此岛蕴藏甚丰：杏仁、番石榴、可可、刺果番荔枝、橙子、来檬，还有马米苹果，品类繁多。正餐之后、天黑之前，是驾车远游的时辰；家眷住在园上的时候，也是走访邻里的时候。没有第三餐；但夜里供应咖啡，有时供应茶。通常十点钟便早早就寝，因为一天开始得早，主要的户外劳作和活跃的消遣都得赶在早饭之前。

除宅子、黑人住区和糖坊之外，还有一排石头房子，尽头是厨房，住着轮机师、总管（*mayoral*）、牛倌（*boyero*）和管家，有一个老黑人妇女替他们做饭，另一个伺候他们。另有第二排石屋，包括库房、监狱、医院和产房。监狱我已描写过。医院和产房空气流通、通风良好，各得其用。今天两处都没有人住。建筑群的中央立着一个高架，挂着种植园的大钟。它早晨唤黑人起床，夜里唤他们归屋，还报吃饭的钟点。有特殊事情时它把众人召齐，蔗田失火或别的需要求援的时候，它便长敲大响，向邻园报警。

===Original===
After dinner, today, a volante, with two horses, and a postilion in bright jacket and buckled boots and large silver spurs, the harness well-besprinkled with silver, drove to the door, and an elderly gentleman alighted and came to the house, attired with scrupulous nicety of white cravat and dress coat, and with the manners of the ancien régime. This is M. Bourgeoise, the owner of the neighboring large plantation, Santa Catalina, one of the few cafetals remaining in this part of the island. He is too old, and too much attached to his plantation, to change it to a sugar estate; and he is too rich to need the change. He, too, was a refugee from the insurrection of St. Domingo, but older than M. C﻿—﻿—. Not being able to escape, he was compelled to serve as aid decamp to Jacques Dessalines. He has a good deal to say about the insurrection and its results, of a great part of which he was an eye witness. The sight of him brought vividly to mind the high career and sad fate of the just and brave Toussaint L’Ouverture, and the brilliant successes, and fickle, cruel rule, of Dessalines﻿—when French marshals were outmanoeuvred by Negro generals, and pitched battles were won by Negroes and mulattoes against European armies.

This gentleman had driven over in the hope of seeing his friend and neighbor, Mr. C﻿—﻿— the elder. He remained with us for some time, sitting under the veranda, the silvered volante and its black horses and black postilion standing under the trees. He invited us to visit his plantation, which I was desirous to do, as a cafetal is a rarity now.

===Chinese===
今天正餐之后，一辆两马车的沃兰特轻便马车驶到门前，骑马的仆役穿着鲜亮的短上衣、带扣的长靴、硕大的银马刺，马具上缀满了银饰；一位上了年纪的绅士下车走进屋来，白领结、礼服大衣，穿戴得一丝不苟，举止一派旧制度的气度。这位是布尔茹瓦先生（M. Bourgeoise），邻近大种植园圣卡塔利娜（Santa Catalina）的主人——那是本岛这一带残存无几的咖啡园之一。他年纪太大，又太眷恋自己的园子，不肯改成糖园；他也太富有，无须作此改变。他也是圣多明各大暴动的难民，比C——先生年长。当年未能脱身，被迫给雅克·德萨林（Jacques Dessalines）当过副官。关于那场暴动及其后果，他有说不完的话——其中很大一部分，他是亲眼目睹的。看到他，便不由得生动想起正义而勇敢的图森·路维杜尔（Toussaint L'Ouverture）的辉煌一生与悲惨结局，想起德萨林夺目的胜利和他反复无常、残暴的统治——那时法兰西的元帅们被黑人将领们在谋略上打得晕头转向，黑人和黑白混血者在堂堂之阵中战胜了欧洲的军队。

这位绅士乘车过来，是盼着见见他的朋友兼邻居C——老先生。他在我们这里坐了些时，坐在游廊下，那缀银的沃兰特马车、黑马和黑仆役停候在树下。他邀我们去他的种植园做客，我正巴不得去，如今咖啡园已是稀罕物了。

===Original===
My third day at La Ariadne, is much like the preceding days: the early rising, the coffee and fruit, the walk, visits to the mill, the fields, the garden, and the quarters, breakfast, rest indoors with reading and writing, dinner, out of doors again, and the evening under the veranda, with conversations on subjects now so interesting to me. These conversations, and what I had learned from other persons, open to me new causes for interest and sympathy with my younger host. Born in South Carolina, he has secured his rights of birth, and is a citizen of the United States, though all his pecuniary interests and family affections are in Cuba. He went to Paris at the age of nine, and remained there until he was nineteen, devoting the ten years to thorough courses of study in the best schools. He has spent much time in Boston, and has been at sea, to China, India, and the Pacific and California﻿—was wrecked in the Boston ship Mary Ellen, on a coral reef in the India seas, taken captive, restored, and brought back to Boston in another ship, whence he sailed for California. There he had a long and checkered experience, was wounded in the battle with the Indians who killed Lieut. Dale and defeated his party, was engaged in scientific surveys, topographical and geological, took the fever of the South Coast at a remote place, was reported dead, and came to his mother’s door, at the spot where we are talking this evening, so weak and sunken that his brothers did not know him, thinking it happiness enough if he could reach his home, to die in his mother’s arms. But home and its cherishings, and revived moral force, restored him, and now, active and strong again, when, in consequence of the marriage of his brothers and sisters, and the departure of neighbors, the family leave their home of thirty-five years for the city, he becomes the acting master, the *administrador* of the estate, and makes the old house his bachelor’s hall.

An education in Europe or the United States must tend to free the youth of Cuba from the besetting fault of untravelled plantation masters. They are in no danger of thinking their plantations and Cuba the world, or any great part of it. In such cases, I should think the danger might be rather the other way﻿—rather that of disgust and discouragement at the narrowness of the field, the entire want of a career set before them﻿—a career of any kind, literary, scientific, political, or military. The choice is between expatriation, and contentment in the position of a secluded cultivator of sugar by slave labor, with occasional opportunities of intercourse with the world and of foreign travel, with no other field than the limits of the plantation afford, for the exercise of the scientific knowledge, so laboriously acquired, and with no more exciting motive for the continuance of intellectual culture than the general sense of its worth and fitness.

===Chinese===
我在拉·阿里阿德涅的第三天，与前两天大同小异：早早起身，咖啡与水果，散一回步，看看磨坊、田野、花园和住区，用早饭，在屋里读读写写歇一歇，用正餐，再到户外去，傍晚坐在游廊下，谈些如今我最感兴趣的话题。这些交谈，加上我从旁人那里了解到的种种，为我这位年轻东家引出了新的趣味与同情。他生在南卡罗来纳，保住了出生的权利，是合众国公民，尽管他全部的金钱利益与亲眷之情都在古巴。他九岁去巴黎，一直住到十九岁，十个年头都用在最好学校里系统的学业上。他在波士顿住过很久，也下过海，到过中国、印度、太平洋和加利福尼亚——曾随波士顿船「玛丽·埃伦号」（Mary Ellen）在印度洋的珊瑚礁上失事，被俘过，又获归还，由另一条船带回波士顿，再从那里扬帆去加利福尼亚。在那里他有过一段漫长而坎坷的经历：在同杀死戴尔中尉、击溃其队伍的印第安人作战时负了伤；参加过地形与地质的科学勘测；在南岸一个偏僻地方染上热病，一度被报了死讯，然后走到他母亲的门前——就在今晚我们谈话的这个地方——形容枯槁、衰弱不堪，兄弟们都认不出他来，只想着他若能到家、能死在母亲怀里，就算是万幸了。然而家和家的呵护，加上重振的元气，把他救了回来；如今他又活跃又强健，赶上兄弟姐妹相继成婚、邻里陆续迁走，全家离开了居住三十五年的家园搬进城去，他便成了实际的主人、庄园的管理员（*administrador*），把这老宅改成了他的单身居所。

在欧罗巴或合众国受的教育，必能帮助古巴青年摆脱足不出户的种植园主们易犯的通病。他们绝不至于把自己的种植园和古巴当成全世界，或当成世界的一大部分。在这种人身上，我想危险恐怕倒在另一头——倒是对天地之狭窄生出的厌烦与沮丧，是摆在眼前的前程的全然阙如——无论哪种前程，文学的、科学的、政治的、还是军事的，一概没有。要做的抉择无非是：或离乡去国；或安于做一个与世隔绝的、用奴隶劳力种糖的人，偶尔有机会同外界往来、出国游历，除了种植园的疆界之外再无别处，可以施展辛辛苦苦求来的科学知识；而维系智识修养的动力，除了对它价值与体面的一般信念之外，也再没有更叫人兴奋的了。

## XIV / 十四

===Original===
Life of a planter and his family﻿—Coffee estate of St. Catalina﻿—Afternoon ride﻿—Departure from La Ariadne﻿—Return to Matanzas.

If the master of a plantation is faithful and thorough, will tolerate no misconduct or imposition, and yet is humane and watchful over the interests and rights, as well as over the duties of the Negroes, he has a hard and anxious life. Sickness to be ministered to, the feigning of sickness to be counteracted, rights of the slaves to be secured against other Negroes, as well as against whites, with a poor chance of getting at the truth from either; the obligations of the Negro quasi marriage to be enforced against all the sensual and childish tendencies of the race; theft and violence and wanderings from home to be detected and prevented; the work to be done, and yet no one to be overworked; and all this often with no effectual aid, often with only obstructions, from the intermediate whites! Nor is it his own people only that are to be looked to. The thieving and violence of Negroes from other plantations, their visits by night against law, and the encroachments of the neighboring free blacks and low whites, are all to be watched and prevented or punished. The master is a policeman, as well as an economist and a judge. His revolver and rifle are always loaded. He has his dogs, his trackers and seizers, that lie at his gate, trained to give the alarm when a strange step comes near the house or the quarters, and ready to pursue. His hedges may be broken down, his cane trampled or cut, or, still worse, set fire to, goats let into his pastures, his poultry stolen, and sometimes his dogs poisoned. It is a country of little law and order, and what with slavery and free Negroes and low whites, violence or fraud are imminent and always formidable. No man rides far unarmed. The Negroes are held under the subjection of force. A quarterdeck organization is established. The master owns vessel and cargo, and is captain of the ship and he and his family live in the cabin and hold the quarterdeck. There are no other commissioned officers on board, and no guard of marines. There are a few petty officers, and under all, a great crew of Negroes, for every kind of work, held by compulsion﻿—the results of a press-gang. All are at sea together. There are some laws, and civil authorities for the protection of each, but not very near, nor always accessible.

===Chinese===
种植园主一家的生活——圣卡塔利娜咖啡园——午后骑行——辞别拉·阿里阿德涅——返回马坦萨斯。

倘若种植园的主人尽责而彻底，既不容忍恶行与欺蒙，又对黑人的利益与权利、如同对其义务一样殷勤留心，那他的日子便是辛苦而忧心的。疾病要照护，装病要识破；奴隶的权利要保障，防的既是别的黑人，也是白人，而想从哪一方问出真情都难乎其难；黑人准婚姻的义务，要顶住这个种族种种耽于声色、孩子气的倾向强制维持；偷窃、暴力、擅离家门，要侦破、要防范；活计要完成，又不能让任何人劳累过度；而这一切，常常得不到中层白人得力的臂助，反倒处处掣肘！何况要看管的还不止自己的人。别园黑人的偷盗与强横、他们夜间违法的串访、邻近自由黑人和下等白人的蚕食，都得监视、防范或惩治。主人既是经济师和法官，又是警察。他的左轮枪和步枪永远上着膛。他养着狗——他的追踪者与擒拿手——卧在大门口，训练得一有生人脚步靠近宅子或住区便吠起报警，随时准备追击。他的篱笆可能被拆毁，他的甘蔗可能被踩坏、被偷砍，或者更糟，被放火烧掉；山羊可能被放进他的牧场，家禽可能被盗，有时连狗也会被毒死。这是一个几乎没有法律与秩序的乡野，加上奴隶制、自由黑人和下等白人，暴力和欺诈随时可能发生，永远叫人不敢掉以轻心。没有人出远门不带武器。黑人被置于强力的统制之下。这里实行的是一种后甲板体制。主人拥有船和货，兼任船长，他同家眷住在舱房里、占着后甲板。船上没有别的军官，也没有陆战队护卫。有几名小头目，底下便是一大群黑人水手，什么活都干，全凭强制——那是强征队拉来的结果。所有人都同在海上。也有一些法律和民政当局保护各方，只是都不太近，也并非随时够得着。

===Original===
After dinner today, we take saddle horses for a ride to Santa Catalina. Necessary duties in the field and mill delay us, and we are in danger of not being able to visit the house, as my friend must be back in season for the close of work and the distribution of provisions, in the absence of his *mayoral*. The horses have the famous “march,” as it is called, of the island, an easy rapid step, something like pacing, and delightful for a quiet ride under a soft afternoon sky, among flowers and sweet odors. I have seen but few trotting horses in Cuba.

The afternoon is serene. Near, the birds are flying, or chattering with extreme sociability in close trees, and the thickets are fragrant with flowers; while far off, the high hills loom in the horizon; and all about us is this tropical growth, with which I cannot yet become familiar, of palms and cocoas and bananas. We amble over the red earth of the winding lanes, and turn into the broad avenue of Santa Catalina, with its double row of royal palms. We are in﻿—not a forest, for the trees are not thick and wild and large enough for that﻿—but in a huge, dense, tropical orchard. The avenue is as clear and straight and wide as a city mall; while all the ground on either side, for hundreds of acres, is a plantation of oranges and limes, bananas and plantains, cocoas and pineapples, and of cedar and mango, mignonette and allspice, under whose shade is growing the green-leaved, the evergreen-leaved coffee plant, with its little dark red berry, the tonic of half the world. Here we have a glimpse of the lost charm of Cuba. No wonder that the aged proprietor cannot find the heart to lay it waste for the monotonous cane field, and make the quiet, peaceful horticulture, the natural growth of fruit and berry, and the simple processes of gathering, drying, and storing, give place to the steam and smoke and drive and life consuming toil of the *ingenio*!

===Chinese===
今天正餐之后，我们骑上鞍马，前往圣卡塔利娜。田间和磨坊里必要的公务耽搁了我们，眼看要来不及登门了，因为我的朋友必须赶回去主持收工和分发口粮——他的总管（*mayoral*）不在。马走的是本岛有名的「行进步」（march），一种轻松而急促的步子，有点像溜蹄，在柔和的午后天空下、在花木芬芳之间安闲地骑乘，再惬意不过。在古巴，快步马我见得很少。

午后天色澄静。近处，鸟儿或翻飞，或躲在密树间极亲热地絮语，灌木丛里花香扑鼻；远处，高山在地平线上隐隐耸峙；四围尽是这我至今亲近不起来的热带草木——棕榈、可可、香蕉。我们踏着蜿蜒小径的红土缓辔而行，转入圣卡塔利娜宽阔的林荫道，两列王棕夹峙。我们进入的——不是森林，树木还不够密、不够野、不够大——而是一座巨大茂密的热带果园。林荫道像城里的林荫大道一样洁净、笔直、宽敞；两侧的土地，几百英亩几百英亩的，全是橘子和来檬、香蕉和大蕉、可可和菠萝，还有雪松与芒果、木樨草与多香果的种植园，树荫下生长着绿叶的——常绿叶的——咖啡树，结着小小的深红色果子，那是半个世界的提神剂。在这里，我们窥见了古巴逝去的魅力。难怪那位年迈的园主狠不下心，为了单调的蔗田把这园子毁掉，让这安静平和的园艺、果蔬的自然生长、采集晾晒贮藏的简单工序，让位给糖坊（*ingenio*）的蒸汽、浓烟、催逼和耗人生命的苦役！

===Original===
At a turn in the avenue, we come upon the proprietor, who is taking his evening walk, still in the exact dress and with the exact manners of urban life. With truly French politeness, he is distressed, and all but offended, that we cannot go to his house. It is my duty to insist on declining his invitation, for I know that C﻿—﻿— is anxious to return. At another turn, we come upon a group of little black children, under the charge of a decent, matronly mulatto, coming up a shaded footpath, which leads among the coffee. C﻿—﻿— stops to give a kind word to them.

But it is sunset, and we must turn about. We ride rather rapidly down the avenue, and along the highway, where we meet several travellers, nearly all with pistols in their holsters, and one of the mounted police, with carbine and sword; and then cross the brook pass through the little, mean hamlet of Limonar, whose inmates are about half blacks and half whites, but once a famed resort for invalids, and enter our own avenue, and thence to the house. On our way, we pass a burying ground, which my companion says he is ashamed to have me see. Its condition is bad enough. The planters are taxed for it, but the charge of it is with the padre, who takes big fees for burials, and lets it go to ruin. The bell has rung long ago, but the people are waiting our return, and the evening duties of distributing food, turning on the night gang for night work, and closing the gates, are performed.

===Chinese===
林荫道一拐弯，我们遇上了园主，他正在做傍晚的散步，衣着举止仍是一丝不苟的城市做派。凭着道地的法国式礼貌，他因为我们不能去他宅上而深感不安，几乎要见怪了。我不得不坚持婉谢他的邀请，因为我知道C——急着回去。又拐过一个弯，我们遇上一小群黑孩子，由一个体面的、主妇模样的黑白混血妇女领着，从一条荫凉的小径上走来，小径蜿蜒穿过咖啡树间。C——勒住马，对他们说了几句亲热的话。

可是日落了，我们必须折返。我们顺着林荫道、沿着公路相当快地骑行，路上遇见几拨行人，鞍袋里几乎都插着手枪，还有一名骑警，带着马枪和佩刀；随后涉过小溪，穿过利莫纳尔那寒碜的小村——村民约摸半黑半白，这里却曾是有名的病人疗养地——再进了自家的林荫道，径直到家。路上我们经过一处墓地，同伴说，让他被我看见这地方，他很惭愧。墓地的情形确实够糟。种植园主为它纳税，管理却握在教区神父手里，神父收着昂贵的下葬费，任凭园地朽坏。钟早已敲过了，人们正等着我们回来，晚间的那些事——分发口粮、派夜班上工、闩上大门——一一照办。

===Original===
Tonight the hounds have an alarm, and C﻿—﻿— is off in the darkness. In a few minutes he returns. There has been someone about, but nothing is discovered. A Negro may have attempted to steal out, or some strange Negro may be trying to steal in, or some prowling white, or free black, has been reconnoitering. These are the terms on which this system is carried on; and I think, too, that when the tramp of horses is heard after dark, and strange men ride to wards the piazza, it causes some uneasiness.

The morning of the fourth day, I take my leave, by the early train for Matanzas. The hour is half-past six; but the habits of rising are so early that it requires no special preparation. I have time for coffee, for a last visit to the sugar house, a goodbye to the engineer, who will be back on the banks of the Merrimack in May, and for a last look into the quarters, to gather the little group of kneelers for “*la bendicion*,” with their “*Buenos dias, Señor.*” My horse is ready, the Negro has gone with my luggage, and I must take my leave of my newly-made friend. Alone together, we have been more intimate in three days than we should have been in as many weeks in a full household. Adios!﻿—May the opening of a new home on the old spot, which I hear is awaiting you, be the harbinger of a more cheerful life, and the creation of such fresh ties and interests, that the delightful air of the hill country of Cuba, the dreamy monotony of the day, the serenity of nights which seem to bring the stars down to your roof or to raise you halfway to them, and the luxuriance and variety of vegetable and animal life, may not be the only satisfactions of existence here. A quiet amble over the red earth, to the station, in a thick morning mist, almost cold enough to make an overcoat comfortable; and, after two hours on the rail, I am again in Matanzas, among close-packed houses, and with views of blue ocean and of ships.

===Chinese===
今夜猎犬叫起警报，C——摸黑出去了。不几分钟便回来了。有人到过附近，但什么也没发现。也许是个黑人想偷跑出去，也许是个外来的黑人想偷溜进来，也许是哪个游荡的白人或自由黑人来踩过点。这套制度就是照这样的条件运转的；而且我想，天黑之后一听见马蹄声、看见生人朝游廊骑来，也总要叫人心里发紧。

第四天早晨，我搭早班火车向马坦萨斯告辞。时刻是六点半；但此地起居的习惯如此之早，无须什么特别的准备。来得及喝咖啡，来得及最后看一眼糖坊，同轮机师道个别——他五月里就该回到梅里马克河畔了——也来得及最后到住区看一眼，招聚起那一小群跪着求「*la bendicion*」（祝福）的孩子，听他们那句「*Buenos dias, Señor.*」（先生日安）。我的马备好了，黑人已提着行李先行，我须向这位新交的朋友道别了。我们两人单独相处，三天的交情，胜过人口兴旺的全家大宅里三个星期。再见了！——愿你在老地方那个我听说正等着你的新家之落成，预示一段更快乐的人生，愿它缔结出新的纽带与新的寄托，使古巴丘陵地带那怡人的空气、白日里梦一般的单调、仿佛把星辰引到你屋檐下或把你举到半空的静谧夜晚、动植物的繁盛与多样，不至成为此间人生仅有的满足。踏着红土，在浓重的晨雾中缓辔走向车站，雾冷得几乎可以让一件大衣穿得舒舒服服；火车坐了两个钟头，我又回到了马坦萨斯，回到密匝匝的屋宇之间，又望得见蓝色的海洋和船帆了。

## XIX / 十九

===Original===
Drive over the Paseo de Tacon﻿—Count de la Fernandina﻿—Cuban nobility﻿—Hospital of San Juan de Dios﻿—The Presidio and grand prison; its inmates, discipline, etc.

===Chinese===
驱车畅游塔孔大道——费尔南迪纳伯爵——古巴的贵族——圣胡安·德·迪奥斯医院——要塞监狱与大监狱；其囚犯、纪律等。

===Original===
Drove out over the Paseo de Tacon to the Cerro, a height, formerly a village, now a part of the suburbs of Havana. It is high ground, and commands a noble view of Havana and the sea. Coming in, I met the Bishop, who introduced me to the Count de La Fernandina, a dignified Spanish nobleman, who owns a beautiful villa on this Paseo, where we walked a while in the grounds. This house is very elegant and costly, with marble floors, high ceilings, piazzas, and a garden of the richest trees and flowers coming into the courtyard, and advancing even into the windows of the house. It is one of the most beautiful villas in the vicinity of Havana.

There are several noblemen who have their estates and titles in Cuba, but are recognized as nobles of Spain;﻿—in all, I should say, about fifty or sixty. Some of these have received their titles for civil or military services; but most of them have been raised to their rank on account of their wealth, or have purchased their titles outright. I believe there are but two grades, the marquis and the count. Among the titles best known to strangers are Villanueva, Fernandina, and O’Reilly. The number of Irish families who have taken rank in the Spanish service and become connected with Cuba, is rather remarkable. Beside O’Reilly, there are O’Don nel, O’Farrel, and O’Lawlor, descendants of Irishmen who entered the Spanish service after the battle of the Boyne.

===Chinese===
驱车出城，沿塔孔大道（Paseo de Tacon）前往塞罗（Cerro）。那是一处高地，从前是个村子，如今成了哈瓦那郊外的一部分。这里地势高敞，哈瓦那全城与大海尽收眼底，景色壮阔。归途中我遇见主教，他把我引见给费尔南迪纳伯爵（Count de La Fernandina）。伯爵是位气度庄重的西班牙贵族，在这条大道上拥有一座美丽的别墅，我们便在园中漫步了片刻。这座宅邸精美华贵，大理石的地面，高高的天花，环列的游廊，还有满园繁盛的花木，一直漫进庭院，甚至直抵宅子的窗前。这是哈瓦那近郊最美的别墅之一。

有几位贵族，领地和封号都在古巴，却被承认是西班牙的贵族；——总计起来，我估摸约有五六十位。其中有的因文官职守或军功而获封；但大多数是因家资豪富而跻身此列，或是干脆花钱把头衔买了下来。据我所知，贵族只有两等：侯爵与伯爵。外人最熟知的封号有比利亚努埃瓦（Villanueva）、费尔南迪纳（Fernandina）和奥赖利（O’Reilly）。在西班牙王家效力、又与古巴结缘的爱尔兰家族，数目相当可观。除奥赖利外，还有奥唐奈（O’Don nel）、奥法雷尔（O’Farrel）和奥劳勒（O’Lawlor），都是博因河之役（battle of the Boyne）后投效西班牙的爱尔兰人的后裔。

===Original===
Dr. Howe had seen the Presidio, the great prison of Havana, once; but was desirous to visit it again; so he joined me, under the conduct of our young friend, Señor —﻿—﻿—, to visit that and the hospital of San Juan de Dios. The hospital we saw first. It is supported by the government﻿—that is to say, by Cuban revenues﻿—for charity patients chiefly, but some, who can afford it, pay more or less. There are about two hundred and fifty patients. This, again, is in the charge of the Sisters of Charity. As we came upon one of the Sisters, in a passageway, in her white cap and cape, and black and blue dress, Dr. Howe said, “I always take off my hat to a Sister of Charity,” and we paid them all that attention, whenever we passed them. Dr. Howe examined the book of prescriptions, and said that there was less drugging than he supposed there would be. The attending physician told us that nearly all the physicians had studied in Paris, or in Philadelphia. There were a great many medical students in attendance, and there had just been an operation in the theatre. In an open yard we saw two men washing a dead body, and carelessly laying it on a table, for dissection. I am told that the medical and surgical professions are in a very satisfactory state of advancement in the island, and that a degree in medicine, and a license to practise, carry with them proofs of considerable proficiency. It is always observable that the physical and the exact sciences are the last to suffer under despotisms.

===Chinese===
豪博士（Dr. Howe）从前去过一次哈瓦那的大监狱——要塞监狱（Presidio），但还想再去看一趟，于是由我们那位年轻的朋友———先生（Señor ————）带领，与我同去参观监狱和圣胡安·德·迪奥斯医院（San Juan de Dios）。我们先看的是医院。它由政府供养——也就是说，由古巴的税收供养——收治的主要是慈善病人，不过也有些人付得起钱，多少缴纳一些。院里约有二百五十名病人，这里同样由慈善修女会（Sisters of Charity）照管。我们在过道里迎面遇见一位修女，白帽白披肩，穿着黑蓝两色的会衣，豪博士说：“见了慈善会的修女，我总要脱帽致敬。”此后每逢修女经过，我们都一一脱帽为礼。豪博士查看了处方簿，说用药比他预想的要少。主治医师告诉我们，几乎所有的医生都在巴黎或费城念过书。在场的医学生很多，手术示教室里刚做完一台手术。在一个露天的院子里，我们看见两个男人在冲洗一具尸体，又漫不经心地把它抬上桌子，供解剖之用。听说岛上的内外科医术均精进可观，令人满意；要取得医学学位和行医执照，都须证明确有相当的造诣。在专制制度下，自然科学与精密科学总是最后受害——这向来是引人注目的事实。

===Original===
The Presidio and Grand Cárcel of Havana is a large building, of yellow stone, standing near the fort of the Punta, and is one of the striking objects as you enter the harbor. It has no appearance of a jail without, but rather of a palace or court; but within, it is full of live men’s bones and of all uncleanness. No man, whose notions are derived from an American or English penitentiary of the last twenty years, or fifty years, can form an idea of the great Cuban prison. It is simply horrible. There are no cells, except for solitary confinement of “incomunicados,”﻿—who are usually political offenders. The prisoners are placed in large rooms, with stone floors and grated windows, where they are left, from twenty to fifty in each, without work, without books, without interference or intervention of anyone, day and night, day and night, for the weeks, months or years of their sentences. The sights are dreadful. In this hot climate, so many beings, with no provision for ventilation but the grated windows﻿—so unclean, and most of them naked above the waist﻿—all spend their time in walking, talking, playing, and smoking; and, at night, without bed or blanket, they lie down on the stone floor, on what clothes they may have, to sleep if they can. The whole prison, with the exception of the few cells for the “incomunicados,” was a series of these great cages, in which human beings were shut up. Incarceration is the beginning, middle and end of the whole system. Reformation, improvement, benefit to soul or body, are not thought of. We inquired carefully, both of the officer who was sent to attend us, and of a *capitan de partido*, who was there, and were positively assured that the only distinction among the prisoners was determined by the money they paid. Those who can pay nothing, are left to the worst. Those who can pay two reals (twenty-five cents) a day, are placed in wards a little higher and better. Those who can pay six reals (seventy-five cents) a day, have better places still, called the “Salas de distincion,” and some privileges of walking in the galleries. The amount of money, and not the degree of criminality, determines the character of the punishment. There seems to be no limit to the right of the prisoners to talk with any whom they can get to hear them, at whatever distance, and to converse with visitors, and to receive money from them. In fact, the whole scene was a Babel. All that was insured was that they should not escape. When I say that no work was done, I should make the qualification that a few prisoners were employed in rolling tobacco into cigars, for a contractor; but they were very few. Among the prisoners was a *capitan de partido* (a local magistrate), who was committed on a charge of conniving at the slave trade. He could pay his six reals, of course; and had the privileges of a “Sala de distincion” and of the galleries. He walked about with us, cigar in mouth, and talked freely, and gave us much information respecting the prison. My last request was to see the *garrotte*; but it was refused me.

===Chinese===
哈瓦那的要塞监狱与大监狱（Presidio and Grand Cárcel）是一座黄色石料建成的大厦，坐落在蓬塔要塞（fort of the Punta）近旁，是进港时最惹眼的建筑之一。它外表全无监狱的样子，倒像一座宫殿或官署；可是里面，却满是活人的骸骨和种种污秽。凡是从近二十年乃至近五十年的英美感化院中得来监狱概念的人，都无法想象这座古巴大监狱。它简直就是骇人。狱中没有牢房，只有用来单独禁闭“incomunicados”（禁绝交往者）的暗室——这些人通常是政治犯。囚犯们被关进大间里，石头的地面，装着铁栅的窗户，每间塞进二十到五十人，没有活计，没有书本，无人照管也无人干预，日复一日、夜复一夜地熬过刑期中的几个星期、几个月或几年。景象骇人听闻。在这样炎热的气候里，这么多的人挤在一起，除了铁栅窗外再无通风设施——浑身污垢，大半上身赤裸——全靠踱步、闲谈、嬉戏和抽烟打发时光；到了夜里，没有床铺，没有毯子，便躺倒在石地上，垫着自己仅有的几件衣裳，能睡着便睡。整座监狱，除了几间关押“incomunicados”的暗室外，就是一连串这样的大笼子，活人被锁在里面。监禁，就是这套制度的开头、中间和全部；改过迁善、教化改良、于灵魂或身体有何益处，一概无人考虑。我们仔细询问了奉命陪同的军官，也问了在场的*capitan de partido*（地方治安官），得到确凿的答复：囚犯之间唯一的等差，全由他们出钱多少而定。一个钱也付不出的，就落入最坏的境地。付得起每天两雷亚尔（real，合二十五美分）的，住进稍稍高爽洁净些的病室。付得起每天六雷亚尔（合七十五美分）的，住处更好，称作“Salas de distincion”（优待室），还有在回廊里走动的特权。决定刑罚轻重的，是钱的数目，而不是罪行的轻重。囚犯似乎享有毫无限制的权利，隔着任意远的距离同任何听得见他们的人说话，同来探视的人交谈，收受他们带来的钱财。实际上，整个场面就是一座巴别塔（Babel）。唯一被确保的，是他们逃不出去。我说狱中无活可做时，还应补一句：有少数囚犯受雇于一名承包商，卷制雪茄；但人数寥寥。囚犯中有一位*capitan de partido*（地方治安官），因涉嫌纵容奴隶贸易而被收押。他自然付得出每天六雷亚尔，享有“优待室”和回廊的特权。他嘴里叼着雪茄，陪我们四处走动，谈吐随便，向我们讲了不少有关这座监狱的情形。我最后请求看一看*garrotte*（绞刑具），遭到了拒绝。

===Original===
It was beginning to grow dark before we got to the gate, which was duly opened to us, and we passed out, with a good will, into the open air. Dr. Howe said he was nowise reluctant to be outside. It seemed to bring back to his mind his Prussian prison, a little too forcibly to be agreeable. He felt as if he were in keeping again, and was thinking how he should feel if, just as we got to the gate, an officer were to bow and say, “Dr. Howe?” “Yes, sir.” “You may remain here. There is a charge against you of seditious language, since you have been in the island.” No man would meet such a danger more calmly, and say less about it, than he, if he thought duty to his fellow beings called him to it.

The open air, the chainless ocean, and the ships freely coming and going, were a pleasant change to the eye, even of one who had never suffered bonds for conscience’ sake. It seemed strange to see that all persons outside were doing as they pleased.

===Chinese===
我们走到大门口时，天色已经暗下来了。大门照章为我们开启，我们心甘情愿地跨出去，回到露天里。豪博士说，置身狱外，他绝无半点惋惜。这地方似乎把他记忆里的普鲁士监狱又带了回来，那股力量未免太猛，叫人不快。他觉得自己仿佛又被看押了起来，心里正盘算着：倘若我们刚走到门口，就有军官一躬身，说道：“豪博士？”“是的，先生。”“那就请您留步。您入境以来，有发表煽动性言论之嫌。”那时自己会作何感想。然而，倘若他认为对同胞的责任如此召唤他，再没有人能比他更沉着地面对这种危险，也更少把它挂在嘴边了。

露天的空气，不系锁链的大海，自由进出往来的船只——即便对一个从未为良心而身陷缧绁的人，眼前这一变换也令人愉快。看到狱外的人个个各行其是，反倒叫人觉得奇异了。

## XV / 十五

===Original===
Matanzas﻿—The Cumbre﻿—The Yumurí﻿—The family of Mr. C﻿—﻿—﻿—Ensor’s.

Instead of the posada by the waterside, I take up my quarters at a hotel kept by Ensor, an American, and his sister. Here the hours, cooking, and chief arrangements are in the fashion of the country, as they should be, but there is more of that attention to guests which we are accustomed to at home, than the Cuban hotels usually give.

The objects to be visited here are the Cumbre and the valley of the Yumurí. It is too late for a morning ride, and I put off my visit until afternoon. Gazzaniga and some of the opera troupe are here; and several Americans at the hotel, who were at the opera last night, tell me that the people of Matanzas made a handsome show, and are of opinion that there was more beauty in the boxes than we saw at the Villanueva. It appears, too, that at the *Retreta*, in the Plaza de Armas, when the band plays, and at evening promenades, the ladies walk about, and do not keep to their carriages as in Havana.

===Chinese===
马坦萨斯——昆布雷山——尤穆里河谷——C——先生一家——恩索尔旅馆。

我没有住水边的那家客栈，改在恩索尔（Ensor）和他妹妹开的一家旅馆下榻。这里的作息、烹饪和主要安排都是本地的路数——本该如此——但对客人的那种殷勤，比古巴旅馆通常给的要多，是我们国内惯见的那一种。

此地要游览的去处是昆布雷山和尤穆里河谷。上午出游已来不及，我把行程推到下午。加扎尼加（Gazzaniga）和歌剧团的一些人正在这里；旅馆里几位昨晚去看过歌剧的美国人对我说，马坦萨斯人士衣香鬓影、颇见气象，并且认为包厢里的美人比我们在比利亚努埃瓦剧院（Villanueva）看到的还要多。据说在武器广场（Plaza de Armas）的「夜游」（*Retreta*）——乐队奏乐的时分——以及傍晚散步的时候，女士们是下地步行的，不像在哈瓦那那样只守在马车里。

===Original===
As soon as the sun began to decline, I set off for the Cumbre, mounted on a pacer, with a Negro for a guide, who rode, as I soon discovered, a better nag than mine. We cross the stone bridges, and pass the great hospital, which dominates over the town. A regiment, dressed in seersucker and straw hats, is drilling, by trumpet call, and drilling well, too, on the green in front of the barracks; while we take our winding way up the ascent of the Cumbre.

The bay, town, and shipping lie beneath us; the Pan rises in the distance to the height of some 3,000 feet; the ocean is before us, rolling against the outside base of the hills; and, on the inside, lies the deep, rich, peaceful valley of the Yumurí. On the top of the Cumbre, commanding the noblest view of ocean and valley, bay and town, is the *ingenio* of a Mr. Jenkes, a merchant, bearing a name that would put Spanish tongues to their trumps to sound, were it not that they probably take refuge in the Don Guillermo, or Don Enrique of his Christian name. The estate bears the name of La Victoria, and is kindly thrown open to visitors from the city. It is said to be a model establishment. The house is large, in a classic style, and costly, and the Negro quarters, the storehouses, mechanic shops, and sugar house are of dimensions indicating an estate of the first class.

On the way up from the city, several fine points of sight were occupied by villas, all of one story, usually in the Roman or Grecian style, surrounded by gardens and shade trees, and with every appearance of taste and wealth.

===Chinese===
太阳刚一偏西，我便动身前往昆布雷山，骑一匹走溜蹄马，由一个黑人当向导——我很快就发现，他胯下那匹比我这匹强。我们跨过石桥，经过俯瞰全城的大医院。一个团穿着皱纱军装、戴着草帽，正在兵营前的草地上听号令操练，而且操练得还不错；我们则取蜿蜒小道，登上昆布雷山的上坡路。

海湾、城镇和船只都在脚下；潘山（Pan）在远处耸起，高约三千英尺；海洋在面前，波涛拍打着山冈外侧的山脚；而内侧，便是深邃、丰饶、安宁的尤穆里河谷。昆布雷山顶上，雄踞着海洋与河谷、海湾与城镇最壮丽的景致，是一位詹克斯先生（Mr. Jenkes）——一个商人——的糖坊（*ingenio*）；他那个姓氏，倘若西班牙人的舌头不想办法躲进他教名里的「唐·纪廉」（Don Guillermo）或「唐·恩里克」（Don Enrique），真要把它们难为到极处。庄园名叫拉·维多利亚（La Victoria），好意对城里来的游人敞开。据说是一座模范庄园。宅子宏大，古典式样，造价不菲；黑人住区、库房、工匠作坊和糖坊的规模，都显出第一流庄园的气派。

从城里上山的路上，几处绝佳的观景点都让别墅占了，全是单层，多为罗马式或希腊式，围有花园和荫凉树，处处透出趣味与财富的气象。

===Original===
It is late, but I must not miss the Yumurí; so we dive down the short, steep descent, and cross dry brooks and wet brooks, and over stones, and along bridle paths, and over fields without paths, and by wretched hovels, and a few decent cottages, with yelping dogs and cackling hens and staring children, and between high, overhanging cliffs, and along the side of a still lake, and after it is so dark that we can hardly see stones or paths, we strike a bridle path, and then come out upon the road, and, in a few minutes more, are among the gaslights and noises of the city.

At the hotel, there is a New York company who have spent the day at the Yumurí, and describe a cave not yet fully explored, which is visited by all who have time﻿—abounding in stalactites, and, though much smaller, reminding one of the Mammoth Cave of Kentucky.

I cannot leave Matanzas without paying my respects to the family to whose kindness I owe so much. Mr. C﻿—﻿— lives in a part of the suburbs called Versailles, near the barracks, in a large and handsome house, built after the style of the country. There I spend an agreeable evening, at a gathering of nearly all the family, sons and daughters, and the sons-in-law and daughters-in-law. There is something strangely cosmopolitan in many of the Cuban families﻿—as in this, where are found French origin, Spanish and American intermarriage, education in Europe or the United States, home and property in Cuba, friendships and sympathies and half a residence in Boston or New York or Charleston, and three languages at command.

Here I learn that the Thirty Millions Bill has not passed, and, by the latest dates, is not likely to pass.

My room at Ensor’s is on a level with the courtyard, and a horse puts his face into the grating as I am dressing, and I know of nothing to prevent his walking in at the door, if he chooses, so that the Negro may finish rubbing him down by my looking glass. Yet the house is neatly furnished and cared for, and its keepers are attentive and deserving people.

===Chinese===
天色已晚，但尤穆里河谷不可错过；于是我们冲下又短又陡的坡，蹚过干涸的小溪和有水的小溪，越过石头，沿马道走，穿过没有路的田野，路过破烂的窝棚和几座体面的小屋，一路是狂吠的狗、咯咯叫的母鸡和瞪眼的孩子，穿行于高峻压顶的峭壁之间，沿着一片静水湖的岸边，直到天黑得几乎看不清石头和路径，才踏上一条马道，随后上了大路，再过几分钟，就置身于城市的煤气灯和人声之中了。

旅馆里有一伙纽约人，白天刚游罢尤穆里回来，说起一个尚未探明的岩洞，凡有工夫的人都要去一看——洞里钟乳石丛生，规模虽小得多，却叫人想起肯塔基的猛犸洞。

我不能不对那一家人致敬告别——我受他们的恩惠太多了。C——先生住在近兵营的一片叫凡尔赛（Versailles）的郊外，宅子宽大漂亮，是本地的式样。我在那里度过了一个愉快的夜晚，全家几乎聚齐了，儿子女儿、女婿儿媳济济一堂。许多古巴家庭都有一种奇特的四海为家的气质——这一家便是：法兰西的血统，西班牙人与美国人的联姻，在欧洲或合众国受的教育，家与产业在古巴，友谊与情感、外加半个居所在波士顿、纽约或查尔斯顿，还能自如运用三种语言。

在这里我得知，「三千万法案」没有通过，而且照最新的消息看，多半也通不过。

我在恩索尔旅馆的房间同院子齐平，更衣时一匹马把脸探进铁栅栏，我看不出有什么能拦住它从门口径直走进来——要是它乐意的话——好让那黑人凑着我的穿衣镜把它擦抹完。不过屋子布置得整洁、打理得尽心，店主夫妇也是殷勤可靠的人。

## XVI / 十六

===Original===
Railroad from Matanzas to Havana﻿—Stations, views of interior, from railroad train﻿—Short sketch of the position of Cuba; its productions, resources, civil and political rights, religion, professions, sciences, and literature﻿—Return to Havana.

*Saturday, February 28*.﻿—At eight o’clock this morning, I take my leave of Matanzas, by the railroad for Havana.

Although the distance to Havana, as the bird flies, is only sixty miles, the railroad, winding into the interior, to draw out the sugar freights, makes a line of nearly one hundred miles. This adds to the length of our journey, but also greatly to its interest.

In the cars are two Americans, who have also been visiting plantations. They give me the following statistics of a sugar plantation, which they think may be relied upon.

Lands, machinery, 320 slaves, and 20 Coolies, worth $500,000. Produce this year, 4,000 boxes of sugar and 800 casks of molasses, worth $104,000. Expenses, $35,000. Net, $69,000, or about 14 percent. This is not a large interest on an investment so much of which is perishable and subject to deterioration.

===Chinese===
马坦萨斯至哈瓦那的铁路——车站与车窗中的内陆风光——古巴境况简述：物产、资源、公民权利与政治权利、宗教、职业、科学与文学——回到哈瓦那。

*二月二十八日，星期六*。——今晨八点，我乘火车辞别马坦萨斯，前往哈瓦那。

到哈瓦那的直线距离虽只有六十英里，铁路为了深入内陆揽运食糖，弯弯曲曲修成了将近一百英里。这让旅程变长了，趣味也大增。

车厢里有两个美国人，也是刚参观过种植园的。他们向我提供了下面这份糖业庄园的统计数字，他们认为可以信赖。

土地、机器、三百二十名奴隶和二十名苦力，值五十万元。今年出产四千箱糖、八百桶糖蜜，值十万零四千元。开销三万五千元。净得六万九千元，约合百分之十四的利。对一笔这么大份额易毁损、易折旧的投资来说，这算不上丰厚的收益。

===Original===
The day, as has been every day of mine in Cuba, is fair and beautiful. The heat is great, perhaps even dangerous to a Northerner, should he be exposed to it in active exercise, at noon﻿—but, with the shade and motion of the cars, not disagreeable, for the air is pure and elastic, and it is only the direct heat of the sun that is oppressive. I think one notices the results of this pure air, in the throats and nasal organs of the people. One seldom meets a person that seems to have a cold in the head or the throat; and pocket handkerchiefs are used chiefly for ornament.

I cannot weary of gazing upon these new and strange scenes; the stations, with the groups of peasants and Negroes and fruit sellers that gather about them, and the stores of sugar and molasses collected there; the *ingenio*s, glimmering in the heat of the sun, with their tall, furnace chimneys; the cane fields, acres upon acres; the slow oxcarts carrying the cane to the mill; then the intervals of unused country, the jungles, adorned with little wild flowers, the groves of the weeping, drooping, sad, homesick cocoa; the royal palm, which is to trees what the camel or dromedary is among animals﻿—seeming to have strayed from Nubia or Mesopotamia; the stiff, close orange tree, with its golden balls of fruit; and then the remains of a cafetal, the coffee plan growing untrimmed and wild under the reprieved groves of plantain and banana. How can this tire an eye that two weeks ago today rested on the midwinter snow and mud of the close streets of lower New York?

===Chinese===
这一天同我在古巴的每一天一样，晴朗明媚。天气很热，对北方人来说，正午若在剧烈活动中暴晒，兴许还有危险——但在车厢的荫凉与行进之中，并不难受，因为空气纯净而有弹性，逼人的只是太阳的直射。我想，这种纯净空气的效力，是能在人们的喉咙和鼻腔上看出来的：难得遇见一个似乎伤风鼻塞或嗓子发炎的人；手帕主要是当装饰用的。

这些新奇的景象我看不厌：车站，以及围聚在车站四周的农夫、黑人和水果贩子，堆存在那里的糖与糖蜜；烈日下熠熠生辉的糖坊（*ingenio*）和它们高高的炉烟囱；一英亩连一英亩的蔗田；把甘蔗缓缓运往磨坊的牛车；再就是闲置乡野的间隔地带，点缀着小小野花的丛林，一丛丛垂泪低首、凄然思乡的可可树；王棕——它在树木中间的地位，好比骆驼之于动物——仿佛是从努比亚或美索不达米亚走失到此；僵硬齐整的橘树，挂着金球般的果实；还有咖啡园的残迹，咖啡树在暂获缓刑的大蕉香蕉林下，无人修剪，疯长成野。距这只眼睛上望见纽约下城街头隆冬的冰雪泥泞，今天才不过两周，这样的景色，它怎么会厌倦呢？

===Original===
It is certainly true that there is such a thing as industry in the tropics. The labor of the tropics goes on. Notwithstanding all we hear and know of the enervating influence of the climate, the white man, if not laborious himself, is the cause that labor is in others. With all its social and political discouragements, with the disadvantages of a duty of about twenty-five percent on its sugars laid in the United States, and a duty of full one hundred percent on all flour imported from the United States, and after paying heavier taxes than any people on earth pay at this moment, and yielding a revenue, which nets, after every deduction and discount, not less than sixteen millions a year;﻿—against all these disadvantages, this island is still very productive and very rich. There is, to be sure, little variety in its industry. In the country, it is nothing but the raising and making of sugar; and in the towns, it is the selling and exporting of sugar. With the addition of a little coffee and copper, more tobacco, and some fresh fruit and preserves, and the commerce which they stimulate, and the mechanic and trading necessities of the towns, we have the sum of its industry and resources. Science, arts, letters, arms, manufactures, and the learning and discussions of politics, of theology, and of the great problems and opinions that move the minds of the thinking world﻿—in these, the people of Cuba have no part. These move by them, as the great Gulf Stream drifts by their shores. Nor is there, nor has there been in Cuba, in the memory of the young and middle-aged, debate, or vote, or juries, or one of the least and most rudimental processes of self-government. The African and Chinese do the manual labor, the Cubans hold the land and the capital, and direct the agricultural industry, the commerce is shared between the Cubans, and foreigners of all nations; and the government, civil and military, is exercised by the citizens of Old Spain. No Cuban votes, or attends a lawful political meeting, or sits on a jury, or sees a lawmaking assembly, except as a curiosity abroad, even in a municipality; nor has he ever helped to make, or interpret, or administer laws, or borne arms, except by special license of government granted to such as are friends of government. In religion, he has no choice, except between the Roman Catholic and none. The laws that govern him are made abroad, and administered by a central power, a foreign Captain-General, through the agency of foreign civil and military officers. The Cuban has no public career. If he removes to Old Spain, and is known as a supporter of Spanish royal power, his Creole birth is probably no impediment to him. But at home, as a Cuban, he may be a planter, a merchant, a physician, but he cannot expect to be a civil magistrate, or to hold a commission in the army, or an office in the police; and though he may be a lawyer, and read, sitting a written argument to a Court of Judges, he cannot expect to be himself a Judge. He may publish a book, but the government must be the responsible author. He may edit a journal, but the government must be the editor in-chief.

===Chinese===
热带确实是有「实业」这么一回事的。热带的劳动照常进行。尽管我们听说的、也确知的气候委靡之影响不一而足，白人自己纵不操劳，也是使他人操劳的原因。虽有种种社会与政治上的挫抑，虽有种种不利——食糖输入合众国要纳约百分之二十五的税，从合众国输入的面粉要缴足足百分之百的税——虽然缴的税比当今世上任何一国人民都重，还上缴着岁入，折净扣减之后每年不少于一千六百万；——同这一切不利相抗，这座岛屿依然多产而富庶。当然，它的实业种类寥寥。乡间无非种蔗制糖；城镇无非售糖运糖。再加上一点咖啡和铜、更多烟草、若干鲜果蜜饯，连同它们带动的商业和城镇的手艺买卖之需，便是它实业与资源的全部总和。科学、文艺、学术、武备、制造业，以及政治学、神学和搅动思想界心智的种种重大问题与议论的研求——在这些上面，古巴人全无份。这些东西从他们身旁经过，一如浩荡的湾流从他们的海岸边流过。在古巴，在年轻人和中年人的记忆里，也从来没有过辩论、投票、陪审团，没有过自治的哪怕最细小、最雏形的程序。体力劳动由非洲人和中国人承担，古巴人占有土地与资本，指挥农业实业；商业由古巴人和各国侨民分掌；而政府，民政的与军政的，操在旧西班牙公民的手里。没有古巴人投票，没有古巴人出席合法的政治集会、坐上陪审席、见过立法议会——除非在国外当作稀奇看，连市一级也没有；他也不曾协助制定、解释或执行过法律，不曾服过兵役——除非得到政府发给政府之友的特许。宗教上，他无从选择，只有罗马天主教与不信之间可选。治理他的法律在海外制定，由一个中央权力——一位外国的都督（Captain-General）——借助外国文武官员之手执行。古巴人没有公共生涯。他若迁居旧西班牙，又以西班牙王权的拥护者知名，他的克里奥尔出身多半碍不着他。但在本国，作为一个古巴人，他可以做种植园主、商人、医生，却休想当文职长官，休想在军队里挂衔，或供职警界；他可以做律师，坐下来向合议法庭递交书面辩词，却休想自己当上法官。他可以出一本书，但政府必须是那个负责任的作者。他可以编一份报纸，但政府必须是总编辑。

===Original===
At the chief stations on the road, there are fruit-sellers in abundance, with fruit fresh from the trees: oranges, bananas, sapotes, and cocoas. The cocoa is eaten at an earlier stage than that in which we see it at the North, for it is gathered for exportation after it has become hard. It is eaten here when no harder than a melon, and is cut through with a knife, and the soft white pulp, mixed with the milk, is eaten with a spoon. It is luscious and wholesome, much more so than when the rind has hardened into the shell, and the soft pulp into a hard meat.

A little later in the afternoon, the character of the views begins to change. The *ingenio*s and cane fields become less frequent, then cease altogether, and the houses have more the appearance of pleasure retreats than of working estates. The roads show lines of mules and horses, loaded with panniers of fruits, or sweeping the ground with the long stalks of fresh fodder laid across their backs, all moving towards a common centre. Pleasure carriages appear. Next comes the distant view of the Castle of Atares, and the Príncipe, and then the harbor and the sea, the belt of masts, the high ridge of fortifications, the blue and white and yellow houses, with brown tops; and now we are in the streets of Havana.

It seems like coming home; and I feel as if I had been an age away, when it is only eight days since I first saw Cuba. Here are the familiar signs﻿—*Por mayor y menor*, *Posada y Cantina*, *Tienda*, *Panadería*, *Relojería*, and the fanciful names of the shops, the high pitched falsetto cries of the streets, the long files of mules and horses, with panniers of fruit, or hidden, all but their noses and tails, under stacks of fresh fodder, the volantes, and the motley multitude of whites, blacks, and Chinese, soldiers and civilians, and occasionally priests﻿—Negro women, lottery ticket vendors, and the girl musicians with their begging tambourines.

The same idlers are at the door of Le Grand’s; a rehearsal, as usual, is going on at the head of the first flight; and the parrot is blinking at the hot, white walls of the court yard, and screaming bits of Spanish. My New York friends have got back from the country a day before me. I am installed in a better room than before, on the housetop, where the sun is hot, but where there is air and a view of the ocean.

===Chinese===
沿线大站上水果贩子多得很，卖的都是刚从树上摘的：橘子、香蕉、萨波特果和可可果。可可果在这里吃的阶段，比我们在北方见到的要早，因为供出口的须等它变硬了才采。这里吃它，是在还不过甜瓜那么硬的时候，用刀剖开，雪白的软瓤混着椰汁，拿勺子舀着吃。又香甜又滋养，比外皮硬成壳、软瓤结成硬肉之后再吃，强得多了。

午后稍晚，景物的性格开始变了。糖坊和蔗田渐渐稀少，随后绝迹，房屋愈发像消闲的别业，不像劳作的庄园。路上出现一队队骡马，驮着满驮的水果，或让鲜草料的长茎横搭在背上扫着地，全都朝着同一个中心移动。游乐的马车出现了。接着远远望见阿塔雷斯城堡（Atares）和王子堡（Príncipe），然后是港湾与大海、桅樯的环带、高高的炮台山脊、蓝白黄三色而顶子棕黄的房屋；转眼我们已身在哈瓦那的街道上了。

这竟像是回家一般；我恍然觉得离开了一世之久，其实距我初见古巴不过八天。满眼都是熟悉的招牌——「批发零售」（*Por mayor y menor*）、「客栈酒馆」（*Posada y Cantina*）、「杂货」（*Tienda*）、「面包房」（*Panadería*）、「钟表行」（*Relojería*）——还有店铺那些别致的字号，街头尖细高亢的叫卖，长长的骡马队伍，驮着水果，或几乎全身埋在青草料堆下、只露着鼻子和尾巴，还有沃兰特马车，以及杂色的众生——白人、黑人、中国人，军人和平民，偶尔还有教士——黑人妇女、卖彩票的小贩，还有摇着讨钱铃鼓的卖艺女孩。

勒格朗旅馆门口还是那群闲人；头一段楼梯顶上照例在进行着排练；鹦鹉对着院子里灼热的白墙眨眼，尖声叫着几句西班牙语。我的纽约朋友们比我早一天从乡间回来。我被安置进一间比先前好的屋子，在屋顶上，太阳很毒，但有风，还望得见海。

## XVII / 十七

===Original===
At Havana﻿—Dr. Howe﻿—Trial of Señor Maestri﻿—Music of the contradanza.

The warm bath round the corner, is a refreshment after a day’s railroad ride in such heat; and there, in the front room, the man in his shirt sleeves is serving out liquor, as before, and the usual company of Creoles is gathered about the billiard tables. After a dinner in the handsome, airy restaurant of Le Grand’s, I drive into the city in the evening, to the close streets of the Entramuros, and pay a visit to the lady whom I failed to see on my arrival. I am so fortunate as to meet her, and beside the pleasure to be found in her society, I am glad to be able to give her personal information from her attached and sympathizing friends, at the North.

While I am there, a tinkling sound of bells is heard in the streets, and lights flash by. It is a procession, going to carry the viaticum, the last sacrament, to a dying person.

===Chinese===
在哈瓦那——豪医生——马埃斯特里先生受审——对舞曲的音乐。

街角那家温水浴池，在这样的大热天坐了一天火车之后，正是一大清凉；浴池前厅里，那个只穿衬衫的男人还像先前那样斟酒分酒，弹子台四周照例聚着一群克里奥尔人。在勒格朗旅馆漂亮通风的餐厅用过正餐之后，傍晚我驱车进城，到城墙内区（Entramuros）那些逼仄的街道，拜访我抵达那天没能见到的那位夫人。我运气好，正遇见她在家里；除了与她相处的愉快，我也很高兴能当面捎来她在北方那些情深意厚、时时挂念的朋友们的音讯。

我在那里的时候，街上响起一阵铃铛的叮当声，灯光一队队掠过。那是一支队伍，正给一个垂死的人送临终圣体（viaticum）。

===Original===
From this house, I drove towards the water side, past the Plaza de Armas, the old Plaza de San Francisco, with its monastery turned into an almazen (a storehouse of merchandise), through the Calle de los Officios, to the boardinghouse of Madame Almy, to call upon Dr. and Mrs. Howe. Mr. Parker left Havana, as he intended, last Tuesday, for Santa Cruz. He found Havana rather too hot for his comfort, and Santa Cruz, the most healthful and temperate of the islands, had always been his destination. He had visited a few places in the city, and among others, the College of Belen, where he had been courteously received by the Jesuits. I found that they knew his reputation as a scholar and writer, and a leading champion of modern Theism in America. Dr. Howe had called at Le Grand’s, yesterday, to invite me to go with him to attend a trial, at the Audiencia, which attracted a good deal of interest among the Creoles. The story, as told by the friends of Señor Maestri, the defendant, is, that in the performance of a judicial duty, he discharged a person against whom the government was proceeding illegally, and that this led to a correspondence between him and the authorities, which resulted in his being deposed and brought to trial, before the Audiencia, on a charge of disrespect to the Captain-General. I have no means of learning the correctness of this statement, at present﻿—

> “I say the tale as ’twas said to me.”

The cause has, at all events, excited a deep interest among the Creoles, who see in it another proof of the unlimited character of the centralized power that governs them. I regret that I missed a scene of so much interest and instruction. Dr. Howe told me that Maestri’s counsel, Señor Azcárate, a young lawyer, defended his friend courageously; but the evidence being all in writing, without the exciting conflicts and vicissitudes of oral testimony, and the written arguments being delivered sitting, there was not much in the proceedings to stimulate the Creole excitability. No decision was given, the Court taking time to deliberate. It seems to have been a Montalembert trial, on a small theatre.

===Chinese===
从这座宅子，我驱车驶向水边，经过武器广场（Plaza de Armas）、旧日的圣弗朗西斯科广场（Plaza de San Francisco）——它那座修道院如今改作了货栈（almacen）——取道奥菲西奥斯街（Calle de los Officios），来到阿尔米太太（Madame Almy）的公寓，拜访豪医生（Dr. Howe）伉俪。帕克先生（Mr. Parker）按他的计划，已于上星期二离开哈瓦那，前往圣克鲁斯（Santa Cruz）。他觉得哈瓦那太热，住着不适意，而圣克鲁斯是诸岛中最卫生、气候最温和的，本来就是他一直要去的地方。他在城里游览了几处，其中之一是贝伦学院（College of Belen），耶稣会士们殷勤接待了他。我发现他们知道他的名声——学者、作家、美国现代有神论的一位主将。豪医生昨天到勒格朗旅馆来过，邀我同去高等法院（Audiencia）旁听一场审判，这案子在克里奥尔人中引起了相当大的关注。据被告马埃斯特里先生（Señor Maestri）的朋友们讲，事情的原委是：他在履行一项司法职责时，开释了一个政府正以非法手段构陷的人，由此引来他与当局之间的公文往来，结果是先被撤职，后被提交高等法院受审，罪名是对都督不敬。这番说法是否确实，我此刻无从查考——

> 「这故事是人家怎么说，我便怎么说。」

无论如何，这桩案子在克里奥尔人中激起了深切关注，他们在其中又一次看到了统治他们的那个中央化权力毫无限度的性质。我很遗憾错过了这样一场既有趣又有教益的场面。豪医生告诉我，马埃斯特里的辩护人阿斯卡雷特先生（Señor Azcárate）——一位年轻律师——为他的朋友作了勇敢的辩护；但证据全是书面的，没有口头对质那种扣人心弦的交锋与起伏，书面辩词又是坐着递交的，所以整个过程里，能刺激克里奥尔人兴奋之处的并不多。法庭没有当庭判决，留待审议。这似乎是一场小舞台上的蒙塔朗贝尔式审判。

===Original===
Tonight there is again a máscara at the next door, but my room is now more remote, and I am able to sleep through it. Once I awoke. It was nearly five o’clock. The music was still going on, but in softer and more subdued tones. The drums and trumpets were hushed, and all had fallen, as if by the magic touch of the approaching dawn, into a trance of sound, a rondo of constantly returning delicious melody, as nearly irresistible to the charmed sense as sound can be conceived to be﻿—just bordering on the fusing state between sense and spirit. It is a *contradanza* of Cuba. The great bells beat five, over the city; and instantly the music ceases, and is heard no more. The watchmen cry the hour and the bells of the hospitals and convents sound their matins though it is yet dark.

===Chinese===
