今夜隔壁又是一场化装舞会（máscara），但我这回的屋子离得远了，能够睡得着。中途醒过一回。将近五点了。音乐还在继续，只是换成了更柔和、更收敛的调子。鼓和号都歇了，一切仿佛被渐近的黎明的魔法一触，都沉入了一种出神的声音境界——一支不断回旋往复的、美妙的回旋曲，对着了迷的听觉而言，几乎到了声音所能想象的最难抗拒的地步——恰恰行走在感官与心灵之间将融未融的临界上。这是一支古巴的对舞曲（*contradanza*）。大钟在全城上空敲了五下；音乐应声而止，再不闻响。更夫报着时辰，医院和修道院的钟响起了晨祷，虽然天还没有亮。

## XVIII / 十八

===Original===
Mass at the Belen﻿—Worship in Cuban churches﻿—Casa de Beneficencia﻿—Hospital Militar﻿—Sisters of Charity﻿—Worship of the Sisters in their chapel﻿—Sick soldiers.

At break of day, I am in the delightful sea baths again, not ill-named Recreo and Elíseo. But the forlorn chain gang are mustered before the Presidio. It is Sunday, but there is no day of rest for them.

At eight o’clock I present myself at the Belen. A lady, who was passing through the cloister, with head and face covered by the usual black veil, turned and came to me. It was Mrs. —﻿—﻿— whom I had seen last evening. She kindly took me to the sacristy, and asked someone to tell Father —﻿—﻿— that I was there, and then went to her place in church. While waiting in the sacristy, I saw the robing and unrobing of the officiating priests, the preparation of altar ceremonials by boys and men, and could hear the voices and music in the church, on the other side of the great altar. The manner of the Jesuits is in striking contrast with that at the Cathedral. All is slow, orderly and reverential, whether on the part of men or boys. Instead of the hurried walk, the nod and duck, there is a slow march, a kneeling, or a reverential bow. At a small side altar, in the sacristy, communion is administered by a single priest. Among the recipients are several men of mature years and respectable position; and side by side with them, the poor and the Negroes. In the Church, there is no distinction of race or color.

===Chinese===
贝伦学院的弥撒——古巴教堂中的礼拜——慈善堂——陆军医院——仁爱会修女——修女们在小堂中的礼拜——患病的士兵。

破晓时分，我又到了那几处可爱的海水浴场——名叫「欢乐」（Recreo）与「极乐」（Elíseo），名不虚传。可那凄惨的锁链苦工队，却在监狱（Presidio）前集合了。这天是星期日，他们却没有安息的日子。

八点钟，我到贝伦去。一位正从回廊走过的女士，头上脸上罩着惯常的黑纱，转过身朝我走来。原来是昨晚见到的那位———夫人。她好心领我到圣器室，请人去通报———神父说我在这里，随后便到她在教堂里的座位上去了。在圣器室等候时，我看见主祭神父们穿祭衣、脱祭衣，男孩和男人们预备祭台的仪节，还听得见大祭台另一面教堂里的歌声与音乐。耶稣会士的做派同大教堂里的判然不同。一切都从容、有序而虔敬，无论大人孩子。没有那种匆匆的脚步、点头哈腰，而是缓慢的行进、跪拜或恭敬的鞠躬。圣器室里一座小小的侧祭台前，一位神父独自在送圣体。领圣体的人中有几位是上了年纪、身家体面的人；同他们并排的，是穷人和黑人。在教会里，没有种族与肤色之分。

===Original===
Father —﻿—﻿— appears, is unrobed, and takes me to the gallery of the church, near the organ. From this, I looked down upon a sea of rich costumes of women, veiled heads, and kneeling figures, literally covering the floor of the church. On the marble pavement, the little carpets are spread, and on these, as close as they can sit or kneel, are the ladies of rank and wealth of Havana. A newcomer glides in among them, seeking room for her carpet, or room of charity or friendship on a carpet already spread; and the kneelers or sitters move and gather in their wide skirts to let her pass. Here and there a servant in livery winds his way behind his mistress, bearing her carpet, and returns to the porch when it has been spread. The whole floor is left to women. The men gather about the walls and doorways, or sit in the gallery, which is reserved for them. But among the women, though chiefly of rank and wealth, are some who are Negroes, usually distinguished by the plain shawl, instead of the veil over the head. The Countess Villanueva, immensely rich, of high rank, and of a name great in the annals of Cuba, but childless, and blind, and a widow, is led in by the hand by her Negro servant. The service of the altar is performed with dignity and reverence, and the singing, which is by the Jesuit Brothers themselves, is admirable. In the choir I recognized my new friends, the Rector and young Father Cabre, the professor of physics. The “Tantum ergo Sacramentum,” which was sung kneeling, brought tears into my eyes, and kept them there.

After service, Mr. —﻿—﻿— came to me, and made an engagement to show me the benevolent institutions on the Bishop’s list, accepting my invitation to breakfast at Le Grand’s, at eleven o’clock. At eleven he came, and after a quiet breakfast in a side room, we went to the house of Señor —﻿—﻿— whom he well knows, in the hope that he would go with us. The Señor was engaged to meet one of the Fathers at noon, and could not go, but introduced to me a relative of his, a young student of medicine in the University, who offered to take me to the Presidio and other places, the next day.

===Chinese===
———神父来了，脱下祭衣，领我上了教堂的楼廊，就在管风琴近旁。从那里俯望下去，是一片妇女华服的海洋——罩纱的头、跪着的人影，实实在在铺满了教堂的地面。大理石地面上铺开一方方小地毯，哈瓦那的贵妇名媛们挨挨挤挤地或坐或跪在地毯上。新来的人在她们中间悄然滑入，为自己的地毯寻一块地方，或者凭着慈善或交情，在已铺开的地毯上求一席之地；跪着的坐着的都挪动身子、拢起宽大的裙子让她过去。这里那里，一个穿号衣的仆人跟在女主人身后曲折穿行，捧着她的地毯，铺好之后便退回门廊。整个地板都留给妇女。男人们聚在墙边和门口，或者坐在专为他们留出的楼廊里。而在妇女中间——虽多为有身份的富贵人家——也有几个黑人女子，通常以素色披巾代替头纱，一望可辨。比利亚努埃瓦伯爵夫人（Countess Villanueva）——家资巨万、门第显赫、姓氏在古巴史册上赫赫有名，却无儿无女、双目失明、又是孀居——由她的黑人仆人牵着手领进来。祭台的仪节行得庄重虔敬，唱诗——由耶稣会神父们亲任——出色之极。唱诗班里我认出了我的新交：院长神父和年轻的卡布雷神父（Father Cabre），那位物理学教授。那首跪唱的《圣事颂》（Tantum ergo Sacramentum），唱得我热泪盈眶，久久不干。

礼拜之后，———先生过来找我，约定带我去看主教单子上的那几处慈善机构，并接受了我的邀请，十一点到勒格朗旅馆用早餐。十一点他来了，在侧间安静地用过早餐，我们便去———先生府上——我朋友同他很熟——盼着他能同去。那位先生约好正午要去见一位神父，去不成了，但把他的一位亲戚介绍给我，一个大学里的年轻医科学生，他提出第二天带我去看监狱和其他几处地方。

===Original===
It occurred to us to call upon a young American lady, who was residing at the house of a Spanish lady of wealth and rank, and invite her to go with us to see the Beneficencia, which we thought she might do, as it is an institution under the charge of nuns, and she was to go with a Padre in full dress. But the customs of the country are rigid. Miss —﻿—﻿— was very desirous to go, but had doubts. She consulted the lady of the house, who would know, if anyone could, the etiquette of Havana. The Señora’s reply was, “You are are American, and may do anything.” This settled the matter in the negative, and we went alone. Now we drive to Don Juan —﻿—﻿—’s. The gate is closed. The driver, who is a white, gets off and makes a feeble and timid rap at the door. “Knock louder!” says my friend, in Spanish. “What cowards they are!” he adds to me. The man makes a knock, a little louder. “There, see that! Peeking into the keyhole! Mean! An Englishman would beat the door down before he would do that.” Don Juan is in the country﻿—so we fail of all our expected companions. The Casa de Beneficencia is a large institution, for orphan and destitute children, for infirm old persons, and for the insane. It is admirably situated, bordering on the open sea, with fresh air and very good attention to ventilation in the rooms. It is a government institution, but is placed under charge of the Sisters of Charity, one of whom accompanied us about the building. Though called a government institution, it must not be supposed that it is a charity from the crown. On the contrary, it is supported by a specific appropriation of certain of the taxes and revenues of the island. In the building, is a church not yet finished, large enough for all the inmates, and a quiet little private chapel for the Sisters’ devotions, where a burning lamp indicated the presence of the Sacrament on the small altar. I am sorry to have forgotten the number of children. It was large, and included both sexes, with a separate department for each. In a third department, are the insane. They are kindly treated and not confined, except when violent; but the Sister told us they had no medical treatment unless in case of sickness (Dr. Howe told me that he was also so informed). The last department is for aged and indigent women.

===Chinese===
我们想到去拜访一位年轻的美国小姐，她寄住在一位有钱有地位的西班牙太太家里，便邀她同去看慈善堂（Beneficencia）。我们想她或许可以去，因为那是一所修女主管的机构，而且她将有位身穿全套法衣的神父陪同。但此地风习森严。———小姐极想去，却有顾虑。她请教了那家的太太——若有人懂得哈瓦那的规矩，非她莫属。太太的答复是：「你是美国人，什么事都可以做。」这一句便把事情断然否决了，我们只好自己去。接着我们驱车到唐·胡安———府上。大门紧闭。车夫——是个白人——下来，怯生生地在门上轻轻敲了两下。「敲响些！」我朋友用西班牙语说。「这帮胆小鬼！」他又对我说。那人敲得响了一点。「瞧见没有？扒着钥匙孔往里瞅！下作！英国人宁可把门砸烂，也不干这个。」唐·胡安下乡去了——于是我们指望的同伴一个也没凑齐。慈善堂（Casa de Beneficencia）是一所大机构，收容孤儿和贫困儿童、老弱无依的人，以及疯人。位置好极了，濒临开阔的海面，空气新鲜，各房间的通风也照顾得很周到。这是一所官办机构，但交由仁爱会修女（Sisters of Charity）管理，一位修女陪我们参观了全楼。虽说是官办，却不可以为是来自王室的恩赐。相反，它靠岛上某几项税捐岁入的专项拨款维持。楼里有一座尚未完工的教堂，容得下全院的人，另有一间供修女们祈祷的清静小堂，小祭台上燃着一盏灯，表明圣体的临在。孩子们的人数我很遗憾忘了。数目不小，男女兼收，各居一部。第三部住疯人。他们受到善待，除狂躁时外不加拘禁；但修女告诉我们，除非染病，他们不受任何医治（豪医生说他也听人这样讲过）。最后一部收容年老贫苦的妇女。

===Original===
One of the little orphans clung to the Sister who accompanied us, holding her hand, and nestling in her coarse but clean blue gown; and when we took our leave, and I put a small coin into her little soft hand, her eyes brightened up into a pretty smile.

The number of the Sisters is not full. As none have joined the order from Cuba (I am told literally none), they are all from abroad chiefly from France and Spain; and having acclimation to go through, with exposure to yellow fever and cholera, many of those that come here die in the first or second summer. And yet they still come, in simple, religious fidelity, under the shadow of death.

The Casa de Beneficencia must be pronounced by all, even by those accustomed to the system and order of the best charitable institutions in the world, a credit to the island of Cuba. The charity is large and liberal, and the order and neatness of its administration are beyond praise.

===Chinese===
一个小孤儿依偎着陪我们的修女，拉着她的手，贴着她那粗糙却干净的蓝袍；我们告辞时，我把一枚小钱放进她柔软的小手里，她眼睛一亮，绽出一个可爱的笑。

修女的人数不足编制。因为没有古巴本地人入会（我听说简直一个也没有），她们全来自海外，主要是法国和西班牙；她们要过水土气候这一关，又要暴露在黄热病和霍乱之下，来的人许多在头一个或第二个夏天便死去了。然而她们仍旧来，怀着单纯的、宗教的忠贞，行在死亡的荫翳之下。

这所慈善堂，任何人——哪怕是见惯了世上最好慈善机构的规制与条理的人——都必得承认，是古巴岛的一份光荣。慈善事业宏大而慷慨，管理的整饬与洁净更无可挑剔。

===Original===
From the Beneficencia we drove to the Military Hospital. This is a huge establishment, designed to accommodate all the sick of the army. The walls are high, the floors are of brick and scrupulously clean, as are all things under the charge of the Sisters of Charity; and the ventilation is tolerable. The building suffered from the explosion of the magazine last year, and some quarters have not yet been restored for occupation. The number of sick soldiers now in hospital actually exceeds one thousand! Most of them are young, some mere lads, victims of the conscription of Old Spain, which takes them from their rustic homes in Andalusia and Catalonia and the Pyrenees, to expose them to the tropical heats of Cuba, and to the other dangers of its climate. Most had fevers. We saw a few cases of *vómito*. Notwithstanding all that is said about the healthfulness of a winter in Cuba, the experienced Sister Servant (which, I believe, is the title of the Superior of a body of Sisters of Charity) told us that a few sporadic cases of yellow fever occur in Havana, in all seasons of the year; but that we need not fear to go through the wards. One patient was covered with the blotches of recent smallpox. It was affecting to see the wistful eyes of these poor, fevered soldier boys, gazing on the serene, kind countenances of the nuns, and thinking of their mothers and sisters in the dear home in Old Spain, and feeling, no doubt, that this womanly, religious care was the nearest and best substitute.

The present number of Sisters, charged with the entire care of this great hospital, except the duty of cooks and the mere manual and mechanic labor necessarily done by men, is not above twenty-five. The Sister Servant told us that the proper complement was forty. The last summer, eleven of these devoted women died of yellow fever. Every summer, when yellow fever or cholera prevails, some of them die. They know it. Yet the vacancies are filled up; and their serene and ever happy countenances give the stranger no indication that they have bound themselves to the bedside of contagious and loathsome diseases every year, and to scenes of sickness and death every day.

===Chinese===
从慈善堂我们驱车前往陆军医院（Hospital Militar）。这是一座庞大的机构，设计收容陆军的全部病员。墙高，砖地，洁净得一丝不苟——仁爱会修女经管之处莫不如此——通风也过得去。这建筑去年毁于火药库爆炸，有些部分还没有修复住人。眼下住院的病兵竟超过一千人！他们大多年轻，有的还是半大孩子，是旧西班牙征兵制的牺牲品——把他们从安达卢西亚、加泰罗尼亚和比利牛斯的农家带走，抛到古巴的热带酷暑和气候的种种险恶之中。多数人害着热病。我们见到几例黄热病（*vómito*）。尽管人们把在古巴过冬说得如何有益卫生，那位经验丰富的主管修女（Sister Servant——我想这是一队仁爱会修女之首的称谓）告诉我们，哈瓦那一年四季都有零星的黄热病例；不过我们穿行病房不必害怕。一个病人身上出着新近天花的痘斑。看着这些可怜的、发烧的士兵孩子那双双渴念的眼睛，望着修女们宁静慈和的面容，想着旧西班牙家中亲爱的母亲姐妹，无疑也觉得这女性宗教的看护，是最近便、最好的替身——此情此景，令人感动。

如今照管这座大医院全部事务的修女——厨役和必须由男人做的纯体力、机械活计除外——不满二十五人。主管修女告诉我们，足额应是四十人。去年夏天，这些忘我的女性有十一位死于黄热病。每逢黄热病或霍乱流行的夏天，总有几位死去。她们自己知道。然而缺额照样补齐；她们那宁静而永远愉快的面容，绝不向生人透露：她们已把自己年年拴在传染恶疾的床边，日日拴在疾病与死亡的现场。

===Original===
As we walked through the passageways, we came upon the little private chapel of the Sisters. Here was a scene I can never forget. It was an hour assigned for prayer. All who could leave the sick wards﻿—not more than twelve or fourteen﻿—were kneeling in that perfectly still, secluded, darkened room, in a double row, all facing to the altar, on which burned one taper, showing the presence of the Sacrament, and all in silent prayer.﻿—That double row of silent, kneeling women, unconscious of the presence of anyone, in their snow-white, close caps and long capes, and coarse, clean, blue gowns﻿—heroines, if the world ever had heroines, their angels beholding the face of their Father in heaven, as they knelt on earth!

It was affecting and yet almost amusing﻿—it would have been amusing anywhere else﻿—that these simple creatures, not knowing the ways of the world, and desirous to have soft music fill their room, as they knelt at silent prayer, and not having (for their duties preclude it) any skill in the practice of music, had a large music box wound and placed on a stand, in the rear, giving out its liquid tones, just loud enough to pervade the air, without forcing attention. The effect was beautiful; and yet the tunes were not all, nor chiefly, religious. They were such as any music box would give. But what do these poor creatures know of what the world marches to, or dances to, or makes love by? To them it was all music, and pure and holy! Minute after minute we stood, waiting for, but not desiring, an end of these delightful sounds, and a dissolving of this spell of silent adoration. One of the Sisters began prayers aloud, a series of short prayers and adorations and thanksgivings, to each of which, at its close, the others made response in full, sweet voices. The tone of prayer of this Sister was just what it should be. No skill of art could reach it. How much truer than the cathedral, or the great ceremonial! It was low, yet audible, composed, reverent: neither the familiar, which offends so often, nor the rhetorical, which always offends, but that unconscious, sustained intonation, not of speech, but of music, which frequent devotions in company with others naturally call out; showing us that poetry and music, and not prose and speech, are the natural expressions of the deepest and highest emotions.

They rose, with the prayer of benediction, and we withdrew. They separated, to station themselves, one in each ward of the hospital, there, aloud and standing, to repeat their prayers﻿—the sick men raising themselves on their elbows, or sitting in bed, or, if more feeble, raising their eyes and clasping their hands, and all who can or choose, joining in the responses.

===Chinese===
我们走过过道时，正遇上修女们的小小私堂。那幅景象我永不能忘。正是一个祷告的时辰。凡能离开病房的——不过十二三人——都跪在那间绝对静谧、幽闭、昏暗的屋子里，排成两行，全体面向祭台；台上燃着一支细烛，标示圣体的临在；人人默祷。——那两行沉默跪着的女子，浑然不觉有旁人在场，戴着雪白的紧帽，披着长长的披肩，穿着粗糙而洁净的蓝袍——倘若世上真有女杰，她们便是；她们跪在地上的时候，她们的天使正在天上瞻仰天父的圣容！

这一幕动人，却又有几分可笑——在别处准会可笑——这些单纯的人不谙世事，想让轻柔的音乐充满她们跪祷的静室，而她们的职责又不允许她们习练音乐，便叫人上满一架大八音盒，摆在后面的架上，流出清越的曲调，恰好弥漫空气而不逼人注意。效果好极了；然而曲子并非全然、也非主要是宗教的，不过随便哪架八音盒都会奏的那些。可是，这世上的人行军用什么曲子、跳舞用什么曲子、谈情说什么曲子，这些可怜的人又知道什么呢？在她们听来，这一切都是音乐，都是纯净神圣的！一分钟又一分钟，我们站着，等这美妙的声响结束，等这静默崇拜的符咒解除，却又并不真盼望它结束。一位修女开始出声祈祷，一连串短短的祷词、崇敬与感恩，每念完一句，其余的人便用饱满甜美的声音应答。这位修女祈祷的声调正是它应有的样子，任何艺术的技巧都达不到。比大教堂、比盛大的仪典真纯多少啊！它低而可闻，安详虔敬：既非那常常冒犯人的随便，也非那永远冒犯人的雕琢，而是那种不自觉的、绵延的吟诵——不是言语的调子，而是音乐的调子——是常与众人共同灵修自然唤出的声音；它昭示我们：诗与音乐，而非散文与言语，才是最深最高情感的本然表达。

她们念过祝福祷文起身，我们便退了出来。她们四散分开，在医院每间病房里各站一位，站在那里出声诵祷——病人们用臂肘支起身子，或坐在床上，更虚弱的便抬起眼睛、合起双手，凡是能够而且愿意的，都跟着应答。

## XX / 二十

===Original===
A bullfight.
===Chinese===
一场斗牛。

===Original===
A bullfight has been advertised all over the town, at the Plaza de Toros. Shall we go? I would not, if ’twere only pleasure that I was seeking. As I am sure I expect only the contrary, and wish merely to learn the character of this national recreation, I will go.

The Plaza de Toros is a wooden amphitheatre, in the suburbs, open at the top﻿—a circle of rising seats, with the arena in the centre. I am late. The cries of the people inside are loud, sharp, and constant; a full band is blowing its trumpets and beating its drums; and the late stragglers are justling for their tickets. I go through at a low door﻿—find myself under benches filled with an eager, stamping, shouting multitude, make my way through a passage, and come out on the shady side, for it is a late afternoon sun, and take my place at a good point of view. A bull, with some blood about his forequarters and two or three darts (*bandarillas*) sticking in his neck, is trotting harmlessly about the arena, “more sinned against than sinning,” and seeming to have no other desire than to get out. Two men, each carrying a long, stout, wooden pole, pointed with a short piece of iron, not long enough to kill, but only to drive off and to goad, are mounted on two of the sorriest nags eyes ever beheld﻿—reprieved jades, whom it would not pay to feed and scarcely pay to kill, and who have been left to take their chances of death here. They could hardly be pricked into a trot, and were too weak to escape. I have seen horses in every stage of life and in every degree of neglect, but no New York Negro hack-driver would have taken these for a gift, if he were obliged to keep them. The bull could not be said to run away from the horses, for they did not pursue; but when, distracted by sights and sounds, he came against a horse, the horse stood still to be gored, and the bull only pushed against him with his head, until driven off by the punching of the iron-pointed pole of the horseman.
===Chinese===
全城各处贴出了告示：斗牛场（Plaza de Toros）有斗牛。去不去呢？倘若我寻求的只是乐趣，我是不去的。但我确知自己预期到的必是相反的东西，又只想见识见识这项国民娱乐的品性，那便去罢。

斗牛场是城郊的一座木造圆形剧场，顶上露天——一圈层层升起的座位，正中是沙场。我来迟了。场内人声鼎沸，尖锐刺耳，喊个不停；一支完整的乐队正吹着号、敲着鼓；迟来的人还在门口挤着买票。我从一扇矮门钻进去，发觉自己置身于坐满了急切跺脚、大呼小叫的人群的长凳之下，穿行过一条过道，走到背阴的一面——此时已是下午的斜阳——拣了个视野好的位置坐下。一头公牛，前躯带着血迹，颈上插着两三支花镖（*bandarillas*），正无害地在场中慢跑，「受的罪多于造的孽」，看上去别无他愿，只想出去。两个汉子，各执一根粗长的木杆，杆头镶一截短铁，短得杀不了命，只能驱赶和戳刺，胯下骑的是眼睛所见过的最寒碜的两匹驽马——它们是得了缓刑的老马，喂养不合算，宰杀也几乎不合算，便被丢在这里听天由命、碰运气等死的。针刺都难叫它们小跑起来，虚弱得连逃跑都不能。马的各色生涯、各等疏于照管的惨状我都见识过，可这样的马，纽约的黑人马车夫就是白送也不要，若是还得养着的话。说公牛躲着马跑也不尽然，因为马根本不来追；只是当它被眼前的光景和耳边的声响弄得心慌意乱、撞到一匹马身上时，那马便一动不动站着挨抵，而牛也只用头顶顶着它，直到被骑手的铁头杆捅开才算完。

===Original===
Around the arena are sentry boxes, each large enough to hold two men, behind which they can easily jump, but which the bull can not enter; and from these, the cowardly wretches run out, flourish a red cloth at the bull, and jump back. Three or four men, with darts in hand, run before the bull, entice him by flapping their red cloths, and, as he trots up to them, stick *bandarillas* into his neck. These torment the bull, and he tries to shake them off, and paws the ground; but still he shows no fight. He trots to the gate, and snuffs to get out. Some of the multitude cry “*Fuera el toro! Fuera el toro*!” which means that he is a failure, and must be let out at the gate. Others are excited, and cry for the killer, the *matador*; and a demoniacal scene follows, of yells and shouts, half drowned by twenty or thirty drums and trumpets. The cries to go on prevail; and the matador appears, dressed in a tight-fitting suit of green smallclothes, with a broad silver stripe, jerkin, and stockings﻿—a tall, light complexioned, elegantly made, glittering man, bearing in one hand a long, heavy, dull black sword, and in the other a broad, red cloth. Now comes the harrying and distracting of the bull by flags, and red cloths, and darts; the matador runs before, flings his cloth up and down; the bull trots towards it﻿—no furious rush, or maddened dash, but a moderate trot﻿—the cloth is flashed over his face, and one skilfully directed lunge of the sword into his back neck, and he drops instantly dead at the feet of the matador, at the very spot where he received the stab. Frantic shouts of applause follow; and the matador bows around, like an applauded circus rider, and retires. The great gate opens, and three horses abreast are driven in, decked with ribbons, to drag the bull round the arena. But they are such feeble animals that, with all the flourish of music and the whipping of drivers, they are barely able to tug the bull along over the tan, in a straight line for the gate, through which the sorry pageant and harmless bull disappear.

Now, some meagre, hungry, sallow, sweaty mean-looking degenerates of Spain jump in and rake over the arena, and cover up the blood, and put things to rights again; and I find time to take a view of the company. Thankful I am, and creditable it is, that there are no women. Yes﻿—there are two mulatto women, in a seat on the sunny side, which is the cheap side. And there are two shrivelled, dark, Creole women, in a box; and there is one girl of eight or ten years, in full dress, with an elderly man. These are all the women. In the State Box, under the faded royal arms, are a few officials, not of high degree. The rest of the large company is a motley collection, chiefly of the middle or lower classes, mostly standing on the benches, and nearly all smoking.
===Chinese===
沙场四周是一圈岗亭，每座大小恰容两人，人可以轻易跳到亭后藏身，牛却进不去；那群怯懦的可怜虫便从亭后窜出来，朝牛抖开一块红布，又赶紧跳回去。三四条汉子手执花镖，在牛前头跑动，抖动红布逗引它；等它小跑过来，便把*bandarillas*插进它的颈子。这些花镖折磨着牛，它竭力想甩掉，刨着地面；可它依然没有半点斗志。它小跑到大门口，喷着鼻子想出去。人群中有的喊「*Fuera el toro! Fuera el toro*!」（把牛牵出去！），意思是这头牛不中用，得放它从门里出去。另一些人却兴奋起来，嚷着要杀手，要*matador*（执剑士）；接下来便是一场群魔乱舞，喊声叫声，被二三十面鼓和号压去了一半。叫继续的声音占了上风；执剑士出场了，穿一身绿色紧身的及膝裤装，缀着宽阔的银条，配上短上衣和长袜——是个高挑、肤色白皙、身段漂亮、浑身闪亮的人物，一手擎着一柄又长又重的黑鞘钝剑，一手拿着一幅宽大的红布。接着便是用旗帜、红布和花镖轮番搅扰、迷惑这头牛；执剑士跑在前头，把红布上下翻飞；牛朝布小跑过去——不是狂怒的冲撞，也不是发疯的突进，只是不紧不慢的小跑——红布在它脸前一晃，一剑认准部位刺进它的后颈，它当场倒在执剑士脚边，倒在挨那一剑的地方，立时毙命。疯狂的喝彩声随之而起；执剑士四面鞠躬，像个博得满场彩的马戏骑手，随即退场。大门口开了，三匹马并辔驱入，身上披红挂彩，要把牛绕场拖走。可这几匹马实在孱弱，任凭乐队如何热闹、驭者如何抽打，它们勉勉强强把牛拖过沙地，照直朝门口去，那寒碜的排场和无害的公牛便从门里消失了。

这时，几个面黄肌瘦、饿相、淌汗、面相卑琐的西班牙退化之徒跳进场来，耙平沙地，盖掉血迹，把一切归置如初；我便得空打量打量这满场的看客。值得庆幸、也足称道的是，场中没有女人。是有的——日头晒着的那一面，也就是便宜的那一面，坐着两个穆拉托（mulatto）女人。包厢里有两个干瘦黝黑的克里奥尔（Creole）女人；还有一个八九岁或十来岁的女孩，盛装打扮，陪着一个上了年纪的男人。女人就这么多。官方包厢里，在褪了色的王家纹章底下，坐着几个官员，品级不高。其余的大群看客是杂七杂八的一伙，多为中等或下等阶级，大都站在长凳上，几乎人人嘴里叼着烟。

===Original===
The music beats and brays again, the great gates open, and another bull rushes in, distracted by sights and sounds so novel, and for a few minutes shows signs of power and vigor; but, as he becomes accustomed to the scene, he tames down; and after several minutes of flaunting of cloths and flags, and piercing with darts, and punching with the poles of the horsemen, he runs under the poor white horse, and upsets him, but leaves him unhurt by his horns; has a leisurely trial of endurance with the red horse, goring him a little with one horn, and receiving the pike of the driver﻿—the horse helpless and patient, and the bull very reasonable and temperate in the use of his power﻿—and then is enticed off by flags, and worried with darts; and, at last, a new matador appears﻿—a fierce-looking fellow, dressed in dark green, with a large head of curling, snaky, black hair, and a skin almost black. He makes a great strut and flourish, and after two or three unsuccessful attempts to get the bull head on, at length, getting a fair chance, plunges his black sword to the hilt in the bull’s neck﻿—but there is no fall of the bull. He has missed the spinal cord, and the bull trots off, bleeding in a small stream, with a sword-handle protruding a few inches above the hide of his back-neck. The spectators hoot their contempt for the failure; but with no sign of pity for the beast. The bull is weakened, but trots about and makes a few runs at cloths, and the sword is drawn from his hide by an agile dart-sticker, (*bandarillero*), and given to the black bully in dark green, who makes one more lunge, with no better success. The bull runs round, and reels, and staggers, and falls half down, gets partly up, lows and breathes heavily, is pushed over and held down, until a butcher dispatches him with a sharp knife, at the spinal cord. Then come the opened gates, the three horses abreast, decked with ribbons, the hard tug at the bull’s body over the ground﻿—his limbs still swaying with remaining life, the clash and clang of the band, and the yells of the people.

Shall I stay another? Perhaps it may be more successful, and﻿—if the new bull will only bruise somebody! But the new bull is a failure. After all their attempts to excite him, he only trots round, and snuffs at the gates; and the cry of “*Fuera el toro*!” becomes so general, with the significant triple beat of the feet, in time with the words, all over the house, that the gates are opened, and the bull trots through, to his quarters.
===Chinese===
乐声又咚咚呱呱地响起来，大门洞开，另一头公牛冲了进来，被这闻所未闻的光景和声响弄得魂不守舍，头几分钟里倒还显出几分力气和精神；可等它看惯了这场面，便也驯了下来。红布旗帜招摇了几分钟，花镖扎了几分钟，骑手的木杆戳了几分钟之后，它从那匹可怜的白马肚皮下钻过，把马掀翻在地，却没让角伤着它；又同那匹红马不慌不忙地较量了一场耐性，用一只角轻轻抵了它一下，也挨了驭者的长杆——马无可奈何地忍着，牛使起力气来倒也十分有理有节——随后又被旗帜引开，被花镖折磨；最后，新的执剑士出场了——一个面目凶悍的家伙，穿一身深绿，生着一大头卷曲如蛇的黑发，皮肤黑得近乎黑色。他大摇大摆、耀武扬威了一番，试了两三次都没能让牛正面对着他，终于得了个正着的机会，把那柄黑剑连柄插进牛颈——可是牛没有倒下。他没扎中脊髓，牛小跑着走开，淌出一道细细的血流，后颈皮毛外还露出几寸剑柄。看客们冲这失败发出轻蔑的嘘声；对那头牲口却无半点怜惜之意。牛已虚弱，却还小跑着，朝红布冲了几回；一个灵巧的插镖手（*bandarillero*）把剑从它皮肉里拔出，交还给那深绿衣裳的黑脸莽汉，他又刺了一剑，仍不见起色。牛绕着场子跑，摇晃着，踉跄着，半倒下去，又挣扎着半起来，哞哞地叫，喘着粗气，被推倒按住，直到一个屠夫用快刀对准脊髓结果了它。接着又是洞开的大门，三匹披红挂彩并辔的马，拖着牛身在地上艰难地拽——它的四肢还带着残生在摆动，乐队一片铿锵，人群一片嚎叫。

再看一头么？也许下一场会更精彩些，再说——要是新来的这头牛肯伤个人才好呢！可新来的这头是个废物。任凭他们百般撩拨，它只管绕场小跑，凑着大门喷鼻子；「*Fuera el toro*!」（把牛牵出去）的喊声越来越齐，满场的脚跟着那三个字合拍地跺出三响，大门于是开了，公牛小跑着穿门出去，回它的栏去了。

===Original===
But the meanness, and cruelty, and impotency of this crowd! They cry out to the spear-men and the dart-men, and to the tormentors, and to the bull, and to the horses, and to each other, in a Babel of sounds, where no man’s voice can possibly be distinguished ten feet from him, all manner of advice and encouragement or derision, like children at a play. One full grown, well-dressed young man, near me, kept up a constant cry to the men in the ring, when I am sure no one could distinguish his words, and no one cared to﻿—until I became so irritated that I could have throttled him.

But, such you are! You can cry and howl at bullfights and cockfights and in the pits of operas and theatres, and drive bulls and horses distracted, and urge gallant gamecocks to the death, and applaud opera singers into patriotic songs, and leave them to imprisonment and fines﻿—and you yourselves, cannot lift a finger, or join hand to hand, or bring to the hazard life, fortune, or honor, for your liberty and your dignity as men. Work your slaves, torture your bulls, fight your gamecocks, crown your dancers and singers﻿—and leave the weightier matters of judgment and justice, of fame by sea and land, of letters and arts and sciences, of private right and public honor, the present and future of your race and of your native land, to the care of others﻿—of a people of no better blood than your own, strangers and sojourners among you!
===Chinese===
可是这人群的卑劣、残忍和无能啊！他们朝持矛的、执镖的、行折磨之实的喊，朝牛喊，朝马喊，也彼此对喊，闹成一片巴别塔似的声浪，离谁十步之外便谁也听不清谁的话；喊的无非是种种主意、鼓劲或者嘲骂，像看戏的孩子一般。我近旁有一个成年体面、衣着齐整的青年，对着场中的人不停地喊，我敢说谁也听不清他喊的字眼，也没人想听——直喊得我心头火起，恨不得掐死他。

可是，你们就是这么一副人！你们会在斗牛场、斗鸡场里，在歌剧院、戏院的池座里又叫又吼，会把公牛和马逼得发疯，会驱使骁勇的斗鸡去送死，会鼓掌撺掇歌剧伶人唱起爱国之歌，然后任他们去坐牢、罚款——而你们自己，为了你们的自由和做人的尊严，却抬不起一根指头，拉不起一双手，不肯把性命、财产或名誉拿去冒一回险。奴役你们的奴隶，折磨你们的公牛，斗你们的斗鸡，给你们的名伶歌手加冕罢——把是非公义，把海陆的令名，把文学、艺术和科学，把私权与公誉，把你们民族和祖国的现在与将来，一概托付给别人——托付给一个血统并不比你们高贵的民族，托付给你们中间的外人和过客！

===Original===
The next bull is treated to a refinement of torture, in the form of darts filled with heavy China crackers, which explode on the neck of the poor beast. I could not see that even this made him really dangerous. The light complexioned, green-and-silver matador dispatches him, as he did the first bull, with a single lunge, and﻿—a fall and a quiver, and all is over!

The fifth bull is a failure and is allowed to go out of the ring. The sixth is nearly the same with the others, harmless if let alone, and goaded into short-lived activity, but not into anything like fury or even a dangerous animosity. He is treated to firecrackers, and gores one horse a little﻿—the horse standing, side on, and taking it, until the bull is driven off by the punching of the spear; and runs at the other horse, and, to my delight, upsets the rider, but unfortunately without hurting him, and the black-haired matador in green tries his hand on him and fails again, and is hooted, and takes to throwing darts, and gets a fall, and looks disconcerted, and gets his sword again, and makes another false thrust; and the crippled and bleeding animal is thrown down and dispatched by the butcher with his short knife, and drawn off by the three poor horses. The gates close, and I hurry out of the theatre, in a din of shouts and drums and trumpets, the great crowd waiting for the last bull;﻿—but I have seen enough.
===Chinese===
下一头牛受的是更精妙的酷刑：花镖里填足了重磅的中国爆竹，在这可怜牲口的颈上炸响。依我看，纵然这样也没能叫它真有什么危险。那位肤色白皙、绿衣银条的执剑士，像结果第一头牛那样一剑结果了它——一倒，一颤，万事皆休！

第五头牛是个失败，被放出圈外去了。第六头同前头的差不多，不去惹它便无害，被戳出片刻的活跃，却谈不上狂怒，连像样的敌意都没有。它也领受了爆竹，把一匹马抵了一下——那马侧身站着，硬挨着，直到牛被长矛捅开；又朝另一匹马冲去，叫我心中一喜——它把骑手掀了下来，可惜没伤着他。那个黑发绿衣的执剑士又来试身手，又失了手，挨了嘘，便改掷花镖，摔了一跤，面有窘色，又拾起剑来，再补了一记空刺；这头残废流血的牲畜终于被掀翻在地，屠夫用短刀结果了它，由那三匹可怜的马拖了出去。大门关上，我在呐喊、鼓号的一片喧腾中匆匆出了场——大群的人还在等最后一头牛；可我看够了。

===Original===
There is no volante in waiting, and I have to take my seat in an omnibus, and wait for the end of the scene. The confusion of cries and shouts and the interludes of music still goes on, for a quarter of an hour, and then the crowd begins to pour out, and to scatter over the ground. Four faces in a line are heading for my omnibus. There is no mistaking that head man, the file leader. “Down East” is written legibly all over his face. Tall, thin, sallow, grave, circumspect! The others are not counterparts. They vary. But “New England” is graven on all.

“Wa-a-al!” says the leader, as he gets into the omnibus. No reply. They take their seats, and wipe their foreheads. One expectorates. Another looks too wise for utterance. “By,”﻿ ﻿… a long pause﻿—How will he end it?﻿—“Jingoes!” That is a failure. It is plain he fell short, and did not end as he intended. The sentiment of the four has not yet got uttered. The fat, flaxen-haired man makes his attempt. “If there is a new-milch cow in Vermont that wouldn’t show more fight, under such usage, than them bulls, I’d buy her and make a present of her to Governor Cunchy﻿—or whatever they call him.”﻿—This is practical and direct, and opens the way to a more free interchange. The northern ice is thawed. The meanness and cruelty of the exhibition is commented upon. The moral view is not overlooked, nor underrated.﻿—None but cowards would be so cruel. And last of all, it is an imposition. Their money has been obtained under false pretences. A suit would lie to recover it back; but the poor devils are welcome to the money. The coach fills up with Cubans; and the noise of the pavements drowns the further reflections of the four philanthropists, patriots and economists.
===Chinese===
门外没有候着的沃兰特马车（volante），我只得上了公共马车，等这一场收梢。喊声叫声夹着断续的乐声又闹了一刻钟，随后人群开始涌出，在场上散开。四张一列排开的面孔正朝我这辆马车走来。领头的那位，错认不了。「唐恩伊斯特」（Down East，新英格兰东乡）几个字清清楚楚写在他整张脸上：高个，瘦削，蜡黄，严肃，处处小心！其余几位不是他的翻版，各有各的样子，但「新英格兰」都刻在每张脸上。

「哇——阿——尔！」（Wa-a-al）领头的上了车，拖长了调子说道。无人应声。他们各就各位，抹着额头。一个吐了口唾沫。另一个一脸的高深，不屑开口。「凭——」……好长的停顿——他打算怎么收这个尾呢？——「京戈斯！」（Jingoes）——还是砸了。明摆着他气不足，没能照原先想好的说完。这四位的感想还没发表出来。那位亚麻色头发的胖子开了腔：「佛蒙特要是有一头新下犊的奶牛，受了这样的作弄还不比这些个牛多几分斗志，我就把她买下来，送给昆奇（Cunchy）总督——或是他们管他叫什么来着。」——这一句实实在在、直截了当，打开了畅所欲言的门。北方的坚冰化开了。这场表演的卑劣与残忍挨了议论；道德的见地也没被忽略，没被小看。——只有懦夫才会这样残忍。末了，这是一场骗局。他们的钱是叫人用假招牌骗去的。真要打官司，这钱是可以告回来的；不过这几个穷鬼要贪这笔钱，就由他们去罢。车厢渐渐上满了古巴人；街石上的辚辚车声，盖过了这四位慈善家、爱国志士兼经济学家的进一步的思考。

## XXI / 二十一

===Original===
Habits of the Cubans, beckoning, smoking, etc.﻿—Visit to the Bishop, at Jesus del Monte﻿—Coolie mart, in the Cerro﻿—The condition and prospects of the Coolies; their importation, contracts, and treatment.
===Chinese===
古巴人的习惯：招手、吸烟等——往访赫苏斯德尔蒙特（Jesus del Monte）的主教——塞罗的苦力市场——苦力的境况与前途；他们的输入、契约与待遇。

===Original===
The people of Cuba have a mode of calling attention by a sound of the tongue and lips, a sort of “P﻿—s﻿—t!” after the fashion of some parts of the continent of Europe. It is universal here; and is used not only to servants and children, but between themselves, and to strangers. It has a mean sound, to us. They make it clear and penetrating; yet it seems a poor, effeminate sibilation, and no generous, open-mouthed call. It is the mode of stopping a volante, calling a waiter, attracting the attention of a friend, or calling the notice of a stranger. I have no doubt, if a fire were to break out at the next door, a Cuban would call “P﻿—s﻿—t!”

They beckon a person to come to them by the reverse of our motion. They raise the open hand, with the palm outwards, bending the fingers toward the person they are calling. We should interpret it to be a sign to go away.

Smoking is universal, and all but constant. I have amused myself, in the street, by seeing what proportion of those I meet have cigars or *cigarettos* in their mouths. Sometimes it has been one half, sometimes one in three. The cigar is a great leveller. Any man may stop another for a light. I have seen the poor porters, on the wharf, bow to gentlemen, strangers to them, and hold out a cigar, and the gentlemen stop, give a light, and go on﻿—all as of course.
===Chinese===
古巴人有一种用舌头和嘴唇发声唤人注意的法子，类似「噗——嘶——特！」（P—s—t!），是欧洲大陆某些地方的做派。这里人人用它；不但对仆人和孩子，彼此之间、对生人也用。在我们听来，这声音鄙俗。他们发得清脆而穿透力十足；可在我们听来，那总是一种寒酸的、女人气的嘶声，算不得爽朗开口的呼喊。叫停一辆沃兰特马车，唤一名侍者，引一位朋友的注意，或提醒一个陌生人，都用这一套。我毫不怀疑，倘若隔壁失了火，古巴人喊的也会是「噗——嘶——特！」

他们招手叫人过来的姿势，同我们正相反。他们抬起摊开的手，掌心向外，朝所唤的人屈动手指。换了我们，倒要以为这是叫人走开的手势。

吸烟人人如此，几乎无时或辍。我在街上曾拿这个自娱：数数迎面而来的人里，嘴上叼着雪茄或纸烟（*cigarettos*）的占几成。有时是一半，有时是三分之一。雪茄是了不起的平等之物。无论什么人，都可以拦住别人借个火。我见过码头上穷苦的脚夫，向素不相识的绅士们鞠一躬，递上一支雪茄，绅士们便站住，给他点上火，再走开——一切理所当然。

===Original===
In the evening, called on the Señoritas F﻿—﻿—, at the house of Mr. B﻿—﻿—, and on the American young lady at Señor M﻿—﻿—’s, and on Mrs. Howe, at Mde. Almy’s, to offer to take letters or packets.

At Mrs. Almy’s, there is a gentleman from New York, Mr. G﻿—﻿—, who is dying of consumption. His only wish is to live until the *Cahawba* comes in, that he may at least die at sea, if he cannot survive until she reaches New York. He has a horror of dying here, and being buried in the Potter’s Field.﻿—Dr. Howe has just come from his chamber.
===Chinese===
傍晚，拜访了B——先生宅里的F——家小姐们、M——先生宅里的美国小姐，还有阿尔米夫人（Mde. Almy）宅里的豪太太（Mrs. Howe），提出可以代带书信或包裹。

阿尔米夫人宅里有一位纽约来的绅士，G——先生，已是痨病垂危。他唯一的愿望是活到卡哈瓦号（*Cahawba*）进港，纵然熬不到纽约，好歹也要死在海上。他一想到死在这里、葬进义冢（Potter's Field），就不寒而栗。——豪博士刚从他房里出来。

===Original===
I drove out to the bishop’s, to pay my parting respects. It is about half-past eight in the evening. He has just returned from his evening drive, is dressed in a cool, cambric dressing gown, after a bath, and is taking a quiet cigar in his high-roofed parlor. He is very cordial and polite, and talks again about the Thirty Millions Bill, and asks what I think of the result, and what I have seen of the island, and my opinion of the religious and charitable institutions. I praise the Belen and the Sisters of Charity, and condemn the prison, and he appears to agree with me. He appreciates the learning and zeal of the Brothers of Belen; speaks in the highest terms of the devotedness of the Sisters of Charity; and admits the great faults of the prison, but says it was built recently, at an enormous outlay, and he supposes the government is reluctant to be at the expense of abandoning it and building another. He charges me with messages of remembrance and respect to acquaintances we have in common. As I take my leave, he goes with me to the outer gate, which is kept locked, and again takes leave, for two leave takings are the custom of the country, and returns to the solitude of his house.
===Chinese===
我驱车前往主教寓所，作辞行之拜。时约晚上八点半。他刚兜完晚风回来，浴罢穿一件凉快的细麻布晨衣，正在高顶的客厅里安安静静地抽雪茄。他极为恳切有礼，又谈起三千万法案（Thirty Millions Bill），问我对结果的看法，问我在岛上见到了什么，问我对宗教与慈善机构的意见。我称赞了贝伦学校（Belen）和慈善修女会，批评了那座监狱，他似乎同意我的话。他推重贝伦修士们的学问与热忱；对慈善修女会的献身精神称颂备至；也承认监狱弊病深重，只是说它新近才建成，耗费浩大，他料想政府舍不得弃之不用、再费一笔钱另建新狱。他托我向我们共同相识的友人致意问安。临别时，他一直送我到外门——那门平日上着锁——再次道别，因为道别两次是这个国家的习俗——然后回到他宅子的清寂中去。

===Original===
Yesterday I drove out to the Cerro, to see the Coolie jail, or market, where the imported Coolies are kept for sale. It is a well-known place, and open to all visitors. The building has a fair-looking front; and through this I enter, past two porters, into an open yard in the rear, where, on the gravel ground, are squatting a double line of Coolies, with heads shaved, except a tuft on the crown, dressed in loose Chinese garments of blue and yellow. The dealer, who is a calm, shrewd, heartless looking man, speaking English as well as if it were his native tongue, comes out with me, calls to the Coolies, and they all stand up in a double line, facing inward, and we pass through them, preceded by a driver armed with the usual badge of the plantation driver, the short, limber whip. The dealer does not hesitate to tell me the terms on which the contracts are made, as the trade is not illegal. His account is this﻿—The importer receives $340 for each Coolie, and the purchaser agrees to pay the Coolie four dollars per month, and to give him food, and two suits of clothes a year. For this, he has his services for eight years. The contract is reduced to writing before a magistrate, and two originals are made, one kept by the Coolie and one by the purchaser, and each in Chinese and Spanish.
===Chinese===
昨天我驱车到塞罗，去看苦力监，或者说苦力市场——输入的苦力就关在那里待售。这是个人人皆知的地方，对一切来客开放。房子正面还算体面；我从这里进去，过了两个门房，来到后面的一个敞院，沙砾地上蹲着两行苦力，剃光了头，只头顶留一撮头发，穿一身宽大的蓝黄二色的中国衣裤。经销商是个神色沉静、精明而无情的人，英语说得同母语一样好。他陪我出来，朝苦力们吆喝一声，他们便全体起立，排成两行，相向而立；我们穿过行间，前面引路的是一个监工，带着种植园监工惯常的标帜——那条短而柔韧的鞭子。经销商并不讳言契约的条款，因为这买卖并不违法。他的说法是这样的——进口商每名苦力收 340 美元，买主则允诺每月付给苦力四美元，给他饭吃，每年两身衣裳。以此为代价，他得到苦力八年的劳役。契约当着治安法官的面写成文字，正本一式两份，苦力和买主各执一份，每份都用中文和西班牙文写就。

===Original===
This was a strange and striking exhibition of power. Two or three white men, bringing hundreds of Chinese thousands of miles, to a new climate and people, holding them prisoners, selling their services to masters having an unknown tongue and an unknown religion, to work at unknown trades, for inscrutable purposes!

The Coolies did not look unhealthy, though some had complaints of the eyes; yet they looked, or I fancied they looked﻿—some of them, unhappy, and some of them stolid. One I am sure had the leprosy; although the dealer would not admit it. The dealer did not deny their tendency to suicide, and the anger of attempting to chastise them, but alleged their great superiority to the Negro in intelligence, and contended that their condition was good, and better than in China, having four dollars a month, and being free at the end of eight years. He said, which I found to be true, that after being separated and employed in work, they let their hair grow, and adopt the habits and dress of the country. The newly arrived Coolies wear tufts, and blue-and-yellow, loose, Chinese clothes. Those who have been here long are distinguishable from the whites only by the peculiar tinge of the cheek, and the form of the eye.

The only respect in which his account differed from what I heard elsewhere, was in the amount the importer receives, which has always been stated to me at $400.
===Chinese===
这是权力的一场奇特而触目的展示。两三个白人，把成百的中国人从几千里外载来，置于新的气候和新的种族中间，把他们当囚犯拘着，把他们的劳役卖给语言不通、宗教不一的主人，去做闻所未闻的营生，为着不可测度的目的！

苦力们看上去并不羸弱，虽然有些人害眼病；可他们的神情——或者是我自以为看出的神情——有的郁郁不乐，有的麻木不仁。有一个我敢断定是害着麻风，尽管经销商不肯承认。经销商也不否认他们动辄自杀，也不否认惩罚他们会激起暴怒；但他强调他们在智力上远胜黑人，并且力言他们的境遇是好的，比在中国还好：每月有四美元，八年期满便得自由。他还说——后来我发现确是如此——他们被分派出去做工之后，便蓄起头发，习染本地的风俗衣着。新到的苦力留着一撮头发，穿蓝黄的宽大中国衣裤；来得久了的，除了脸颊特有的色泽和眼睛的形状，同白人几乎分辨不出。

他的说法同我别处听来的唯一出入，在进口商所得的数目上——别处向来对我说是 400 美元。

===Original===
While I am talking with him, a gentleman comes and passes down the line. He is probably a purchaser, I judge; and I leave my informant to follow what is more for his interest than talking with me.

The importation has not yet existed eight years. So the question, what will become of these men, exotics, without women or children, taking no root in the land, has not come to a solution. The constant question is﻿—will they remain and mix with the other races? Will they be permitted to remain? Will they be able to go back?

So far as I can learn, there is no law in China regulating the contracts and shipment of Chinese Coolies, and none in Cuba regulating their transportation, landing, or treatment while here. The trade has grown up and been permitted and recognized, but not regulated. It is yet to be determined how far the contract is enforceable against either party. Those Coolies that are taken from the British East Indies to British islands, are taken under contracts, with regulations, as to their exportation and return, understood and enforced. Not so the Chinese Coolies. Their importers are *lege soluti*. Some say the government will insist on their being returned. But the prevailing impression is that they will be brought in debt, and bound over again for their debts or in some other way secured to a lifelong servitude, Mr. —﻿—﻿—, a very wealthy and intelligent planter, tells me he is to go over to Regla, tomorrow morning, to see a lot of slaves offered for sale to him, and asks me if I have ever seen a sale of slaves. I never have seen that sight, and accept his invitation. We are to leave here at half-past six, or seven, at the latest. All work is early here; I believe I have mentioned that the hour of ’Change for merchants is 7:30 a.m.
===Chinese===
正同他说话间，一位绅士走来，沿着队列走过去。我猜他多半是买主；我便别过这位消息灵通之士——同我闲谈，终究不如那桩买卖于他有利。

这种输入至今还不满八年。因此，这些人——一群客居的异乡之种，没有妻儿，在此地扎不下根——将来会怎样，这个问题还没有答案。人们不断在问：他们会留下来，同别的种族混血吗？他们会获准留下吗？他们回得去吗？

据我所知，中国没有管理华工契约与装运的法律，古巴也没有管理他们的转运、登岸和居留期间待遇的法律。这门买卖自行滋长起来，得到默许和承认，却从未受到规制。契约对双方究竟有多大约束力，也尚待分晓。从英属东印度运往英属各岛的苦力，是依契约招运的，其出口与遣返自有章程，为人共知，切实执行。华工却不然。他们的进口商是 *lege soluti*（法外之人）。有人说政府会坚持把他们遣返。但流行的看法是：他们会背上债务，为偿债再度被立契卖身，或用别的法子被拴住，落得终身为奴。———先生，一位极富而聪明的种植园主，告诉我他明天早晨要过海到雷格拉（Regla）去，看一批向他出售的奴隶，问我可曾见过奴隶买卖。这景象我从未见过，便应了他的约。我们明晨六点半动身，最迟七点。这里样样活计都起得早——我记得提过，商家上交易所（'Change）的时刻是上午七点半。

## XXII / 二十二

===Original===
A sale of slaves﻿—Cuban preserves﻿—Breakfast with Mr. —﻿—﻿——The census, and the probable number of slaves, free blacks, Creoles, and whites﻿—Lotteries﻿—Cockfight﻿—The Lopez expedition.
===Chinese===
一场奴隶买卖——古巴蜜饯——与———先生同进早餐——人口普查，以及奴隶、自由黑人、克里奥尔人与白人的约数——彩票——斗鸡——洛佩斯远征。

===Original===
*Tuesday, March 1*.﻿—Rise early, and walk to the sea baths, and take a delightful float and swim. And refreshing it is, after a feverish night in my hot room, where I did not sleep an hour all night, but heard every quarter-hour struck, and the boatswain’s whistle of the watchmen and their full cry of the hour and the weather, at every clock-strike. From the bath, I look out over the wall, far to the northeast, in the hope of catching a glimpse of the *Cahawba*’s smoke. This is the day of her expected arrival. My New York friends and myself feel that we have seen Havana to our satisfaction, and the heat is becoming intense. We are beginning to receive advice against eating fruit after café au lait, or bananas with wine, and in favor of high crowned hats at noon to prevent congestion from heat, and to avoid fogs in the morning. But there is no *Cahawba* in sight, and I hear only the bray of trumpets and roll of drums from the Morro and Cabaña and Punta, and the clanking march of the chain gang down the Paseo, and the march of the guard to trumpet and drum.
===Chinese===
三月一日，星期二（*Tuesday, March 1*）。——一早起身，步行到海水浴场，痛痛快快地浮水畅游了一番。昨夜在那间闷热的房里燥热难眠，整夜没睡上一个钟头，只听着每刻钟的钟响，听更夫们每一钟点吹响水手哨、拖长声调报出时辰与天气；游过这一场，真叫人清爽。从浴场越过围墙向东北极目远望，盼着能望见卡哈瓦号（*Cahawba*）的一缕烟。今天正是她预期抵达的日子。我同纽约的友人们都觉得，哈瓦那已经看够了，天气也热得难当。人们开始纷纷劝告我们：喝过牛奶咖啡（café au lait）不要吃水果，香蕉不可就酒，正午要戴高顶帽免得热气攻心，清晨要避雾。可是海上不见卡哈瓦号的影子，我只听得莫罗、卡瓦尼亚和蓬塔三处要塞传来的号声鼓声，听得锁链囚队沿着大道（Paseo）行进的铿锵，还有卫队在号鼓声中开拔的脚步。

===Original===
Mr. —﻿—﻿— is punctual at seven, his son with him, and a man in a suit of white linen, who is the broker employed by Mr. —﻿—﻿—. We take a ferryboat and cross to the Regla; and a few minutes’ walk brings us to a small nail factory, where all the workmen are Coolies. In the backyard of this factory is a line of low buildings, from which the slaves are brought out, to be shown. We had taken up, at the ferryboat, a small, thin, sharp-faced man, who was the dealer. The slaves are formed in a semicircle, by the dealer and broker. The broker pushed and pulled them about in a coarse, careless manner, worse than the manner of the dealer. I am glad he is not to be their master. Mr. —﻿—﻿— spoke kindly to them. They were fully dressed; and no examination was made except by the eye; and no exhibitions of strength or agility were required, and none of those offensive examinations of which we read so much. What examination had been made or was to be made by the broker, out of my presence, I do not know. The “lot” consisted of about fifty, of both sexes and of all ages; some being old, and some very young. They were not a valuable lot, and Mr. —﻿—﻿— refused to purchase them all. The dealer offered to separate them. Mr. —﻿—﻿— selected about half of them, and they were set apart. I watched the countenances of all﻿—the taken and the left. It was hard to decipher the character of their emotions. A kind of fixed hopelessness marked the faces of some, listlessness that of others, and others seemed anxious or disappointed, but whether because taken or rejected, it was hard to say. When the separation was made, and they knew its purpose, still no complaint was made and no suggestion ventured by the slaves that a tie of nature or affection was broken. I asked Mr. —﻿—﻿— if some of them might not be related. He said he should attend to that, as he never separated families. He spoke to each of those he had chosen, separately, and asked if they had parent or child, husband or wife, or brother or sister among those who were rejected. A few pointed out their relations, and Mr. —﻿—﻿— took them into his lot. One was an aged mother, one a wife, and another a little daughter. I am satisfied that no separations were made in this case, and equally satisfied that neither the dealer nor the broker would have asked the question.
===Chinese===
———先生七点准时到了，带着他的儿子，还有一个穿白麻布衣裳的人，那是他雇的经纪人。我们搭上渡船过海到雷格拉；步行几分钟，来到一家小制钉厂，厂里的工匠全是苦力。工厂后院里有一排低矮的屋子，奴隶们就从那里被带出来示看。渡船上我们还捎带了一个瘦小精明、面孔尖削的人，他便是经销商。经销商和经纪人把奴隶们排成半圆形。经纪人推推搡搡地摆布他们，举止粗鲁而漫不经心，比经销商还要糟糕。我很庆幸他将不是他们的主人。———先生对他们说话倒是和气。他们都穿戴齐整；除了用眼看，并无别的查验；没有要他们展示力气或身手，也没有我们在书里读得很多的那类侮辱性的检查。经纪人背着我还做过或要做什么检查，我就不得而知了。这一「批」约五十人，男女老幼都有；有的已老，有的还极年幼。这批人不值大钱，———先生不肯全数买下。经销商提出可以拆开卖。———先生拣了约半数，他们便被拨到一边。我留意观察所有人的脸色——被选中的和被留下的。他们心绪如何，很难辨认。有的脸上是一副定型的绝望，有的是麻木淡漠，有的显得焦灼或失望，但究竟是因为被选中还是被舍弃，难以断言。分隔既成，他们也明白了这分别的意思，却仍无一声怨言，也没有一个奴隶斗胆提起骨肉或情分就此拆散。我问———先生，他们中间会不会有亲属。他说这一点自会留意，因为他从不拆散人家。他逐一寻问他挑中的每一个人：在被撇下的人里，可有父母子女、丈夫妻子、兄弟姊妹？有几个指出了自己的亲人，———先生便把他们一并收进自己的一批。一个是年老的母亲，一个是妻子，还有一个小女儿。我确信这一次没有造成骨肉离散；也同样确信，无论经销商还是经纪人，是决不会想到问这一句的。

===Original===
I asked Mr. —﻿—﻿— on what principle he made his selection, as he did not seem to me always to take the strongest. “On the principle of race,” said he. He told me that these Negroes were probably natives of Africa, *bozales*, except the youngest, and that the signs of the races were known to all planters. A certain race he named as having always more intelligence and ambition than any other; as more difficult to manage, but far superior when well managed. All of this race in the company, he took at once, whatever their age or strength. I think the preferred tribe was the Lucumí, but am not certain.

From this place, I made a short visit to the Almacen de azúcar, in the Regla, the great storehouses of sugar. These are a range of one-story, stone warehouses, so large that a great part of the sugar crop of the island, as I am told, could be stored in them. Here the vessels go to load, and the merchants store their sugar here, as wine is stored in the London docks.

The Cubans are careful of the diet of foreigners, even in winter. I bought a couple of oranges, and young Mr. —﻿—﻿— bought a sapote, a kind of sweet-sour apple, when the broker said “Take care! Did you not have milk with your coffee?” I inquired, and they told me it was not well to eat fresh fruit soon after taking milk, or to take bananas with wine, or to drink spirits. “But is this in winter, also?” “Yes; and it is already very hot, and there is danger of fever among strangers.”
===Chinese===
我问———先生凭什么原则挑选，因为在我看来，他选的并不总是最强壮的。「凭种族的原则，」他说。他告诉我，这些黑人多半是非洲土生，是新捕黑奴（*bozales*），最年幼的除外；又说各族相貌的标记，种植园主人人认得。他点出某一个种族，说他们素来比别族更多智识和志气；比较难驾驭，可一旦驾驭得当，便远胜他族。这一族人在这批人里有的，无论年龄力气，他一概先挑了去。我记得他属意的是卢库米（Lucumí）一族，但不敢说准。

从这里，我又顺路去看了一眼雷格拉的糖仓（Almacén de azúcar），那是一座座巨大的储糖仓库。这是一排单层的石造货栈，大得据说全岛的糖产大半都装得下。船舶到这里来装货，商家把糖存放在这里，就像葡萄酒存放在伦敦的船坞里一样。

古巴人对外来人的饮食十分经心，冬天也不放松。我买了两只橙子，年轻的———先生买了一只萨波特果（sapote），一种甜中带酸的苹果似的果子，经纪人便说：「当心！你们不是咖啡里兑了牛奶么？」我问究竟，他们告诉我，喝了牛奶不宜马上吃鲜果，香蕉不可就酒，烈酒也不宜饮。「冬天也是这样么？」「也是；何况天气已经很热，外地人容易发热病。」

===Original===
Went to La Dominica, the great restaurant and depot of preserves and sweetmeats for Havana, and made out my order for preserves to take home with me. After consultation, I am advised to make up my list as follows: guava of Peru, limes, mammey apples, soursop, coconut, oranges, guava jelly, guava marmalade, and almonds. The ladies tell me there is a kind of fine linen sold here, called bolan, which it is difficult to obtain in the United States, and which would be very proper to take home for a present. On this advice, I bought a quantity of it, of blue and white, at La Diana, a shop on the corner of Calle de Obispo and San Ignacio.

Breakfasted with a wealthy and intelligent gentleman, a large planter, who is a native of Cuba, but of European descent. A very nice breakfast, of Spanish mixed dishes, rice cooked to perfection, fruits, claret, and the only cup of good black tea I have tasted in Cuba. At Le Grand’s, we have no tea but the green.
===Chinese===
又去了拉多米尼卡（La Dominica），那是哈瓦那的大餐馆，也是蜜饯糖果的总汇，我在那里开了带回国的蜜饯单子。商量一番之后，众人劝我照这样开列：秘鲁番石榴、青柠、曼密苹果（mammey）、刺果番荔枝、椰子、橙子、番石榴冻、番石榴酱，还有杏仁。太太们告诉我，这里卖一种叫博兰（bolan）的细麻布，美国难得买到，带回去送人再合适不过。听了这话，我便在迪亚娜店（La Diana）——奥比斯波街（Calle de Obispo）与圣伊格纳西奥街（San Ignacio）转角上的一家铺子——买了好几段，蓝白两色。

在一位殷富而开明的绅士家用了早餐。他是位大种植园主，生于古巴，祖上却是欧洲人。早餐极精美：西班牙式杂肴、火候恰到好处的米饭、水果、红葡萄酒，还有我在古巴喝到的唯一一杯像样的红茶。在勒格朗饭店（Le Grand's），除了绿茶，再无别的茶。

===Original===
At breakfast, we talked freely on the subject of the condition and prospects of Cuba; and I obtained from my host his views of the economical and industrial situation of the island. He was confident that the number of slaves does not exceed 500,000, to 200,000 free blacks, and 600,000 or 700,000 whites. His argument led him to put the number of slaves as low as he could, yet he estimated it far above that of the census of 1857, which makes it 375,000. But no one regards the census of slaves as correct. There is a tax on slaves, and the government has little chance of getting them stated at the full number. One planter said to a friend of mine a year or two ago, that his two hundred slaves were returned as one hundred. I find the best opinions put the slaves at 650,000, the free blacks at 200,000, and the whites at 700,000.

Havana is flooded with lottery ticket vendors. They infest every eating house and public way, and vex you at dinner, in your walks and rides. They sell for one grand lottery, established and guarantied by the government, always in operation, and yielding to the State a net revenue of nearly two millions a year. The Cubans are infatuated with this lottery. All classes seem to embark in it. Its effect is especially bad on the slaves, who invest in it all they can earn, beg, or steal, allured by the glorious vision of possibly purchasing their freedom, and elevating themselves into the class of proprietors.
===Chinese===
席间我们畅谈古巴的处境与前途；主人也向我讲了他对岛上经济与产业形势的见解。他一口咬定，奴隶的数目不会超过 500,000，自由黑人有 200,000，白人 600,000 或 700,000。他的论点本是要把奴隶数目说得越少越好，可估出来的数仍比 1857 年人口普查高出许多——普查的数目是 375,000。不过谁也不把奴隶普查当真。奴隶是要纳税的，政府很难指望他们照实数呈报。一两年前，一位种植园主对我朋友说，他两百名奴隶只报了一百。据我看到的最可靠的意见，奴隶当在 650,000，自由黑人 200,000，白人 700,000。

哈瓦那满街都是彩票贩子。他们纠缠于每家饭馆、每条要道，吃饭时搅扰你，散步兜风时也搅扰你。他们卖的是同一种大彩票，由政府设立并担保，常年开奖，每年给国库带来近两百万的净收入。古巴人为这彩票如痴如狂，各阶层似乎人人下水。为害最深的还是在奴隶身上：他们把挣来的、讨来的、偷来的一切都投进去，只被那幅光辉的幻景诱惑着——说不定能买回自由，跻身于有产者之列。

===Original===
Some gentlemen at Le Grand’s have been to a cockfight. I shall be obliged to leave the island without seeing this national sport for which every town, and every village has a pit, a Valle de Gallos. They tell me it was a very exciting scene among the spectators. Negroes, free and slave, low whites, Coolies, and men of high condition, were all frantically betting. Most of the bets were made by holding up the fingers and by other signs, between boxes and galleries. They say I should hardly credit the large sums which the most ordinary looking men staked and paid.

I am surprised to find what an impression the Lopez expedition made in Cuba﻿—a far greater impression than is commonly supposed in the United States. The fears of the government and hopes of sympathizers exaggerated the force, and the whole military power of the government was stirred against them. Their little force of a few hundred broken-down men and lads, deceived and deserted, fought a body of eight times their number, and kept them at bay, causing great slaughter. The railroad trains brought the wounded into Havana, car after car; rumors of defeat filled the city; artillery was sent out; and the actual loss of the Spaniards, in killed and wounded, was surprisingly large. On the front wall of the Cabaña, plainly seen from the deck of every vessel that leaves or enters the port, is a monument to the honor of those who fell in the battle with the Filibusteros. The spot where Lopez was garroted, in front of the Punta, is pointed out, as well as the slope of the hill from the castle of Atares, where his surviving followers were shot.
===Chinese===
勒格朗饭店里有几位绅士去看过斗鸡。我竟不得不离开这座岛，而未见过这项国民娱乐——为着它，每个城镇、每个村落都设有一处斗鸡坑（Valle de Gallos）。他们说，看客中间的场面煞是热闹。黑人，自由的和为奴的，下等白人，苦力，还有体面人物，一概狂热地下注。大多数赌注是伸手指头、打手势下的，隔着包厢与回廊彼此交易。他们说，那些最不起眼的人物押下并输付的巨款，我简直不会相信。

我很惊讶地发现，洛佩斯远征（Lopez expedition）在古巴留下的印象竟如此之深——比美国一般人所想的深得多。政府的恐惧和同情者的希望都把那支队伍夸大了，于是政府的全部武力都被动员起来对付他们。他们那几百个困惫不堪的汉子和小伙子，受过欺骗，遭过遗弃，却同一支八倍于己的兵力交了锋，逼得对方久攻不下，使其死伤惨重。火车把伤兵一车又一车运进哈瓦那；败讯的谣言满城风雨；大炮也出动了；西班牙人的实际死伤，数目大得惊人。卡瓦尼亚要塞的正面墙上——进出此港的每条船的甲板上都看得清清楚楚——立着一座纪念碑，纪念在同「冒险匪帮」（Filibusteros）作战中阵亡的将士。蓬塔要塞前面洛佩斯受加罗特刑的地方，有人指给我看过；从阿塔雷斯城堡（castle of Atarés）下来的山坡也有人指点——他的残部就在那里被枪决。

## XXIII / 二十三

===Original===
Condition of Cuba. Different classes of whites, Spaniards, other foreigners, and Cubans. Political condition, before 1825, and since 1825. Powers of the Captain-General. Diminution of freedom, and growth of central power. Army and navy. Taxes and revenue﻿—Religion, past and present. Past and present position of the Roman Catholic Church﻿—Free Blacks; their numbers, condition, rights, and prospects. Laws favoring emancipation﻿—Slaves. Their condition. Laws for their protection. Execution of these laws. Compulsory sale. Purchase of freedom. Bright side. Dark side. Marriage, increase, and importation of Negroes. Different views of the problem of Negro labor﻿—Material resources. Soils, productions, trees, mineral wealth, coal. Climate. The sugar crop, and other staples﻿—Education. Schools and colleges﻿—Reflections and suggestions as to the future of Cuba. Independence. Annexation. Protectorate. Effects of her geographical position.
===Chinese===
古巴的境况。白人的各个阶层：西班牙人、他国侨民与古巴人。1825 年之前与之后的政治处境。都督（Captain-General）的权力。自由的缩减与中央权力的增长。陆军与海军。赋税与财政收入——宗教的今昔。罗马天主教会过去与现在的地位——自由黑人：其数目、境况、权利与前途。有利于解放的法律——奴隶：其境况。保护奴隶的法律。这些法律的执行。强制出售。赎买自由。光明的一面。黑暗的一面。黑人的婚姻、增殖与输入。关于黑人劳力问题的不同见解——物产资源：土壤、物产、树木、矿产、煤。气候。糖产及其他大宗物产——教育：学校与学院——关于古巴前途的思考与建议：独立。并入。保护国。地理位置的影响。

===Original===
To an American, from the free States, Cuba presents an object of singular interest. His mind is occupied and almost oppressed by the thought of the strange problems that are in process of solution around him. He is constantly a critic, and a philosophizer, if not a philosopher. A despotic civil government, compulsory religious uniformity, and slavery, are in full possession of the field. He is always seeking information as to causes, processes and effects, and almost as constantly baffled. There are three classes of persons in Cuba, from whom he receives contradictory and irreconcilable statements: the Cubans, the Spaniards, and foreigners of other nations. By Cubans, I mean the Criollos (Creoles), or natives of Cuba. By Spaniards, I mean the Peninsulares, or natives of Old Spain. In the third class, are comprised the Americans, English, French, Germans, and all other foreigners, except Spaniards, who are residents on the island, but not natives. This last class is large, possesses a great deal of wealth, and includes a great number of merchants, bankers and other traders.

The Spaniards, or Peninsulares, constitute the army and navy, the officers of the government in all departments, judicial, educational, fiscal and postal, the revenue and the police, the upper clergy, and a large and wealthy class of merchants, bankers, shopkeepers, and mechanics. The higher military and civil officers are from all parts of Spain; but the Catalans furnish the great body of the mechanics and small traders. The Spaniards may be counted on as opponents of the independence of Cuba, and especially of her annexation to the United States. In their political opinions, they vary. Some belong to the liberal, or Progresista party, and others are advocates of, or at least apologists for, the present order of things. Their force and influence is increased by the fact that the government encourages its military and civil officers, at the expiration of their terms of service, to remain in the island, still holding some nominal office, or on the pay of a retired list.
===Chinese===
对一个来自自由州的美国人来说，古巴是一个别具意味的对象。他心里盘桓着、几乎压着这样的念头：他周围正有一些奇特的难题在求解之中。他不断充当批评家，若不算哲学家，也总是个玄想着。专制的民政、强制的宗教划一、奴隶制度，把这块场地全数占据。他总在探究原因、过程与结果，又几乎同样不断地碰壁。在古巴有三类人给他彼此矛盾、无法调和的说法：古巴人、西班牙人和别国的外国人。所谓古巴人，我指的是克里奥尔人（Criollos），即生于古巴的人。所谓西班牙人，我指的是半岛人（Peninsulares），即生于旧西班牙的人。第三类包括美国人、英国人、法国人、德国人，以及除西班牙人以外的一切外国人——他们侨居岛上，却非本地所生。这最后一类人数众多，拥有大量财富，包括一大批商人、银行家和其他买卖人。

西班牙人，即半岛人，构成了陆军和海军，政府各部门——司法、教育、财政、邮政——的官员，税务与警察，上层教士，以及一个人数众多、财力雄厚的商人、银行家、店主和工匠阶层。高级军政官员来自西班牙各地；但工匠和小贩的主体则由加泰罗尼亚人（Catalans）充当。西班牙人可以算作古巴独立的反对者，尤其反对并入美国。他们的政见各有不同：有的属于自由派，即进步党（Progresista），有的是现行秩序的鼓吹者，至少是辩护者。政府鼓励其军政官员任职期满后留在岛上，或仍挂一个虚衔，或领一份退役薪俸，这一层又增加了他们的力量和影响。

===Original===
The foreign residents, not Spaniards, are chiefly engaged in commerce, banking, or trade, or are in scientific or mechanic employments. These do not intend to become citizens of Cuba. They strike no root into the soil, but feel that they are only sojourners, for purposes of their own. Of all classes of persons, I know of none whose situation is more unfavorable to the growth and development of sentiments of patriotism and philanthropy, and of interest in the future of a race, than foreigners, temporarily resident, for purposes of moneymaking only, in a country with which they have nothing in common, in the future or the past. This class is often called impartial. I do not agree to that use of the term. They are, indeed, free from the bias of feeling or sentiment; and from the bias generated by the combined action of men thinking and feeling alike, which we call political party. But they are subject to the attractions of interest and interest will magnetize the mind as effectually as feeling. Planted in a soil where the more tender and delicate fibres can take no hold, they stand by the strong taproot of interest. It is for their immediate advantage to preserve peace and the existing order of things; and even if it may be fairly argued that their ultimate interests would be benefited by a change, yet the process is hazardous, and the result not sure; and, at most, they would do no more than take advantage of the change, if it occurred. I should say, as a general thing, that this class is content with the present order of things. The island is rich, production is large, commerce flourishes, life and property are well protected, and if a man does not concern himself with political or religious questions, he has nothing to fear. Of the Americans in this class, many, doubtless, may be favorably inclined toward annexation, but they are careful talkers, if they are so; and the foreigners, not Americans, are of course earnestly opposed to it, and the pendency of the question tends to draw them towards the present government.
===Chinese===
非西班牙籍的外国侨民主要经营商业、银行或贸易，或从事科学、机械的行当。他们并不打算做古巴的公民。他们不向这土壤扎根，只觉得自己是过客，为着各自的目的而来。据我所知，再没有哪一类人的处境，比这些只为赚钱、暂居一国、与此国过去未来皆无干系的外国人，更不利于爱国心与仁爱心之生长，更不利于对一个种族的前途发生兴味了。这一类人常被称为「不偏不倚」。我不能同意这样用这个词。他们确实没有情感好恶的偏见，也没有那种由思想感情一致的人们共同行动所生的偏见——那便是我们所说的政党。但他们逃不开利害的引力，而利害磁化人心的效力，同情感一样彻底。栽种在一片细嫩纤弱的根须扎不住的土壤里，他们便全凭利害这一条粗壮的主根立身。维持和平与现行秩序，于他们眼下有利；即便可以公允地论证，他们的长远利益其实系于变革，这过程也凶险，结果也无把握；至多，变革果真发生时，他们趁势取利而已。照一般的情形说，我该讲这一类人对现行秩序是心满意足的。岛上富庶，出产浩大，商业繁盛，生命财产保障周全，一个人只要不去招惹政治宗教的问题，便无可畏惧。这一类中的美国人，许多无疑倾向并入美国，但果真如此，他们说话也很谨慎；至于非美国的外国人，自然极力反对，这问题一日悬着，便一日把他们往现行政府那边推。

===Original===
It remains only to speak of the Cubans. They are commonly styled Creoles. But as that word includes natives of all Spanish America, it is not quite definite. Of the Cubans; a few are advocates of the present government﻿—but very few. The far greater part are disaffected. They desire something approximating to self-government. If that can be had from Spain, they would prefer it. If not, there is nothing for them but independence, or annexation to some other power. Not one of them thinks of independence; and if it be annexation, I believe their present impulse is toward the United States. Yet on this point, among even the most disaffected of the Cubans, there is a difference of opinion. Many of them are sincere emancipationists, and fear that if they come in at the southern end of our Union, that question is closed forever. Others fear that the Anglo-Saxon race would swallow up the power and property of the island, as they have done in California and Texas, and that the Creoles would go to the wall.

It has been my fortune to see persons of influence and intelligence from each of these chief divisions, and from the subdivisions, and to talk with them freely. From the sum of their conflicting opinions and conflicting statements, I have endeavored to settle upon some things as certain; and, as to other things, to ascertain how far the debatable ground extends, and the principles which govern the debate. From all these sources, and from my own observations, I will endeavor to set down what I think to be the present state of Cuba, in its various interesting features, trusting to do it as becomes one whose acquaintance with the island has been so recent and so short.
===Chinese===
剩下的只须说古巴人了。他们通称克里奥尔人。但这个词包括全西属美洲的土生者，不算确切。古巴人中，少数拥护现政府——极少数。绝大多数心怀不满。他们渴望某种近于自治的东西。若能得之于西班牙，他们宁愿如此；若不能，他们便只剩独立，或并入别强。无人真想独立；而若是并入，我相信他们眼下的冲动是朝向美国。然而在这一点上，连最不满的古巴人也各有意见。他们中许多人是真诚的废奴论者，深恐一旦从南端并入合众国，这个问题便永无翻案之日。另一些人则怕盎格鲁-撒克逊种族会把全岛的权与利一口吞尽，像他们在加利福尼亚和得克萨斯干的那样，克里奥尔人只有靠边的份。

我有机缘同这几大营垒——连同其中的分支——里那些有势力、有识见的人物一一见面，畅谈。从他们互相冲突的意见和说法的总和中，我力图认定某些事情为确定，对其余的事，则查明争议的地界何在、辩论所依的原理为何。凭这一切来源，加上我自己的观察，我要力求写下我所认为的古巴现状，及其种种耐人寻味的方面——只望写得像一个对此岛相识既晚又浅的人所应有的那样。


### Political Condition / 政治处境

===Original===
When the liberal constitutions were in force in Spain, in the early part of this century, the benefits of them extended to Cuba. Something like a provincial legislature was established; juntas, or advisory boards and committees, discussed public questions, and made recommendations; a militia was organized; the right to bear arms was recognized; tribunals, with something of the nature of juries passed upon certain questions; the press was free, and Cuba sent delegates to the Spanish Cortes. This state of things continued, with but few interruptions or variations, to 1825. Then was issued the celebrated Royal Order of May 29, 1825, under which Cuba has been governed to the present hour. This Royal Order is the only constitution of Cuba. It was probably intended merely as a temporary order to the then Captain-General; but it has been found convenient to adhere to it. It clothes the Captain-General with the fullest powers, the tests and limit of which are as follows: “… fully investing you with the whole extent of power which, by the royal ordinances, is granted to the governors of besieged towns. In consequence thereof, His Majesty most amply and unrestrictedly authorizes your Excellency not only to remove from the island such persons, holding offices from government or not, whatever their occupation, rank, class, or situation in life may be, whose residence there you may believe prejudicial, or whose public or private conduct may appear suspicious to you.﻿ ﻿…”
===Chinese===
本世纪初，当自由主义宪法在西班牙施行时，其惠泽也及于古巴。曾建立过近似省议会的东西；各色政务会（juntas）、谘议局和委员会讨论公共问题、提出建言；组织过民兵；携带武器的权利得到承认；具有若干陪审性质的法庭审理某些案件；出版自由；古巴还派出代表出席西班牙议会（Cortes）。这一局面持续到 1825 年，间有中断或变故，但不多。随后颁布了著名的 1825 年 5 月 29 日敕令（Royal Order），古巴受它的统治，直到今日。这道敕令就是古巴唯一的宪法。它当初大概只是给时任都督的一道临时指令；但大家觉得沿用方便，便沿用下来。它授予都督最充分的权力，其检验与限度如下：「……授你以皇家条例给予被围城镇总督的全部权力。据此，国王陛下极其宽泛、毫无限制地授权阁下：凡居留岛上之人——无论其是否受命于官府、从事何业、品级、阶级、身分如何——只要您认为其居留有害，或其公私行止在您看来可疑，即可将其逐出该岛。……」

===Original===
So that, since 1825, Cuba has been not only under martial law, but in a state of siege.

As to the more or less of justice or injustice, of honesty or peculation, of fidelity or corruption, of liberality or severity, with which these powers may have been exercised, a residence of a few days, the reading of a few books, and conversations with a few men, though on both sides, give me no right to pronounce. Of the probabilities, all can judge; especially when we remember that these powers are wielded by natives of one country over natives of another country.
===Chinese===
这样，自 1825 年起，古巴便不仅处于戒严法之下，而且处在被围状态之中。

至于这些权力行使起来究竟多一分公正还是多一分不义，多几分廉洁还是多几分贪墨，是忠诚还是腐化，是宽还是猛——小住数日、读几本书、同两方面的人谈几回话，都不给我下断语的资格。至于其或然与否，人人都能评判；尤其当我们记起：这些权力是由一国之人凌驾于另一国之人头上行使的时候。

===Original===
Into the details and anecdotes, and the controversies respecting motives, I do not enter. Certain things we know. Since 1825, there has been no legislative assembly in Cuba, either provincial or municipal. The municipal corporations (ayuntamientos) were formerly hereditary, the dignity was purchasable, and no doubt the bodies were corrupt. But they exercised some control, at least in the levying and expending of taxes; and, being hereditary, were somewhat independent, and might have served, like those of Europe in the middle ages as nuclei of popular liberties. These have lost the few powers they possessed, and the members are now mere appointees of the Captain-General. Since 1836, Cuba has been deprived of its right to a delegation in the Cortes. Since 1825, vestiges of anything approaching to popular assemblies, juntas, a jury, independent tribunals, a right of voting, or a right to bear arms, have vanished from the island. The press is under censorship; and so are the theatres and operas. When “*I Puritani*” is played, the singers are required to substitute Lealtà for Libertà, and one singer was fined and imprisoned for recusancy; and Facciolo, the printer of a secretly circulated newspaper, advocating the cause of Cuban independence, was garroted. The power of banishing, without a charge made, or a trial, or even a record, but on the mere will of the Captain-General, persons whose presence he thinks, or professes to think, prejudicial to the government, whatever their condition, rank, or office, has been frequently exercised, and hangs at all hours over the head of every Cuban. Besides, that terrible power which is restrained only by the analogy of a state of siege, may be at any time called into action. Cubans may be, and I suppose usually are, regularly charged and tried before judges, on political accusations; but this is not their right; and the judges themselves, even of the highest court, the Real Audiencia, may be deposed and banished, at the will of the military chief.
===Chinese===
种种细节、掌故，以及关于动机的争讼，我不置一词。有些事我们知道。1825 年以来，古巴没有任何立法议会，省的市的都没有。市政议会（ayuntamientos）从前是世袭的，名衔可以花钱买，其团体的腐败可想而知。但它们多少还行使一点监督，至少在赋税的征收与支出上；而且既是世袭，便有几分独立，原可以像中世纪欧洲的同类那样，充作民众自由的种子。这些机构连仅有的一点权力也失掉了，其成员如今全由都督委派。1836 年起，古巴被剥夺了在议会中的代表权。1825 年以来，凡略近民众会议、政务会、陪审、独立法庭、投票权或携枪权的东西，都在岛上绝了迹。出版受审查；戏院和歌剧院也受审查。演《清教徒》（*I Puritani*）时，演唱者须把 Libertà（自由）改唱作 Lealtà（忠诚），有一名歌手因抗命被罚款监禁；法乔洛（Facciolo），一份鼓吹古巴独立的秘密发行报纸的印刷人，被处了加罗特刑。放逐之权——不加罪名，不经审讯，甚至不留案牍，全凭都督一己之意，将其认为、或自称认为居留有害于政府之人，无论其身分、品级、官职如何，逐出岛外——屡经行使，时时刻刻悬在每个古巴人头上。何况那可怕的、仅以围城状态的类比才稍受约束的权力，随时可以出动。古巴人遭到政治控罪，或许——我想通常也是——由法官正式起诉审判；但这并非他们的权利；而法官本身，连最高法院皇家高等法院（Real Audiencia）的法官，也可以凭那位军事首脑的意志被罢黜、被放逐。

===Original===
According to the strictness of the written law, no native Cuban can hold any office of honor, trust, or emolument in Cuba. The army and navy are composed of Spaniards, even to the soldiers in the ranks, and to the sailors at the guns. It is said by the supporters of the government that this order is not adhered to; and they point to a capitan-general, an intendente, and a chief of the customs, who were Cubans. Still, such is the written law; and if a few Cubans are put into office against the law, those who are so favored are likely to be the most servile of officers, and the situation of the rest is only the more degraded. Not withstanding the exceptions, it may be said with substantial truth, that an independent Cuban has open to him no career, civil or military There is a force of volunteers, to which some Cubans are admitted, but they hold their places at the will of the government; and none are allowed to join or remain with them unless they are acceptable to the government.

There are vexatious and mortifying regulations, too numerous and minute to be complied with or even remembered, and which put the people in danger of fines or extortion at every turn. Take, for instance, the regulation that no man shall entertain a stranger overnight at his house, without previous notice to the magistrate. As to the absolute prohibition of concealed weapons, and of all weapons but the regulation sword and pistols﻿—it was no doubt introduced and enforced by Tacon as a means of suppressing assassinations, broils and open violence; and it has made life safer in Havana than it is in New York; yet it cannot be denied that it created a serious disability. In fine, what is the Spanish government in Cuba, but an armed monarchy, encamped in the midst of a disarmed and disfranchised people?
===Chinese===
按成文法的严格规定，没有一个古巴土生人能在古巴担任任何有名誉、有信责或有俸禄的官职。陆军海军清一色是西班牙人，连列兵和炮位上的水兵也不例外。政府的拥护者说，这条规矩并未死守；并举出一位都督、一位财政长官（intendente）、一位海关长，都是古巴人。然而成文法终归如此；倘若少数古巴人被破格任用，得此恩遇的多半是最奴颜婢膝的官儿，其余人的处境只有更加低贱。虽有例外，大体仍可如实说：一个有独立人格的古巴人，面前没有任何文武出路。有一支志愿兵队伍，收若干古巴人，但其位置全系于政府之意；非经政府首肯，谁也进不去、留不下。

还有种种烦扰屈辱的规章，多得记不住也守不完，使人动辄有罚款和勒索之虞。譬如那条：任何人留宿生客于家，须先报治安官。至于查禁携带暗藏兵器、除制式刀剑手枪外一概不许持械的绝对禁令——那无疑是由塔孔（Tacon）推行，用以压制暗杀、斗殴和公行暴力的；它使哈瓦那的性命比纽约还安全；然而无可否认，它造成了一种严重的无能力。说到底，在古巴的西班牙政府是什么？不过是一座武装的君主政体，扎营在一群被缴了械、剥夺了公权的人民中间罢了。

===Original===
The taxes paid by the Cubans on their property, and the duties levied on their commerce, are enormous, making a net income of not less than $16,000,000 a year. Cuba pays all the expenses of its own government, the salaries of all officers, the entire cost of the army and navy quartered upon it, the maintenance of the Roman Catholic religion, and of all the charitable and benevolent institutions, and sends an annual remittance to Spain.3 The number of Spanish men-of-war stationed on the coast, varies from twenty-five to thirty. Of the number of soldiers of the regular army in Cuba, it is difficult to form an opinion. The official journal puts them at 30,000. The lowest estimate I heard, was 25,000; and the highest was 40,000. Judging from the number of sick I saw at the Hospital Militar, I should not be surprised if the larger estimate was nearer the truth. Education is substantially in the hands of the government. As an instance of their strictness, no man can take a degree at the University, unless he makes oath that he does not belong to, has never belonged to, and will not belong to, any society not known to and permitted by the government. But details are of little importance. The actual administration may be a little more or less rigid or lax. In its legal character, the government is an unmixed despotism of one nation over another.
===Chinese===
古巴人缴纳的财产税、商业关税，为数惊人，净收入每年不下 $16,000,000。古巴支付自己政府的全部开支、全体官员的薪俸、驻岛陆海军的全部费用、罗马天主教的维持费、一切慈善救济机构的经费，还要每年给西班牙汇去一笔款项。3 驻泊海岸的西班牙军舰，自二十五艘至三十艘不等。正规军驻古巴的兵额，很难悬揣。官方的报纸称 30,000。我所听到的最低估计是 25,000，最高 40,000。看我在军医院（Hospital Militar）见到的病兵之数，即便较高的估计近于实情，我也不惊讶。教育实际握在政府手里。举一个严厉的例子：欲在大学取得学位，须先宣誓不属于、从未属于、将来也不属于任何政府不明了且未准许的会社。但细枝末节无关宏旨。实际的施政或松或紧，略有出入；就其法定的性质而论，这政府是一个民族对另一个民族的不掺假的专制。

### Religion / 宗教

===Original===
No religion is tolerated but the Roman Catholic. Formerly the church was wealthy, authoritative and independent, and checked the civil and military power by an ecclesiastical power wielded also by the dominant nation. But the property of the church has been sequestrated and confiscated, and the government now owns all the property once ecclesiastical, including the church edifices, and appoints all the clergy, from the bishop to the humblest country curate. All are salaried officers. And so powerless is the church, that, however scandalous may be the life of a parish priest, the bishop cannot remove him. He can only institute proceedings against him before a tribunal over which the government has large control, with a certainty of long delays and entire uncertainty as to the result. The bishopric of Havana was formerly one of the wealthiest sees in Christendom. Now the salary is hardly sufficient to meet the demands which custom makes in respect of charity, hospitality and style of living. It may be said, I think with truth, that the Roman Catholic Church has now neither civil nor political power in Cuba.
===Chinese===
除罗马公教外，不容任何宗教。从前教会富有、有权威、有独立，以同样由统治民族执掌的教会权力，牵制民政与军权。但教会的产业已被没收入官，昔日属于教会的一切财产——连教堂在内——今皆归政府所有；全体教士，自主教至乡间最卑微的堂区司铎，皆由政府委派，都是领薪的官员。教会毫无力量，一个堂区神父的私生活无论怎样丑声四播，主教也撤换他不得。主教只能在一个政府大有操纵之权的法庭上对他提起讼案，其结果必然是遥遥无期的拖延，胜负全然无定。哈瓦那主教区从前是基督教世界最富的教区之一。如今那薪俸几乎不够应付习俗在慈善、款客和排场上的需索。我想可以如实地说：罗马天主教会今日在古巴，无论民政上、政治上，都已没有权力。

===Original===
That there was a long period of time during which the morals of the clergy were excessively corrupt, I think there can be no doubt. Make every allowance for theological bias, or for irreligious bias, in the writers and tourists in Cuba, still, the testimony from Roman Catholics themselves is irresistible. The details, it is not worthwhile to contend about. It is said that a family of children, with a recognized relation to its female head, which the rule of celibacy prevented ever becoming a marriage, was general with the country priesthood. A priest who was faithful to that relation and kept from cockfighting and gambling, was esteemed a respectable man by the common people. Cuba became a kind of Botany Bay for the Romish clergy. There they seem to have been concealed from the eye of discipline. With this state of things, there existed, naturally enough, a vast amount of practical infidelity among the people, and especially among the men, who, it is said, scarcely recognized religious obligations at all.

No one can observe the state of Europe now, without seeing that the rapidity of communication by steam and electricity has tended to add to the efficiency of the central power of the Roman Catholic Church, and to the efficacy and extent of its discipline. Cuba has begun to feel these effects. Whether they have yet reached the interior, or the towns generally, I do not know; but the concurrent testimony of all classes satisfied me that a considerable change has been effected in Havana. The instrumentalities which that church brings to bear in such cases, are in operation: frequent preaching, and stricter discipline of confession and communion. The most marked result is in the number of men, and men of character and weight, who have become earnest in the use of these means. Much of this must be attributed, no doubt, to the Jesuits; but how long they will be permitted to remain here, and what will be the permanent effects of the movement, I cannot, of course, conjecture.
===Chinese===
有一个很长的时期，教士的道德极端败坏——这一点，我想无可怀疑。把作家和游历者的神学偏见或反宗教偏见一概打足折扣，罗马公教徒自己的证词仍旧无可抵赖。细节，犯不着争。据说乡下神父普遍有一个儿女满堂的家，同那位女主人保持着公开的关系——独身的教规使这关系永远不能成婚。一个忠于这层关系、又不斗鸡不赌博的神父，便算得百姓眼中的正派人了。古巴成了罗马教士的植物学湾（Botany Bay，流放地）。他们在那里似乎躲开了纪律的眼睛。这种局面之下，民间——尤其是男人——自然有大量实际的不信教；据说男人们几乎全不承认什么宗教义务。

今日欧洲的情形，谁看一眼都明白：轮船电报的迅捷，已使罗马公教的中枢权力更有效能，其纪律更有着落、更普及。古巴已开始感到这些影响。它们是否已达于内地、达于一般城镇，我不知道；但各阶层众口一词的证词使我确信，哈瓦那已起了可观的变化。教会在此等场合惯用的手段都已在运转：频繁的讲道，更严格的告解与领圣体纪律。最显著的成效，是许多男人——而且是有品格、有分量的男人——开始热心奉行这些功课。这无疑多半要归功于耶稣会士（Jesuits）；但他们获准在此久留几时，这运动又将有怎样的后效，我当然无从悬揣。

===Original===
I do not enter into the old field of contest. “We care not,” says one side, “which be cause and which effect;﻿—whether the people are Papists, because they are what they are, or are as they are because they are Papists. It is enough that the two things coexist.” The other side replies that no Protestant institutions have ever yet been tried for any length of time, and to any large extent, with southern races, in a tropical climate; and the question﻿—what would be their influence, and what the effect of surrounding causes upon them, lies altogether in the region of conjecture, or, at best, of faith.

Of the moral habits of the clergy, as of the people, at the present time, I am entirely unable to judge. I saw very little that indicated the existence of any vices whatever among the people. Five minutes of a street view of London by night, exhibits more vice, to the casual observer, than all Havana for a year. I do not mean to say that the social morals of the Cubans are good, or are bad; I only mean to say that I am not a judge of the question.
===Chinese===
我不踏进那片旧战场。一方说：「我们不管哪个是因、哪个是果——是人民先做了教皇派才成了现在这样，还是因为成了现在这样才做了教皇派。要紧的只是这两件事并存。」另一方答道：新教的制度还从未在南方种族、热带气候下长时期、大规模地试验过；至于它们的影响如何、周遭种种原因又将对它们有何影响，这问题全然悬在臆测之域，至多悬在信念之域。

至于教士们今日的德行操守，同人民的习俗一样，我全然无从评判。我在民间几乎看不出任何恶行的形迹。伦敦街头夜景随眼扫五分钟，落在漫不经心的旁观者眼里的恶行，就抵得哈瓦那全年。我不是说古巴人的社会道德是好是坏；我只说，我不是这个问题的评判者。

===Original===
The most striking indication of the want of religious control, is the disregard of the Lord’s Day. All business seems to go on as usual, unless it be in the public offices. The chain gang works in the streets, under public officers. House-building and mechanic trades go on uninterrupted; and the shops are more active than ever. The churches, to be sure, are open and well filled in the morning; and I do not refer to amusements and recreations; I speak of public, secular labor. The Church must be held to some responsibility for this. Granted that Sunday is not the Sabbath. Yet, it is a day which, by the rule of the Roman Church, the English Church in England and America, the Greek Church and other Oriental Churches﻿—all claiming to rest the rule on Apostolic authority, as well as by the usage of Protestants on the continent of Europe﻿—whether Lutherans or Calvinists﻿—is a day of rest from secular labor, and especially from enforced labor. Pressing this upon an intelligent ecclesiastic, his reply to me was that the Church could not enforce the observance;﻿—that it must be enforced by the civil authorities; and the civil authorities fall in with the selfishness and gratifications of the ruling classes. And he appealed to the change lately wrought in Paris, in these respects, as evidence of the consistency of his Church. This is an answer, so far as concerns the Church’s direct authority; but it is an admission either of feeble moral power, or of neglect of duty in times past. An embarrassment in the way of more strictness as to secular labor, arises from the fact that slaves are entitled to their time on Sundays, beyond the necessary labor of providing for the day; and this time they may use in working out their freedom.
===Chinese===
最触目的宗教约束缺位的标志，是对主日（Lord's Day）的漠视。除官署外，一切营业照常。锁链囚队在街上、在官员督率下做工。盖房和各行手艺毫不停歇；铺子比平日更热闹。教堂固然开着，早晨也坐得满满的；游乐消遣我不去说；我说的是公开的世俗劳作。这事教会不能不负一份责任。就算礼拜日不是安息日罢，可这一天，按照罗马教会、英美两国的英国教会、希腊教会和其他东方教会的规矩——它们都自称这规矩本于使徒的权威——也照欧洲大陆上新教徒的惯行（无论路德宗还是加尔文宗），是歇除世俗劳作的日子，尤其是歇除强制劳作的日子。我把这一点诘问一位有学识的教士，他答道：教会无法强制遵守——这须由官府强制；而官府呢，正顺着统治阶级的自私与享乐。他还援引巴黎近来在这几件事上的转变，作为他教会前后一贯的凭证。就教会的直接权威而论，这算是一个回答；但这等于承认教会道德力量孱弱，或承认往日失职。至于世俗劳作上更严紧一层，还有一重为难：奴隶在礼拜日有权支配自己的时间——为当日生计而作的必要劳作除外——而这时间正可用来做工赎身。
